MANUFACTURED IN THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA

PREFACE 

THE profound economic disturbances of the past few years have resulted in many important modifications of our financial and industrial structure. This has created a pressing need for a high school textbook that presents a general survey of the field of economics and evaluates and interprets recent changes in the light of past experience and the most widely accepted theory. 

In substance, this is what VISUALIZED PRINCIPLES AND PROB- LEMS OF ECONOMICS attempts to do. Its purpose is to explain the economic world of the here and now, with its marvelous productive efficiency and unemployment; its intricate facilities for exchange and monetary confusion; its frenzied attempts at reform and ordered progress toward institutional readjustments. The author has tried to present this contemporary scene in simple, impartial, but thoroughly realistic terms. He has not hesitated to utilize whatever material (historical, sociological, statistical, etc.) appeared valuable for explaining our economic structure, its merits, imperfections, and possibilities of improvement. 

Some of the unusual features and devices which have been used for this purpose are: 

1. The introductory chapter traces the history of an American family from frontier days to the present time. In this way, the basic economic forces are presented, first in their simplest and clearest form, then in their present-day complexity. This contrast has been used throughout the book to emphasize the relatively stable essentials of our economic system, which the pupil otherwise might fail to recognize in the setting of distracting and highly variable detail. 

2. Every effort has been made to organize the material in the most coherent and logical form. This applies both to the arrangement of the material within chapters and to the sequence of chapters. The first section of the book is devoted, for the most part, to an analysis of the essentials of production and distribution. Each factor of production is considered in connection with its share of the total social income (labor and wages, land iii

iv 

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

and rent, etc.). In the latter part of the book, this material is utilized in a comprehensive and up-to-date discussion of leading economic problems. 

3. The book is extraordinarily concise. This has been achieved by economy and simplicity of language and by stress on essentials. These qualities make the book ideal for review purposes or for use as a supplementary text in conjunction with any larger work. At the same time, the book is complete and three-dimensional; it meets the requirements of the syllabi of leading secondary schools throughout the country and can be used very effectively as a basic text. 

4. The author has attempted to provide authoritative treatments of all the more important recent developments in economic affairs. A chapter is devoted to the pressing problem of conservation. Subjects of such immediate importance as vertical and horizontal unionism, organization among white-collar workers, inflation, the financing of relief, the efforts of the railroads to meet bus and truck competition, etc., are discussed thoroughly and in their proper setting. Important recent legislation (including the Tax Bill of 1936) is analyzed realistically and im partially. 

5. The book contains many original cartoon-illustrations which are designed to arouse interest, clarify difficult points, and impress related facts and concepts on the pupil's mind. 

6. Abundant thought questions, new-type tests, and recent

New York State Regents examination papers are included. 

7. The Appendix contains a classified bibliography specially selected for high school students. 

The author expresses his appreciation to Dr. R. G. Hardy, Head of the Department of Economics, Girls Commercial High. 

School, Brooklyn, N. Y., who read the manuscript and made many valuable suggestions and criticisms; to Professor Clarence

W. Fackler, Associate Professor of Economics, New York University, who read and criticized the chapters dealing with money, banking, and foreign trade; and to the Editor, Professor Arthur

D. Whitman, of New York University, whose advice and guidance, particularly with regard to pedagogical methods and devices, have been invaluable.

VISUALIZED PRINCIPLES AND PROBLEMS OF ECONOMICS CHAPTER I THE DEVELOPMENT OF OUR ECONOMIC STRUCTURE PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 1. To indicate the subject-matter of economics. 

2. To trace the steps by which our present method of making a living developed from a much simpler method. 

3. To show that the same factors are involved in the simple and the complex methods of making a living.

Definition.-Economics is the study of the production, distribution and consumption of the goods and services which man needs in order to live and to enjoy life. Economics attempts to understand those phases of human activity which are concerned with man's living and enjoying life. We call economics a science because, in its investigation, it tries to connect cause and effect in a reasonable and systematic way. 

Introduction.-The United States today is populated by more than 127 million people, living under many different conditions and engaged in a great number of different activities. 

Some till the soil and some dig in mines. Others work in banks, offices and factories. Some operate the ships which take our products to other lands and bring the products of those lands to us.

Some have helped to build a great network of highways and railroads. On these, others run automobiles and trains which carry people and goods from one part of the country to the other, and bind our nation into a unified whole. These represent only a fraction of the thousands upon thousands of different economic activities in which the American people engage every day. In fact, we may safely say that most of our activities have an important economic aspect. 1

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

At first glance, it may seem very difficult to cut through to the core of the intricate details of modern economic life. However, an understanding of economic processes is so important to the proper conduct of our lives that it is certainly worth every effort to acquire that understanding. It will help us a great deal if we go back a bit in history and see how our apparently complex economic structure, which grew from a simple one (like a snowball rolling downhill), has a basic framework that is really easy to understand.

The Overton Saga.-In the lives of four generations of a typical, prosperous Middle-Western family we can find direct reflections of most of the important developments in the growing complexity of American economic life. The following chart gives the dates of the lives of four generations of the Overton family. The activities of this family are sketched briefly in the following pages.

The Pioneer Sets Out.-In 1810, a young man named Mark 

Overton travelled on horseback from Massachusetts, his home state, to Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania. There, he and some companions built a flatboat and floated down the Chio River until While this is not a picture of the progress of an actual Middle- Western family, the facts are typical of life in that region. All of the events which are here described happened over and over again, in similar form, between 1800 and today.

they arrived at a tiny town called Cincinnati, which had been in existence for only about twenty years. At Cincinnati, Overton left his companions and proceeded alone westward to the Wabash River. On the banks of that river, at a point called New Adventure, he found a place which appealed to him. The land was good, the river could be used for transportation to more developed sections, and the Indians were not troublesome. 

THE FRONTIERSMAN WAS SELF-SUFFICIENT|| CLOTHING FOOD

​SHELTER 

Do any self-sufficient families exist in the United States today? 

He selected a site for his farm and went to work on it. For shelter he built a rough log cabin with a fireplace at one end. 

Gradually he cleared away a few acres of land, and thus was able to plant a crop of corn. At first he had to spend much of his time hunting. Deer, turkey, racoons, quail, pigeons, and other game animals and birds abounded. These provided his meat supply; the hides of the animals he used for clothing. 

Thus we see that Mark Overton lived a simple life on the frontier. By the use of his own hands, and a few simple tools, he re-shaped the materials of nature (trees, animals, and soil)

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

so that they provided him with food and shelter. All the articles which he consumed he produced himself. 

Development of a Community. It happened that Mark 

Overton was not altogether satisfied with this simple life. As soon as he was able to produce a crop surplus in excess of his own needs, he floated it to market on a home-made raft and sold it. He used the proceeds of this sale to buy some animals, including a cow, a horse, several sheep, and some pigs. The farm was enlarged, machinery for a saw-mill was brought from the East, and a frame house took the place of the cabin. Other settlers, who liked the New Adventure neighborhood, soon appeared. A mill was built to grind corn into meal; a tavernkeeper set up a tiny inn; a lawyer, a doctor, and a school-teacher appeared, and there the town of Manship took form. Then 

Overton returned to Massachusetts, married Sarah Starke, whom he had known before going West, and returned to Manship with his wife.

Overton spent the rest of his life cultivating his farm. His life was hard, but, on the whole, he was fairly prosperous. His land bore him good crops, and he was able to sell considerable quantities of grain. Part of the money which he earned he spent for machinery which enabled him to increase the productivity of his farm. In addition to this, he bought a few modest comforts for his home, and the remainder of the money he put away, saving it for a "rainy day."

Overton died in 1850, and his widow followed him to the grave the following year. Although the last years of her life were somewhat easier, her health had been impaired by the exhausting labors which frontier life required, such as the making of brooms, candles, butter, soap, leather, and cloth-all of which were produced and consumed in the home. 

Mark and Sarah Overton pass beyond our view, but the family of three sons which they left behind made definite imprints on the Wabash country.

STAGE OF THE SMALL ENTREPRENEUR 

Simple Business Enterprises. After the death of Mark 

Overton, the small sum of money which he had accumulated was divided equally among his three sons.

The first son, Jonathan, went to Indianapolis, where he established himself as a wagon-maker. He was able to do this because this business did not require a great deal of money. He rented a small building, and bought a few simple machines and some raw materials, such as wood, nails, and iron. Then he hired several laborers and went to work with them to manufacture wagons. We would call him a single entrepreneur today, for he was the sole owner of his little business. 

The second son, Arthur, decided to do something with the coal which was to be found in the ground near the Overton land. This required more money than he had, so he formed a partnership with a man from Terre Haute who had a little cash. 

They combined their resources and began to mine the coal. One reason why they needed a comparatively large amount of money was that they had to build houses for the miners whom they employed. Otherwise they could not have secured the necessary labor.

The third son, Anthony, remained on the original Overton land and produced grains, mostly wheat and corn, which he sold in the neighboring towns or shipped to New Orleans. 

Profits. All three sons realized profits from the business enterprises of manufacturing, mining, and farming. The profits which they earned varied from year to year. Low production costs, combined with high prices for their products resulted in large profits, but these two factors were not always present to the same extent. The producers, Arthur, Jonathan, and Anthony, had to work hard and to utilize all their business experience and ability in order to keep costs down and take full advantage of rising prices.

Banks. The sons saved a part of the money which they earned, intending to use it in an emergency or to invest it some day in a productive enterprise. They did not keep this money in their own homes. Instead, they deposited it in certain institutions which had grown up as the country became more developed and as a greater supply of surplus capital in the form of money appeared. These institutions were known as banks. 

Not only did the banks safeguard the money of the Overtons, but they also paid them interest, or a reward for the use of their savings. The bankers were able to do this because they

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

used the money to make loans to other business men who, in turn, paid them interest.

The Overtons were among the business men who borrowed money from the banks. They used these borrowed funds to buy capital goods which aided them in the conduct of their enterprises. Of course, such loans meant the payment of interest charges, but they were willing to undergo this expense because they believed that the added productivity resulting from the use of these capital goods would make their businesses more profitable.

In addition to this, the Overtons borrowed money from the banks because their businesses were seasonal in character, that is, at certain times of the year there was a greater demand for their products than at others. For example, Arthur's coal mines supplied a commodity for which there was a heavy demand during the winter. He had to borrow money from the banker to meet his expenses in the summer, when the coal was being mined. Then, when he sold his coal in the winter, he was able to repay the loan plus the interest. In the same way, the farmer and the wagon manufacturer borrowed to meet expenses during their slack seasons.

The Factors of Production.—In the progress of the Overton family, the three factors of production-labor, land, and capital -were combined in different ways. All of the Overtons performed labor, for they worked with their hands and with their brains. When their businesses grew to such an extent that more labor was needed, they hired other men and paid them wages. 

Mark Overton and his three sons all used land. Arthur owned his land, and used it to mine coal. Jonathan rented his land from someone else and used it as a location for his little wagon factory. Anthony depended on the fertility of the land, which he owned, to produce grain crops.

Capital was also used by each of the three brothers. The mining machinery, the tools and raw materials in the wagon factory, and the reapers on the farm, were all forms of capital. 

The money which the brothers had inherited from their father, and which they saved from their profits, was also capital, for

it could be used to purchase whatever machinery or raw materials they needed. To obtain some of these capital goods, it had often been necessary to borrow from the banks, as described above.

Before land, labor, and capital could be productive, they had to be organized into a productive enterprise. Mark Overton's land would never have borne a crop if he had not used his knowłedge and ability to bring about the proper combination of land, labor, and capital. Therefore, organization is considered a separate factor of production.

Another factor involved in the productive activities of the brothers was the government. The government provided a great many services without which the economic development we have been considering could not have taken place. Transportation, money, banking, protection, education, and many other services were either provided directly or were guided and regulated by the government.

Summary of Changes. You will recall that Mark Overton produced the various things that he needed by working (labor) alone on his land, with the aid of a few simple tools (capital). 

He himself produced almost all the articles that he used up, or consumed. The sons of Mark Overton carried on these same fundamental activities in a somewhat different manner. In the field of production they introduced division of labor. Each of them turned out one particular commodity (food, wagons, coal) and sold it for money, which was then used to buy the many necessities and comforts of life from other people, each of whom was also producing a single commodity. In this specialized production, whether it was farming, mining, or manufacturing, the brothers used more capital than Mark Overton had ever been. able to accumulate. The need for capital was so great that they borrowed the savings of other people, through the banks, or formed partnerships, so that the resources of two men might be combined. This method of production had become dominant because it was more efficient, that is, it enabled men to produce more and better goods. As a result, the brothers consumed a far greater range of commodities than their father had ever known.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS STAGE OF LARGE-SCALE PRODUCTION 

Further Development.-Throughout the years during which these events occurred, there had been a great development of labor-saving machinery. There were various reasons for this: (1) Many men emigrated from the industrial East to the free. 

Western lands, just as Mark Overton had done. As a result, there was a comparative scarcity of labor. (2) In proportion to the available productive capacity of the country, there was a great demand for goods. Manufacturers knew that they would be able to find buyers if they could produce a greater volume of commodities. (3) The country was young and growing, and an optimistic spirit prevailed. This stimulated invention, for people felt that in the future the nation would need greatly expanded productive powers.

This development of labor-saving devices became pronounced in the years following the Civil War. (The War itself had contributed to the development by stimulating the demand for goods which only machine industry could supply.) Probably the most important of the inventions of this period was the improvement of the reaper and the sewing-machine. Agriculture, the clothing industry-indeed, all phases of production, felt the effects of the new machinery. More and better products were placed on the market. People became accustomed to purchasing clothing and household articles from local and from distant manufacturers, as contrasted to the practice of household production which the family of Mark Overton had followed. 

The demand for capital increased, since opportunities for profits in industry and in agriculture were good if the enterprise. was equipped with the new and expensive machinery. As a result, the size of business enterprises tended to become greater, since small firms could not acquire the machinery which was such an advantage in production.

A New Form of Enterprise.-In 1870, the Overton brothers, encouraged by the expansion and growth which was going on all around them, decided to build a woolen mill. The combined capital which they had saved from their earnings as farmer, miner, and manufacturer was not enough to build and equip

such a mill. Conditions had changed since the days when one of the brothers had started a wagon factory with the few dollars which he had inherited from his father. Production, now, required a greater amount of capital-more capital than one man, or even a few men, possessed. 

In order to raise the necessary money, the brothers formed a corporation. They sold shares in the new organization to their friends and neighbors. All these people became part owners of the blanket mill, but the Overtons retained enough of the stock to control the enterprise. In this way, enough money was raised to build the factory and to start production. 

The new enterprise was successful. Its products were in demand because of their comparatively low prices and their superiority to home-made blankets. Moreover, in their own vicinity the Overtons could undersell the New England blanket manufacturers, because of the saving in transportation costs. 

The Depression of 1873. The panic of 1873 caused the brothers some concern. The demand for wagons decreased and the prices of wheat and corn fell. However, the local demand for blankets remained high because of the severe winter weather. 

The discovery of rich veins of coal near the surface cut the production costs of that commodity, and enabled the enterprise to earn some profits, even in the face of a falling demand. The incomes of the brothers were reduced considerably and they found it necessary to adjust their living expenses accordingly. 

However, they suffered no real hardships, and the recovery from the depression was rapid and thorough-going. 

Transportation.-Between 1875 and 1880 the three Overton brothers died and left their business interests in the hands of their sons.

These sons faced new problems which arose from the increasing complexity of the economic structure within which they lived and worked. 

When the effects of the depression of 1873 had worn off, the development of the country proceeded with increased vigor, especially in the Middle-West, and in the lands west of the Mississippi. It became evident that it would be to the advantage of both East and West to build up transportation facilities for trade between the two regions. A period of feverish railroad building followed.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Mark Overton had shipped commodities to market on flatboats, by canal and river. Later his sons had sent them overland, or by canal, to the nearest railroad. As the region developed, the volume of traffic became so great that these facilities were found unsatisfactory. A railroad was therefore built which ran its tracks directly through the town of Manship. 

This road was of great benefit to the community, for it lowered the cost of transportation and further stimulated expansion of production.

Large-Scale Production. We have seen that a trend toward the formation of large business enterprises appeared directly after the Civil War. In the years following the depression of 

1873, this tendency continued. New and more expensive machinery was developed, which was of great aid to production. 

Large accumulations of capital were necessary to buy these machines and to operate them. The great corporations therefore became more and more important, and in many fields began to displace the individual entrepreneur, such as Jonathan Overton, who ran the little wagon shop.

Indeed, in some cases a business enterprise became so large and powerful that it was able to eliminate all its competitors. 

Such a condition is known as a monopoly. Business men are anxious to create monopolies, because they can thus avoid price wars with their competitors. Many monopolies and semimonopolies were formed, the effect of which was to raise the prices which the Overtons paid for manufactured articles. 

These conditions led them to support the Sherman Anti-Trust Act, the object of which was to curb and eliminate the monopoly powers of the great business enterprises. 

The Sherman Act was only one of many laws passed by the Federal, state, and local governments to protect the people from the difficulties and problems resulting from our rapidly developing economic system. In the days of Mark Overton, such governmental interference in business had been unnecessary, but now the increasing complexity of the economic structure seemed to require it, if the general welfare was to be safeguarded. For example, there was much dissatisfaction with the way in which the railroads were administered, and the government passed legislation intended to correct the bad conditions. There had

been no need to apply governmental regulation to the simple methods of transportation which Mark Overton used (flatboats, Indian trails, etc.).

Factors of Expansion. The careers of the several generations of Overtons reflect the fact that these men lived in a growing or expanding country. Ever since the days of Mark 

Overton, wave after wave of new pioneers had rolled westward, until the Pacific coast was reached. The population grew by leaps and bounds. Men set about the exploitation of the vast natural resources of the country. Cities, farms, mines, ships, railroads and factories sprang up where a few years before there had been only wilderness.

All this created a tremendous demand for goods, both to support the growing population and to outfit the new business enterprises. This demand encouraged men to develop more efficient methods of turning out goods, and mechanized, largescale production appeared. All the productive powers which the 

American people could possibly develop were profitably employed in the internal expansion of the country, that is, in the transformation of a rich but undeveloped continent into one of the greatest industrial and agricultural centers of the world.

This internal expansion was, and still is, the great determining force in the development of our economic life. It must not be supposed, however, that the United States did not have extensive economic contacts with foreign countries. Throughout our history, we imported considerable quantities of finished goods from the more advanced European countries. After 

1870, we began to export vast quantities of grain and other foodstuffs to these same countries. Moreover, in the closing years of the nineteenth century, the United States acquired colonial possessions in which considerable amounts of capital were invested.

In 1914, another important influence appeared. Europe became embroiled in the World War and the United States entered the conflict in 1917. As a result of this, the United 

States became the workshop and food-producing center for a considerable part of the world's population. We increased the output of our farms and factories in order to meet the demands

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

of foreign markets. In other words, external expansion became a dominating factor in our economic life. 

These two factors, internal and external expansion, work in the same general direction, for they both encourage a greater production of goods. They are the two driving forces in the process that has continued uninterruptedly since frontier days, that is, the development of America's productive capacity and the increase in the complexity of her economic structure. 

Throughout the 1920's many new machines and industrial processes were developed, so that production became far more efficient than it had been in the pre-war days. Bank credit flowed more freely than ever before in our history, enabling business men to expand their enterprises and to establish new enterprises. The general public became accustomed to investing their savings in the stocks and bonds of corporations. As a result, these organizations found it easy to accumulate considerable amounts of capital, and could increase the scale of their operations.

Refinement of Consumption Phase of Economics. Since all of the above factors tended to increase primarily our productive capacity, a great amount of finished goods was thrown on the market. Competition among business men became very keen and efforts were made to attract customers by elaborate and expensive advertising campaigns and by "high-pressure" salesmanship. In a further attempt to sell the flood of commodities that our efficient productive system was turning out, installment buying was developed. This means that purchasers are given goods in return for a part payment and a promise to pay the balance on specified dates in the future. Thus, we see that a new form of credit had been developed, which was extended to consumers rather than to producers. 

In spite of these and other devices designed to stimulate business, a great depression swept over the country in 1929. 

This depression, which has been the most severe in modern history, brought about some important changes in our economic structure, the outstanding one being an extension of the governmental regulation of business mentioned above. On the whole, however, the depression has not had the effect of checking our industrial development or of reversing its direction.

SUMMARY Stages of Development.-Our story of the Overton family has illustrated for us the nature and extent of our economic development. In the course of this story, we can distinguish three different stages which reflect the principal changes that have taken place. 

1. Stage of Self-Dependence. This stage is illustrated by the early frontier days of Mark Overton. We have explained that at this time he lived almost entirely on what he himself, with the aid of a few simple tools, was able to win from nature. 

Thus we say that this stage is characterized by almost complete self-dependence. 

2. Stage of the Small Entrepreneur.-This stage is illus trated by the careers of the sons of Mark Overton. These men engaged not only in farming, but also in industry on a small scale. They produced few, if any, of the things that they needed, as contrasted to Mark Overton's almost complete dependence on the products of his own labor. The sons converted their products into money, which they used to obtain all sorts of goods produced by other people. 

The enterprises which they carried on-the wagon shop, the coal mine, and the farm-employed considerably more capital than Mark Overton had ever used, but they were still small enough to be financed by the resources of one or two men. You will recall, however, that when they wished to set up a woolen mill they found that their own capital was inadequate. They therefore established a corporation, by means of which they could make use of the capital of their neighbors. 

3. Stage of Large-Scale Production.-The development of the corporation foreshadowed the next stage in our story-that of large-scale production. This stage was brought about primarily by the development of efficient, but expensive machinery which small entrepreneurs could not afford to acquire. Large corporations, which were able to accumulate great masses of capital, were therefore set up. The Overtons became stockholders, that is, part owners of several of these large corporations.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Large-scale production has grown steadily and today is a more dominant factor in our economic life than ever before. 

Basic Elements of Economics.-You have probably been impressed by the differences between the self-dependence of Mark Overton and the economic conditions of our own time. These differences, while very important, must not make you lose sight of the fact that, today, we are doing basically the same things Mark Overton was doing on the frontier. We are using land, labor, and capital to produce and distribute the various things which are later consumed in the satisfaction of human wants. 

The study of all these factors is known as economics. 

There is nothing mysterious or remote about economics. It deals with the thousand and one activities of our daily liveswith a purchase at the grocery store, the performance of a day's work, or a deposit of funds at the bank. For the most part we shall study these activities as they are carried on today. 

However, it will help you if you will bear in mind that our complex economic system developed gradually from the simple frontier life, and that the basic elements in the two systems are the same.

In the pages which follow, if you find anything which is difficult for you to understand, think of Mark Overton's simple economic life. If you can understand clearly what he was doing, you can understand what 127,000,000 Americans, with their large-scale production and their complicated organization, are doing today.

QUESTIONS 

1. (a) Give a definition of economics. (b) Why is it considered a science? 

2. (a) What is meant by "self-dependence"? (b) What are the advantages and disadvantages of self-dependence? 

3. (a) Why did the Overton brothers find it necessary to form a partnership in order to establish a mine? (b) Why did they find it necessary to form a corporation to establish a woolen mill? 

4. What forces account for the great development of labor-saving machinery in the United States? 

5. Why did the primitive methods of transportation used on the frontier (Indian trails, flatboats, etc.) become inadequate?

6. (a) Why was there no need of banks in the early frontier days? (b) Why did the need arise later? 

7. (a) Explain the rôle which "internal expansion" played in our economic development. (b) Explain how "external expansion" appeared. (c) What effect did this have on our economic development? 

8. (a) What devices did business men use in the post-war period to attract purchasers? (b) Why had the use of these devices become necessary? 

9. (a) Explain why the use of machinery in production became widespread. (b) What connection is there between the use of such machinery and the growth of big business? 

10. Is the need for governmental regulation of our economic affairs greater today than it was in the days of the simple frontier life? Explain. 

11. Explain the basic similarity between the simple economic life of the frontier and the economic life of our own day. 

12. (a) Give as many reasons as you can for studying economics. (b) Why is an understanding of economics particularly valuable today?

CHAPTER II  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To examine the basic institutions of our society. 

2. To explain the rôle which profits play in our economic life. 

3. To define some important terms used in economics. 

BASIC INSTITUTIONS 

Economic Institutions.-An institution may be defined as an established way of carrying on some social activity. Our basic economic institutions, therefore, are the general ways in which we have organized the production, distribution and consumption of commodities and services. 

In the account which we have given of the Overton family the more important of these institutions can easily be recognized. They are private property, private enterprise, competition, and freedom.

Private Property. By the institution of private property we mean the permission granted by the law to private individuals, or groups of private individuals, to hold control over valuable things. This institution is the backbone of our society. 

The fact that we are permitted to keep such things as books, houses, patents, shares of stock of corporations, automobiles, and bank accounts as our own exclusive possessions determines the general way in which we live and work. Mark Overton pushed out into the wilderness and built himself a home because he knew that, after he had completed it, it would belong to him. He left his property to his sons, who, in turn, sought to improve and to add to the valuable things which they possessed. The entire plan of the lives of these men and their descendants would have been different if they had not known that they had the right to hold private property. 16

The government imposes certain restrictions on the right to hold and use private property. For example, the government limits the speed at which you may drive your motor car; it levies taxes on your income and property; it may condemn as unsafe a building which you own and thus prevent you from selling or renting it. All these limitations, however, are made in the general public interest, and do not alter the fact that private property is the most important institution of our society. 

Private Enterprise. The valuable things which individuals are permitted to control in our society do not include only such things as the books, homes and bank accounts which we have mentioned. In addition to these things the Overtons possessed a farm, a coal mine, a wagon shop, a woolen mill, and other business organizations.

The Overtons set up these organizations because they believed (although they could not be sure) that they would benefit from them. They had the right to assume the risk of establishing the farm, factories and mine and to try to operate them in accordance with their best interests. 

All such organizations, owned and controlled by private individuals, or groups of individuals, are known as private enterprises. Almost all the goods which are made in our country, are produced by such private enterprises. 

Private Enterprise and the Government. Most Americans have thought that the government should not engage in business activities to any great extent, especially if these activities can be competently performed by private enterprises. Such institutions as libraries, navy yards, arsenals, schools and the postal system are operated by the government, but in all these instances the object is to perform some essential public service which is necessary to the welfare of all of us, and which has not or cannot be satisfactorily performed by a private enterprise. 

1. Protection. Without the government, however, private property could not exist at all. In the first place, it is the government that gives us the right to hold private property, and, if necessary, defends it for us. If we own a factory or a store, what is to prevent another and stronger person from taking it away from us? It is the government, in the form of the policeman and the law courts, which furnishes this protection. The

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

government also protects business enterprises from unfriendly or harmful acts by foreign nations, and, if necessary, the army and navy may be called out to protect our property rights. 

2. Enforcement of Contracts.-In addition, the government provides courts in which contracts can be enforced. A contract is a promise, made in accordance with certain legal formalities, to perform some act in the future. In the course of business activity such promises become necessary, and the government requires that they be fulfilled. In this way business men are enabled to plan for the future, for they know that they can depend on the promises, which other business men make, to buy goods, to deliver raw materials, to rent buildings, etc. 

3. Government Regulation. The government controls and regulates the activities of business enterprises. The extent of this regulation differs in various fields and at different times. 

For example, in some fields, such as medicine, law, plumbing, etc., a man must obtain a license from the government before he can set up a business enterprise. (This is in contrast to the establishment of the various Overton enterprises.) In other fields, such as railroads, gas and electric companies, etc., the government determines the rates which the public is to be charged. Later in our study, we shall explain in greater detail the many ways in which the government regulates and controls business enterprises. The extent of government regulation is one of the big issues in economics today. 

4. Other Government Functions.-The government performs many other services which are absolutely essential to the functioning of business enterprises. It furnishes a system of money, which enters into all business transactions. It levies tariffs, which protect enterprises from competition by foreign manufacturers. It builds roads, which aid in the transportation of goods. It grants copyrights, franchises, and patents, which give exclusive rights without which certain businesses could not be carried on.

If all these services of the government were withdrawn, private enterprises, as we know them, simply could not exist. 

Competition.—In our country there is usually more than one business enterprise engaged in a given type of activity. In such a case the several firms will try to attract the patronage of

the customers. They are said to be in competition with each other. For example, when the Overtons established their woolen mill they had to meet the competition of New England manufacturers.

Similarly, there is competition among buyers to obtain the goods which are offered on the market. This is generally less spectacular and on a smaller scale than the competition among sellers.

Competition is by no means universal in this country, but it is characteristic of our economic system and is generally regarded as the "normal" condition for the operation of business enterprises.

In a later chapter we shall discuss the advantages and disadvantages of competition in some detail.

Freedom. Since the establishment of our nation it has been our proud boast that "America is a free country." By freedom we mean that man is restricted as little as is compatible with the welfare of other people. For example, Mark Overton was free to leave his old home, to go into a wild region, and to make a new life for himself there. His sons were free to leave their father's farm, and to open a coal mine, a wagon shop, and a woolen mill (private enterprise).

Freedom is restricted by two forces. First, the law may restrict the freedom of anyone, if the good of society seems to demand it. The extensive regulation of business enterprises, to which we made reference above, is an important example of this. Another example is the restriction placed on our personal actions and on our use of personal property. We are free to buy an automobile, but the law does not permit us to drive it down a crowded city street at sixty miles per hour. 

A person's freedom is also restricted by his financial condition. Mark Overton was free to leave home and travel down the 

Mississippi River on a flatboat. Today it is extremely difficult to travel on a river unless you have enough money to buy a boat ticket.

Private property, private enterprise, competition, and freedom are the basic economic institutions in our society today. 

We can summarize our discussion by saying that in this country a person is free to hold private property, and is free to establish

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS PROPERTY PRIVATE AMERICAN ENTERPRISE PRIVATE 

בינז and conduct private business enterprises, generally under competitive conditions.

Profits. No study of our basic institutions is complete, however, without noting the motivating force behind the establishment and control of business enterprises-the desire for profits. 

Most people (including the men who manage many of our great 

ECONOMIC COMPETITION FREEDOM FOUNDATION OF OUR ECONOMIC STRUCTURE 

REL Could we eliminate any of these institutions without disturbing the others? corporations) work for wages of one kind or another, but the firms which employ them are not established primarily to pay wages or to provide the community with goods, but to earn profits. This does not mean, of course, that all business enterprises succeed in earning profits. Many do not succeed. 

Nevertheless, the hope of profits is the basic reason for their establishment and determines the manner in which they are administered.

LIFE CONTRACTS

There is one type of business enterprise in which profits are secondary. This is the field of the professions. A doctor, for example, is a business man, as well as a highly skilled worker, and it is theoretically possible for him to earn profits. However, most doctors receive comparatively high wages for their work and very few of them are concerned with the possibility of making profits.

FUNDAMENTAL DEFINITIONS 

Wants. We have seen that the ultimate purpose of our economic system and of our productive efforts is the satisfaction of our wants. These wants can be divided into two classes, primary and secondary.

Primary wants refer to our need for the essentials of life, such as food and shelter, without which we could not survive. 

Secondary wants are cultural-they arise after the need for food and shelter has been satisfied. These wants refer to our desire for the better things of life, the things which make life more enjoyable. They include such things as education, recreation, automobiles, and refinements in food, clothing and shelter. 

Note how the secondary or cultural wants react upon and modify our primary wants. We all want food, but the kind of food depends on our whole social background. 

Goods. The things which people use for the satisfaction of their primary or secondary wants are known as goods. In our modern society these wants are satisfied, in part, by a great variety of material things, such as bread, and watches, and automobiles.

In addition to such material things we make use of the activities of other people to satisfy our wants. When we attend a play, or receive medical treatment, or have a suit pressed, we are deriving benefit and pleasure, just as we do from eating food or wearing clothes. Such valuable activities, which are performed for the benefit of other people, are called services. 

In simple societies, such as the frontier community in which Mark Overton lived, services were comparatively few in number but in our complex modern life most of us use many services, and they are of the greatest importance. 

The many thousands of commodities and services which we call goods can be divided into free goods and economic goods.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Free goods are those which exist in such abundance, and under such conditions, that all desire for them may be satisfied without exertion or payment. Such things as wind, water, rain, snow, air, light, the beauty of a sunset, etc., ordinarily exist in such abundance that they can be enjoyed by everyone. 

It may be that unusual circumstances alter these conditions of abundance. The extremely dry weather of the summer of 1934 made water so rare in certain portions of the Mississippi valley, that motorists travelling in that region had to pay for drinking water and water for the radiators of their cars. Water under such conditions had lost its quality of abundance and had become an economic good.

Economic goods are those which exist in such limited quantities, and under such conditions, that some exertion or payment must be made to obtain their use. Anything which commands a price is an economic good. Services can be classified as economic goods, for ordinarily they will not be performed unless paid for. It is true that a great deal of medical treatment is given to poor people free of charge, but in the end it is paid for by taxes and by the gifts or endowments made by wealthy people.

Economic goods can be divided into two further classifications, consumers' goods, and producers' goods. Consumers' goods are those which satisfy human wants directly, as is done by a dinner, gasoline in the tank of your automobile, or a copy of a novel. Producers' goods, on the other hand, are not used to satisfy our immediate wants, but to aid in the production of other goods. Such things as the pipe lines of an oil company, bars of pig iron, or a printing press cannot satisfy any human wants directly. They are valuable because they enable us to make consumers' goods or other producers' goods.

Utility. At this point another question arises. Why is it that all goods are not equally valuable? A man uses a straw hat for the purpose of covering his head. A cheap straw hat can be bought for $1.50. However, men have been known to spend as much as $200 for a super-fine panama hat. The explanation of this is to be found in the good itself and in the mind of the buyer. It is a matter of utility. Utility refers to the relative ability of a good to satisfy human wants. The value which

23: we place on goods depends on their utility (on what they can do for us) and on their scarcity. 

Here we have the answer to the question of the hats. The cheap straw hat has only a limited degree of usefulness to its owner. It serves to keep the sun off his head. The expensive panama serves other purposes. It satisfies the owner's pride and his tastes as well as his purely physical requirements. Besides deflecting the sun's rays it supplies evidence of the owner's prosperity to the passer-by; the owner may derive satisfaction, directly, from the handsome appearance of the hat; the wearing of such a hat may be necessary to the owner's social position; it may give him an appearance which will aid him in his business or profession.

To repeat, the panama has a greater range of usefulness than the other hat and for that reason it is valued more highly. A house has far greater utility than the panama hat, and is valued accordingly.

Kinds of Utility. It is possible to add utility to various goods and thus increase their desirability. This added utility may take any of several forms. 

1. Form Utility.-When the nature or appearance of an article has been changed, such as the conversion of logs into lumber, form utility has been added to the good, and its original worth has been increased. 

2. Place Utility.-Again, when the value of the goods has been increased by transporting them, place utility has been added to the original value. Coal has its value increased when it is transported from the mine to the cellar of your house. 

3. Time Utility.-It may be that, as time passes, the value or utility of a good increases. The price of eggs rises during the winter months because of the fact that hens do not lay so many eggs then as they do in the summer. Eggs which are kept in storage from summer to winter have their original value. increased, and are said to possess time utility. 

4. Ownership Utility. This is acquired when goods pass into the hands of the people who are going to consume them. 

A motorboat has more utility for the man who will operate it on the water than it has for the dealer who has, perhaps, hundreds of boats in his stock.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

5. Quantitative Utility.-This is the added value a good acquires when the consumer is enabled to purchase it in convenient quantities. The housewife ordinarily wants to buy butter by the pound and cloth by the yard. The retailer who buys a tub of butter and a bolt of cloth, and then divides these goods into the small portions which are adapted to the needs of the housewife, adds quantitative utility to the original value. 

Wealth. What is wealth? It consists of two great classifications of goods and services: (1) current supplies of economic goods, and (2) the productive capacity to produce more goods. 

Current supplies of economic goods refer to the consumers' goods which are already in existence. Clocks, gloves, grand pianos, carpets and pencils are a few examples of the current supply of economic goods. Productive capacity, or the ability to produce more economic goods, includes such items as mills and shipyards, the ingenuity of inventors, the existence of a body of skilled workers, the presence of raw materials, a stimulating climate, and a high degree of managerial ability. 

Of the two items which constitute wealth, productive capacity is by far the more important. The loss of current supplies of economic goods may be made up readily, if the productive capacity of the individual or the group remains unimpaired. If the productive capacity is impaired, then the individual or the group may face disaster indeed, for when the current supply of economic goods is exhausted, where can man turn for help if his own productive capacity is low?

An example of the great importance of productive capacity can be seen in the cases of Belgium and Armenia after the Great War. In both countries the demands and the destruction of the War had almost completely exhausted the stock of consumers' goods. In Belgium, productive capacity remained comparatively high and it was not long before farms, factories, and all the other agents of production were providing enough goods to support the population. In Armenia, productive capacity had been destroyed along with consumers' goods, and for a number of years the Armenians were supported largely by charity from other nations.

Some people tend to believe that wealth consists of money. 

We can now see that this opinion is mistaken. Money provides

a command over wealth and also serves as a measurement of wealth, but wealth itself consists of commodities and services. 

The possession of money does not mean much to the owner unless he has the opportunity to exchange it for commodities and services. We say that an individual with a large bank account is wealthy because his funds give him command over a wide range of goods. However, if the nation in which he lives is so poor that it cannot provide these (as in Armenia after the World War), his money is of no value to him. On the other hand, in a rich country such as the United States, an individual may be poor because he has not been able to earn the money which will give him control over a part of the national wealth. as Wealth may be measured in two ways. We have already pointed out that it may be measured in terms of money, would be the case if a landowner were to say, "I have a farm worth $60,000." It may also be measured in terms of units, as would be true if another landowner replied to the comment of the former, "Well, that is very interesting. I have a 600 acre farm, also."

Summary. The wants of man are satisfied by two classes of goods, economic goods and free goods. Economic goods consist of current supplies and productive capacity. These two together constitute wealth, which may be measured in terms of money or in units of the goods themselves.

CHAPTER III CONSUMPTION PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning of "consumption." 

2. To discuss the different kinds of consumption. 

3. To indicate the importance of the consumer. 

4. To discuss the forces which influence consumption. 

5. To explain what is meant by a “standard of living."

Definition.-Consumption is the use of goods (commodities and services) in the satisfaction of human wants. 

1. Use of goods means the "destroying" of something at a definite time (eating an apple), or the gradual "wearing out" of something (wearing a pair of shoes over a period of six months), or the "availing oneself" of something (enjoying a movie). 

2. You will recall that in our discussion of goods in the preceding chapter, we distinguished between (a) consumers' goods, which are used in the direct satisfaction of human wants (a meal, a pair of shoes, or the performance of a movie star), and (b) producers' goods, which are used for the production of other goods (the seed and fertilizer which the farmer uses to raise his crop, a shoe-making machine, or the studio in which moving pictures are made). Consumption includes the use of both these types of goods. You use an electric toaster to make yourself some toast. Not only do you consume the toast, but you are also using up or consuming the toaster. 

Importance of Consumption.-You can see from what we have said that consumption is the end or goal of all the efforts we make to produce commodities and services. While it is true that many people derive pleasure and satisfaction from their work, the principal reason for the activity of all people who do 27

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

productive work, whether they be laborers, teachers, business men, or members of any trade or profession, is to provide the goods which people will consume or use. Our farms, factories, stores, railroads, etc., exist only because we need the things they produce. 

A MAN IS WHAT HE CONSUMES WHISH EXCESSIVE LIQUOR CONSTANT GAMBLING BAD LITERATURE CHEAP MUSIC CLEAN LIVING CHURCHES GOOD BOOKS CONCERTS ATHLETICS 

Does consumption affect our national as well as our individual well-being?

To a great extent, the things we consume determine the type of life we lead. In the first place, if we do not have good food and adequate shelter we cannot be healthy. When we have gone beyond the necessities of life, we see also that such things as character, culture, and outlook on life are greatly influenced by consumption. The man who consumes too much liquor becomes a drunkard. The man who consumes books, lectures, and instruction becomes a scholar. In the same way, the general character of a nation, the level of its civilization, depends on its consumption. The difference between a civilized nation, such as the United States, and a tribe of savages in the interior of

CONSUMPTION 

29 Africa, can be expressed in terms of the goods which they consume.

Influences Affecting the Consumer. We have seen that consumption is one of the most important activities of our lives, both as individuals and as a nation. Now the question arises, "Since a tremendous supply of commodities and services is available, how do we come to choose the particular things which we do consume?" What are the forces which influence us when we go out into the market to make our purchases? These forces can be divided into two general classes: (1) man's needs and desires, and (2) the supply of commodities and services which he can obtain.

NEEDS AND DESIRES COMMON TO ALL Physical Requirements.-Certain of man's needs and de sires are common to all of us. We all have nearly the same physical requirements. We all want a certain amount of food, clothing and shelter because our physical well-being requires that we have these things. Similarly, such services as transportation, protection and medical attention are needs which are determined by our physical relationship to our environment. 

Common Laws of Consumption. There are certain general similarities about the way we all derive satisfaction from the use of goods. These similarities, which determine our choice of the things we consume, have been summarized and written down. in the form of "laws," which may be described as follows: 

1. Law of Satiety.-At a given time, any particular want can be satisfied, but other unsatisfied wants will rise to take its place. It may be said, therefore, that for all practical purposes the total of human wants is unlimited. You may have enough food after eating a meal, but then you are ready to consume something else, such as a book or a movie. 

2. Law of Variety.-More satisfaction can be derived from the consumption of many different commodities than from the consumption of an equal amount of one commodity or a few commodities. We prefer to satisfy our hunger with a sevencourse meal rather than with a large amount of bread and water. 

3. Law of Harmonious Consumption.-Commodities give greater satisfaction when properly combined than when im-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

66 properly combined or used separately. For example, we try to dress so that our various articles of clothing harmonize or go together" well. 

4. Law of Economic Order of Consumption.-When a pur chaser has to make a choice among a number of commodities, he will select the one the consumption of which gives him the greatest satisfaction. When you spend your allowance, you first buy the thing you want the most. If you have any money left over you will buy the article for which your desire is next greatest, and so on. It may be that you will save some of your allowance because you believe that you can obtain greater satisfaction by buying something in the future rather than at the present. 

5. Law of Diminishing Utility. The desire on the part of a consumer for a commodity falls as the amount of that commodity which he possesses or has consumed increases. For example, as you eat successive pieces of chocolate, your desire for more chocolate will grow less and less. You will eventually arrive at a point where you have no desire for more chocolate. 

VARIABLE NEEDS AND DESIRES 

Individual Variations.-We have examined that phase of our needs and desires which is more or less uniform for all human beings. However, nothing is more obvious than that desires vary from individual to individual and from nation to nation. What accounts for this variation? In the first place, our desires are influenced by our general character and make-up, by the kind of people we are. Two given persons will both want food, but after this want is satisfied one may spend the remainder of his money on liquor, and the other on a ticket to a symphony concert. Opinions may vary as to the cause of this difference in tastes, but no one can deny that different individuals are so constituted that they want different things. 

Social Variations.-Another important force which results in varying desires among different people is the influence of the traditions and standards of the society in which we live. 

Americans want more and better things than do Chinese coolies because our standards, our ideas of the supply and quality of the commodities necessary for a comfortable life, are different from the Chinese standards. Thus, many Americans would

CONSUMPTION 

31 consider themselves in miserable circumstances if they were deprived of the use of an automobile. It would never occur to a 

Chinese coolie to want an automobile. He is content if he gets enough food to live.

Another example of the power which social forces exert over our consumption is the existence of styles and fashions. Why do we all want to wear clothes of the latest style? Old-fashioned clothes protect us from the elements just as well and in many cases are as decorative. However, we prefer the up-todate garments and, if we can, we buy them because people all around us are wearing them. Human beings are naturally imitative. Very few of us like to be conspicuously different.

Therefore, we follow the standards which our society sets down for us.

In this connection we must consider the effects of advertising and salesmanship. By these devices manufacturers and storekeepers are often able to increase the number and the intensity of our desires. Practically all of us have had the experience of forming a desire for a thing as a result of reading an advertisement or a series of advertisements. Of course, a great deal of advertising is directed not so much toward the creation of new wants as it is toward influencing our choice among competing brands.

ACCESSIBILITY OF GOODS 

Another general set of influences on consumption is the supply of goods on the market and our ability to purchase them. 

Supply of Goods. Obviously a nation cannot consume what it has not first produced, or obtained in exchange for what it has produced. The productivity of the Chinese population of 

300 million people is low and, by our standards, the supply of goods available for their use is pitifully inadequate. The consumption of the people of the United States is limited, in the same way, by their particular level of productivity. 

Ability to Purchase. From the individual's point of view, however, the total supply of goods is not the only factor. His consumption is also determined by his purchasing power. It need hardly be said that a millionaire consumes more and dif-

Influence of the Government.-The government exerts an influence on our consumption which has become prominent recently and may become more and more important in the future. The general purpose of the government's activities is to protect the consumer. To this end many laws have been passed, local as well as Federal, which prescribe standards of purity for foods and drugs, forbid misinformation in labelling and advertising, and ban the production of certain types of goods altogether. There are some indications that in the future there will be a further extension of government activity to protect the consumer.

Ignorance of the Consumer. Thus we see that a complex set of influences is at work, guiding our consumption of commodities and services. It may occur to you, however, that all the influences which we have listed include very little that can be considered knowledge of what goods are best suited to satisfy our needs. Unfortunately, this lack of knowledge is characteristic of most modern consumers. What is the quality and what is the usefulness of the things which you purchase? The purchasing agent of a large corporation will probably know whether it is better to buy a low-priced oil for a motor truck or whether it is better to buy a high-priced oil. He knows because of his technical training or because he has made tests of the performances of the various oils. Which is the best for your car? You don't know and neither does any other average person. Oil is only one commodity. You have a vital interest in hundreds of other commodities and you probably know as little about them as you do about the oil. 

Guides to Intelligent Consumption.-We consume intelligently when, within the limits of our income, we choose those goods which are best suited to satisfy our needs and desires. 

Thus, intelligent consumption means the best possible use of our individual and national wealth and a higher degree of individual and national development.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

What agencies exist in our country which help us to consume more intelligently? 

1. The government, through some of its departments, supplies information on the quality of various commodities, but this service has little or no influence in directing the purchases of the average man. Either he does not know about the information which the government distributes, or he does not care to go to the trouble of sending for it. The intelligent consumer should make use of this important free information.

2. High schools and colleges offer courses in which attempts are made to analyze and test products on the basis of usefulness. 

Although people of high school age are usually not vitally interested in such commonplace things as the price of pork chops, the information gained should stand them in good stead in later life. 

3. Private agencies, such as Consumer's Research, make scientific tests of a great many different commodities and publish reports of their findings. The information thus made public

Standard of Living. We have explained that the various influences which guide consumption differ from person to person and from nation to nation. As a result, the consumption of the millionaire is different from that of the poor man, and the consumption of the people of the United States is different from that of the people of China.

In any of these cases, the individual or individuals concerned become accustomed to a particular quantity and quality of commodities and services. These commodities and services, taken as a whole, constitute the standard of living of that individual or group. For example, it may be said that the American standard of living is higher than the French standard of living, and that the French standard is higher than the Chinese. It is generally believed that the average American today enjoys a higher standard of living than any ever attained by the common people in the history of the world. An economist has attempted to define the American standard of living by listing the various things which he thinks are demanded and obtained by the average American family. 

An automobile. 

Part ownership of a modern home.

Living room, dining room, and bedroom furniture. 

Domestic rugs. 

Radio, electric iron, vacuum cleaner.

Bakers' bread, canned goods, prepared cereals. 

Meat once a day, butter, branded coffee. 

Ice cream on Sunday in the summer, candy, tobacco. 

Silk stockings and silk underwear for the wife and daughter. 

Diamond engagement ring, wedding ring, wrist watch for the wife. 

A good watch for the husband. 

A haircut once in two weeks for the husband; a finger wave once a month for the wife.

Nationally advertised soap and tooth paste. 

Wash sent out, done mechanically or done by a hired laundress. 

Movies once a week; a trip in the car on Sunday.

One newspaper and two magazines. 

High school education; a year's music or dancing lessons.

The wife should not need to work for wages. 

Savings of $200 a year.

Insurance policy of $500 for the husband. 

Burial insurance for the other members. 

Medical and dental care.

These items seem to present a good picture of the way in which Americans of average means live. The high standard is due partly to our environment and partly to our own efforts. 

1. The great productive capacity of the North American continent, with its fertile soil, its great mines and forests, and its cool stimulating climate is extremely important. 

2. The vast amounts of capital goods which we have accumulated, such as machines, factories, etc., are another factor. 

3. Efficient labor provides still another factor. 

4. A fourth factor lies in our attitude toward consumption. 

Americans demand comfort and are willing to work for it and do not hesitate to spend money for the better things of life. 

Statistics of Consumption.-Economists have been interested in investigating the consumption of groups with different standards of living. They felt that if they studied a large number of cases they might be able to learn something which is typical of consumption under different standards of living. For example, a study was made in the United States for the years 1918 and 1919 by the Bureau of Labor Statistics at Washington. Their findings are summarized in the table on page 37.* 

This table shows that as the income of the American family increases- 1. The percentage of the income which is spent for food, fuel, light, and rent decreases, while 2. The percentage which is spent for clothing, furniture and furnishings, and miscellaneous items increases. 

The general meaning of these facts is that poor people spend a greater part of their incomes on the bare necessities of life than prosperous people do and, therefore, a smaller part on such things as amusements, education, attractive clothing and fur-

Moreover the small expenditure for education often means that the poverty-stricken individual cannot prepare himself for an occupation which would enable him to rise from his poverty. These statements do not apply only to the United States in 1918. 

With some slight modifications they are valid for any society in which differences of wealth appear.'

We have said above that our individual and our national character and welfare depend largely on our consumption. 

Here we have a very important illustration of this fact. Poverty, which means a low level of consumption, will tend to breed more poverty, ignorance, bad health, crime, and a low level of social usefulness. Our national strength suffers accordingly, for no nation can be stronger than the individuals who compose it. 

Therefore, the elimination of poverty is probably the greatest social problem which confronts us today. This means merely * The first scientific investigation into these facts was made about 1850 by Dr. Ernst Engel, a German statistician. Engel's investigation did not reveal exactly the same things as does our table for the United States in 1918, but he did arrive at the same general conclusion, that is, that poor people spend a greater part of their income for the necessities of life than prosperous people do. This conclusion has become known as Engel's Law.

that the general level of consumption of the poorer classes must be raised. In the United States many steps have been taken toward the solution of this problem. One of the most important is our provision for free popular education. In this way a valuable service is made available to everyone, and members of poor families receive training which may enable them to improve their standard of living.
