CHAPTER IV THE  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning of "production." 

2. To discuss the various factors involved in production. 

3. To explain the Law of Diminishing Returns. 

4. To describe "self-sufficiency" and "division of labor." 

5. To discuss the nature of large-scale production. 

6. To point out the advantages and disadvantages of large-scale production.

Definition.-Production is the creation of commodities and services for the satisfaction of our own or someone else's wants. 

Factors of Production. The production of commodities and services is largely a matter of combination. For example, land if properly combined with labor, seeds, and tools will produce a crop. The factors of production are the various elements which are combined to produce goods. 

1. Land is one factor of production. As this term is used here, it includes not only land which is used for farming purposes, and urban land, but also mines, forests, rivers, seas, and waterfalls. Thus we see that land is the source of all raw materials and furnishes the sites for productive enterprises. 

2. Labor is a second factor of production. Labor is human effort, mental or physical, which is used for productive purposes. 

3. Capital is a third factor of production. By capital we mean those things which man has produced to aid in further production. The land which the farmer cultivates is a creation of nature, but the plough which he uses on the land, and which is necessary to the production of the crop, is made by human efforts. It is a form of capital. 40

4. The entrepreneur, or business man, is a fourth factor of production. His function is to combine the other factors into a productive business unit. The entrepreneur brings together elements of land, labor, and capital and manages them so that commodities and services are produced. The farmer, the factory owner, and the storekeeper are all entrepreneurs. 

5. The state is a fifth factor in production. We have seen in an earlier chapter that the state protects private property, which is our basic economic institution, enforces contracts, and provides a system of money.

In addition, the state engages directly in many important productive activities, such as the provision of gas, water, and electric power and the operation of the postal system. Transportation, which is essential to production, is greatly facilitated by the roads which are built, maintained and owned by the state. 

The state aids some industries by distributing important information, such as statistics which are compiled by government agencies. Other industries (e.g., shipping) have been favored with grants of money (subsidies) or of public lands by the state. 

The state provides tariff laws by which foreign goods are prevented from competing with the products of our own manufacturers. Such matters as relations between employer and employee and the quality of the goods manufactured are extensively regulated by the state.

These are only a few of the functions performed by the state which influence production. The importance of the state in production will be considered more fully in the succeeding chapters. 

Law of Diminishing Returns.—In all forms of productive activity, producers eventually encounter an obstacle known as diminishing returns. A good example of the way this law affects production is to be found in the case of land. 

Suppose that a farmer cultivating a wheat farm applies a total of 500 hours of labor to his land in the course of a year. 

He finds that under these conditions his land yields an annual crop of 500 bushels. The following year, the farmer hires a laborer to help him, so that 1000 hours of labor are applied to the land, which now yields a crop of 1200 bushels. The farmer is encouraged by this increase in productivity and in the third

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

year he hires still another man. The three men apply 1500 hours of labor to the land in the course of the year, and they produce a crop of 1450 bushels. 

Now let us summarize all these facts:

Number of Hours Worked 

500 1000 1500

Number of Bushels 

Average Yield Produced

Per Hour 

500 1 1200 1.2 1450 .97 

We can see from these figures that when the hours of labor applied to the farm are increased beyond 1000 (while the other factors of production remain unchanged) there is not a proportional increase in the amount of wheat produced. In other words, when we go beyond 1000 hours, in this particular case, the average returns for each hour of labor diminish. In the case of this particular farm 1000 hours of labor is the point of diminishing returns.

This principle would have asserted itself in essentially the same way if the farmer had chosen to increase the amount of capital in use (fertilizer, machines, etc.) without increasing the labor, or if he had chosen to increase both the capital and the labor. In either case he would have eventually arrived at a point of diminishing returns. 

This Law of Diminishing Returns, of course, does not apply only to farming. It applies to manufacturing, storekeeping, transportation, and all forms of productive activity. For example, a storekeeper may find that if he engages another clerk he will be able to serve his customers more efficiently and thus increase the total profits which his store yields. If he engages still another clerk, he may obtain similar results. If he continues to hire clerks, however, he will eventually arrive at a point where the increase in business will not be proportional to the increased labor cost. He has arrived at the point of diminishing returns. The storekeeper would ultimately obtain the same results if he made his store larger by renting more land, or if he invested additional capital in the form of attractive fixtures and a better and more varied stock.

The Law of Diminishing Returns can be stated in its general form as follows: There is a point in all productive activity be-

yond which added use of one or several factors will not increase the total output in proportion to the increased use of those factors; that point is known as the point of diminishing returns. 

METHODS OF PRODUCTION 

Self-Sufficiency. We have seen that in Mark Overton's early days on the frontier he himself produced almost all the goods which he consumed, that is, he was self-sufficient. This primitive method of production makes it impossible for the selfsufficient individual to accumulate the producers' goods and to acquire the skill which will make him efficient in all the various fields of production. Because of this, he cannot produce a great deal of goods and a low standard of living results. 

Division of Labor.-Division of labor takes place when selfsufficiency is abandoned and men work together in such a way that each individual specializes in a particular type of work. 

We may distinguish three different kinds of division of labor. 

1. Simple Division of Labor.-You will recall that the sons of Mark Overton, and their neighbors, were not self-sufficient, as their fathers had been, but confined their productive activities to a single commodity (coal, wagons, food, etc.). Men acquired skill at their specialized work and a greater amount of goods was made available for the use of the community. The community itself, however, was still largely self-sufficient. 

2. Territorial Division of Labor.-Territorial division of labor appears when an entire area (a city, a state, or a country) specializes in a particular type of work. This may be caused by various factors, among which are: (a) advantages of soil and climate (production of rice in South Carolina); (b) nearness to market (truck farming around New York City); (c) nearness to raw materials (steel production in Pittsburgh); (d) nearness to suitable labor supply (manufacture of clothing in New York 

City); (e) advantages resulting from an early start (textile industry in New England); and (f) artificial advantages. The artificial advantages include tax exemption, tariffs and subsidies, and variations in the laws of states or countries.* *Other things being equal, if the laws of two states are different, an industry will develop in the state that has the more favorable legislation.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

We are now in a position to see how the territorial division of labor has destroyed the self-sufficiency of the community of which we spoke above. If a community or a nation confines its productive activities to one or to several of the many things which the inhabitants need, it will have to import the other necessities and comforts of life from outside sources. In ex- change it will offer the commodities in which it specializes and

Explain the advantages which have led to the geographical centralization of each of the industries indicated on the map. which it has produced in abundance. Thus, we need the coffee of Brazil and Brazil needs our machinery. This is national interdependence. Inside our own country, the great wheatgrowing areas of the Middle-West exchange their grain for the finished goods of the industrial East. This is regional interdependence. In the modern world virtually no nation or region produces all the things it needs. Instead there is specialization and, as we have seen, specialization means exchange and interdependence. 

3. Technical Division of Labor.-Technical division of labor appears when men perform limited or specialized tasks within a

particular industry. For example, in the automobile industry today there are almost one hundred operations involved in the assembling of the motor alone, and each of these operations is performed by a different man. Each operation is simplified as much as possible and the operator acquires great skill and speed at it.

Technical division of labor has two definite implications. 

First, it implies coöperation, the coöperation of considerable numbers of men working together at specialized tasks in shop and factory. The second implication is the use of large amounts of capital, or producers' goods. Labor cannot be divided to any great extent unless mechanical equipment is used. Large amounts of money are generally required to buy such machinery. 

Since this is so, the technical division of labor cannot be applied to any great extent by small enterprises. For example, there could not be much division of labor in Jonathan Overton's little wagon factory. The manufacture of a wagon is a complicated job and since only a few men were employed, without many tools, each man performed a considerable part of the work that went into the construction of a single unit. 

In short, intensive technical division of labor can be applied only to largescale capitalist production.

LARGE-SCALE PRODUCTION Nature of Large-Scale Production. By large-scale produc tion we mean the making of goods in great quantities by mechanized, standardized methods. The goods are not produced for the use of any particular individual, but for a market which may be nation-wide or world-wide. The producer does not know who is going to use his goods, but he produces them in the belief that there will be a demand for them. In other words, large-scale production is production in anticipation of demand. 

Large-scale production presupposes the combination of a great deal of capital, or producers' goods. This combination may be of two sorts, horizontal or vertical. A horizontal combination is a combination of producers' goods which are used for the same general purpose (a combination of two coal mines or two railroads). A vertical combination is a combination of several different types of producers' goods which represent different stages

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

in the production of a single commodity. For example, a combination of an iron mine, a steel mill, a railroad, and an automobile plant would be vertical. The same company (or its own affiliates) would mine the iron ore, convert it into steel, ship it to the automobile plant, and there manufacture the automobiles, the finished product.

Roundabout Production.-When the pioneer of frontier days needed a pair of shoes, he first went into the forest and killed an animal. Then he took the hide of this animal and, with the aid of a few simple and indispensable tools, he fashioned it into a finished product. Such a method as this, which involves very little capital, is called direct production of goods. 

In modern large-scale production, on the other hand, we use a great deal of capital, and our methods are said to be roundabout. Before we manufacture a pair of shoes, we go through many preliminary steps. Coal and iron ore are mined to produce the metal which will be turned into shoe-making machines. 

These machines are leased or sold to an entrepreneur. The entrepreneur selects a location, erects a factory building (for which the materials, steel, lumber, concrete, etc., have been previously produced), installs the machinery, employs workmen, and finally gets down to the job of making shoes. 

Advantages of Large-Scale Production.-Why do business men choose to employ large-scale production, which involves such long and costly roundabout processes? They do so because of certain very important advantages. 

1. Savings in Labor Costs.-Large-scale production makes possible the use of expensive machines which greatly increase the productivity of the worker. Moreover, as explained above, the intensive technical division of labor becomes practicable. The worker can learn his simple task quickly and develops great speed at it. The work can be arranged so that there is a minimum of waste time and effort. 

2. Savings in the Costs of Raw Materials. The large-scale producer buys his materials in large quantities; this generally means at a low price. For example, a farmer who is trying to sell a crop of five thousand pounds of a particular commodity will be more inclined to accept a lower price per pound if the

purchaser will take his entire crop than he would if the purchaser wanted to buy only 100 pounds. 

3. Savings in Transportation Expenses.-Large enterprises can establish factories in various parts of the country and ship goods to market from the nearest plant. In addition, transportation charges per unit are often less if goods are shipped in bulk.

THE LARGE-SCALE PRODUCER ADVERTISING, SUPERIOR QUALITY EFFICIENT PRODUCTION LOW PRICES, UNIFORMITY. 

289 13 

LOW FIXED CHARGES PERSONAL CONTACTS 

LARGE- SCALE PRODUCER PATRONAGE OF THE PUBLIC 

SMALL- 

SCALE PRODUCER 

In what fields are the advantages of the large-scale producer of comparatively little importance? 

4. Savings Effected by Research Work.-Many great enterprises, such as the General Electric Company, maintain staffs of scientists and technicians. These men do scientific research work, the object of which is to find means of improving the product and the method of manufacture. 

5. Savings in the Use of By-Products.-It often happens that the waste products which appear in the course of the manufacture of a commodity can be made into useful articles. Largescale industry, with its mechanical equipment and its technical experts, is well equipped to take advantage of these so-called

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

by-products. For example, the large meat-packing concerns use the bristles from the hides of the animals for the manufacture of brushes. 

6. Advantages in Marketing.-Large-scale producers can make extensive use of advertising. They can take advantage of opportunities to sell their goods in distant parts of the country or in foreign countries. Some large-scale producers have opened retail stores to serve as outlets for their wares. 

7. Superior Quality.-Because of their efficient methods, large-scale producers can give us goods of superior quality. 

This is particularly true of products which require a high degree of precision, such as fountain pens, tools, automobiles, etc. In addition, the goods of large-scale producers are usually uniform in quality. Every Ford car is exactly like every other 

Ford car of the same model. In other words, these goods are standardized, that is, they follow a single pattern very closely in all details. This increases our confidence and our willingness to buy, for we know that there are no special defects or irregularities in the particular automobile which we will receive for our money.

Effect of Large-Scale Production on Consumption.-As a result of all these advantages, large-scale production has enormously increased the amount of goods which is available for the use of society and has raised the general standard of living. 

The great expansion in production has brought with it a shortening of the working week. The forty-hour week is well established today, while in 1840 it was not unusual to see men, women and children working fourteen hours a day in the few mills and factories then in existence.

Large-scale production has recently brought about one change in the nature of our consumption which is destined to become more important in the future. This is the tendency to buy a new product instead of repairing the old one. This is particularly true of such articles as cheap watches, fountain pens, and sun glasses. It is actually cheaper today to have a new motor installed in your Ford than to have the old motor thoroughly overhauled. The efficiency and productivity of largescale production is making possible this desirable transition from repair to replacement.

Disadvantages of Large-Scale Production 

1. Character of Work.-In large-scale production the operations of the workmen are so standardized and simplified that the work often becomes extremely monotonous. The employee does his work in a mechanical manner and he has little or no opportunity to show special intelligence or ability. 

2. Possibility of a Breakdown.-Large-scale production, as we have seen, involves a high degree of coöperation. Consequently, if there is a breakdown at one single point, the entire system of roundabout production is blocked. Suppose, for example, that the electric generating plant in your city were to break down. There is scarcely a business enterprise in the city that would not be affected. The manufacturing plants which use electric current for power would be forced to suspend all operations. Stores and offices which use electric lights would be greatly handicapped. Trolley systems and subways would not be able to run.

Even doctors and dentists would not be able to treat their patients. The generation of electric power is only one step in the long series of roundabout processes involved in the production of each of these goods and services, but if there is a breakdown at this single point, all further activity is blocked. 

3. Fixed Charges.-We have said that large-scale production is possible only through the use of great and costly industrial plants. Large factories and their equipment cannot be purchased outright, for no one has that much money at any one time. Business men borrow funds to establish these great enterprises and bind themselves to repay the loan at some specific date. Interest must be paid regularly whether or not the business is profitable. These interest charges are known as fixed charges because they remain the same regardless of the amount of business transacted. Other charges of a fixed type are taxes, rent, insurance, depreciation costs, and the salaries of those key employees who must be retained even when business is slow.* 

All of these costs remain fairly constant, whether a business is producing to the limit of its capacity or is at a standstill. * Fixed charges are to be distinguished from operating costs (such as raw materials and general labor costs) which vary with the volume of production.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

50 Because of these fixed charges (which are sometimes referred to as overhead costs) business men have an incentive to produce to the limit of the capacity of their plants. This is so because the same fixed costs must be met in any case and it is desirable to have them distributed over a large number of produced units. 

If this is done the total cost per unit (fixed charges plus operating costs) will be less and the opportunity for profits greater. 

Suppose, however, that the market for a product has decreased to such a degree that a plant is practically at a standstill. The plant cannot be used to manufacture another product because it has been designed and is useful for only one purpose. 

Nevertheless the business man must meet his fixed charges or go into bankruptcy. If, as sometimes occurs, this happens to many entrepreneurs at the same time, we have a situation which may lead to a great panic and depression.* Small-scale and selfsufficient production, in which capital and fixed charges are comparatively unimportant, are not subject to this great danger. 

4. Standardization.-We have previously listed standardization as one of the advantages of large-scale production. This is true in cases in which a high degree of precision and uniformity is necessary, as in most machines, automobiles, medicines, building materials, etc. In some other cases, however, the standardization introduced by the machine age is not altogether desirable. 

It results in a dull, depressing monotony in such fields as clothing, houses, amusements, and, all too often, in thought. Moreover, this universal similarity is frequently on a comparatively low level. For example, in many parts of the country one can see long rows of unattractive houses, all exactly alike. From the point of view of beauty and the development of a national culture, this sort of standardization is undesirable. 

Limitations of Large-Scale Production.-The advantages of large-scale production so far outweigh the disadvantages that it has become the dominant method of production in the modern world. However, there are some businesses to which all these advantages do not apply and in which extreme degrees of specialization are not profitable. Businesses which are based upon * The subject of panics and depressions will be considered more fully in the chapter on the business cycle (page 312).

personal services and upon the personality of the producer fall in this class. For example, such services as legal and medical advice, where individual skill and the confidence of the consumer play an important part, are not adapted to the methods of large-scale production. The same is true of the production of certain luxury goods and works of art. In these cases the personal skill of the worker cannot be replaced by the methods of mechanized large-scale production. In addition, many small retail businesses flourish because people like to deal with an individual storekeeper whom they know personally.

In spite of these few exceptions, large-scale production is the leading method of making goods in the modern world and year by year increases in extent and importance. 

Summary.-Consumers, in order to satisfy their wants, make choices among the goods which are offered for sale. These goods have been placed on the market by producers, who have tried to anticipate the desires of the consumers. The producer engages in these activities because he hopes to make profits.

CHAPTER V  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning of "land." 

2. To point out the limited nature of land and its consequences. 

3. To show how the Law of Diminishing Returns applies to land. 

4. To explain the meaning of “rent.” 

5. To show why rent is paid. 

6. To explain how rent determines land values. 

7. To examine the ideas of Henry George.

LAND 

Definition. In economics, the term "land" refers to all natural resources. It includes mines, farms, timberlands, bodies of water, city lots, and the air itself. Thus we see that land is the source of all food and raw materials and provides the locations on which we erect buildings and other structures. No production of any kind is possible without the use of land. 

Limited Nature of Land.—The supply of land available for our use is limited. The supply of labor, as represented by the population, can grow and has grown very rapidly. The same is true of the supply of capital. When Mark Overton settled in 

Indiana, there was very little labor and capital in that region. 

Today, it is thickly populated with skilled workers of all sorts and there is a tremendously valuable supply of capital goods, such as machines, factory buildings and produced raw materials. 

At the same time, there is no more land in Indiana today than in Mark Overton's day. Land is a creation of nature which man can use but which he cannot himself produce. The number of square miles on the surface of the earth is definitely limited, and the natural resources which the earth contains, coal, oil, timber, etc., are limited in the same way. 53

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Conservation. Since land is limited, it is desirable that we use it with extreme care. The Conservation Movement aims: (1) To avoid all waste in the use of our natural resources; and (2) By the application of science and scientific methods, to exploit more thoroughly and effectively the land (farming land and natural resources) at our disposal. Man cannot increase the amount of land which Nature has given to him, but he can use it so that it yields greater wealth. (See Conservation, Chapter 

XIX.) 

Methods of Cultivating Land.-In some parts of the world, land is relatively more limited or scarce than in others. As a result of these differences, two general methods of cultivating land can be distinguished. 

1. The intensive method of cultivation is used in densely populated countries, that is, countries where there is a small amount of land in proportion to the number of inhabitants. 

Under these conditions a great deal of labor and capital will be applied to a given piece of land. Most European farming land is cultivated intensively. 

2. Extensive cultivation is used in countries in which land. is relatively plentiful, as in the United States. The American farmer does not apply so much labor and capital to a given acre as does the European, because the American has comparatively much more land to farm and he can raise a satisfactory crop without attempting to extract the maximum yield from each acre. 

Law of Diminishing Returns as Applied to Land.-Like all other factors of production, land is subject to the Law of Diminishing Returns. If we continue to apply more capital, or labor, or both to a given piece of land, we will eventually arrive at a point where the added yield is not proportionate to the increased use of these factors of production. You will recall that in the example given in Chapter IV, Production, the farmer who hired additional laborers to work on his land found that, after a certain number of men had been hired, the average output for each man-hour began to drop.

If a farmer continues to add capital or labor (or both) to his land until the added yield just covers the added cost of production, he is said to have reached the intensive margin of cultiva-

tion. If the farmer were cultivating extensively, he would not put so much labor and capital into any single tract of land, but would bring new land under cultivation. (See extensive margin of cultivation, page 59.)

The Law of Diminishing Returns applies also to urban land. 

Capital and labor are applied to urban land by erecting buildings upon it. The size of the building indicates the builder's estimation of the point of diminishing returns, that is, the point beyond which it would not be profitable to add more capital and labor to the land by increasing the size of the building. 

In cities such as New York, where there is a great demand for factory and office space and people are willing to pay very high rentals, it is profitable to continue building long after the point of diminishing returns would have been reached in a smaller city. This explains why buildings are so much taller in New 

York than they are in small cities.

However, even the great skyscrapers are limited by the Law of Diminishing Returns. It is possible to erect taller buildings than the Empire State but it has not been done because the builders do not believe that the great expense involved would result in proportionate profits.* Indeed, there is some doubt as to whether the Empire State building is not built beyond the point of diminishing returns.

RENT The term "rent" is used in two senses: economic rent and commercial rent.

Definitions 

1. Economic rent refers to the earning capacity of land, that is, the net return or surplus which results from the use of a piece of land. In the case of a coal mine, for example, the economic rent would be the value of the coal it yielded over and above all the costs (capital and labor) involved in mining the coal. 

2. Commercial rent is the price paid to the owner for the temporary use of land. This may include the use of capital * The upper stories of a skyscraper are more expensive to build than the lower stories because more power, care, and time must be used than on the lower level.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

goods, such as buildings, which stand upon the land. (Commercial rent is sometimes known as contract rent.) 

Commercial and Economic Rent.-When we use the word rent in everyday speech, we generally mean commercial rent. 

For example, if a farmer rents land in order to cultivate it, the money which he pays to the landlord is commercial rent. However, the net income earned as a result of the fertility of the land itself (because of which he is willing to pay the landlord) is economic rent.

We can see from this that the commercial rent for a piece of land (exclusive of the capital goods which may stand upon the land) tends to be about the same as the economic rent. If the commercial rent is greater, the tenant will be paying more for the use of the land than the land yields to him. On the other hand, if the commercial rent is less than the economic rent, other people will become aware of this, in time, and will offer the landlord a higher price (commercial rent) for the use of his land.*

We must realize, however, that there is only a tendency for commercial rent to equal economic rent. A number of factors may keep them apart. For example, the landlord or the tenant may fail to estimate the productivity of the land correctly. (This is especially common in the case of urban land.) In addition, leases of land are often made for a long period so that the same commercial rent must be paid although the economic rent may have risen or fallen. 

Origin of Rent. When Mark Overton first settled in the frontier country, he chose the best land he could find and began to cultivate it. (The land was "best" because it was the most fertile, the most advantageously located, and required the least work to clear it of trees and undergrowth.) When other settlers arrived, there was plenty of good land available for them, and they began to farm land which was comparable in quality to that of Mark Overton. Let us say that land of this type is grade A land. * All this, of course, does not apply to land which is used for nonproductive purposes, such as residences. The tenant of this land does not receive an economic rent. He pays a commercial rent to the landlord as he would pay for other consumers' goods.

You will recall that the population of the region grew very rapidly. Settlers who came several years later found that all the grade A land was occupied. However, there was a great demand for wheat and other foodstuffs, and these newcomers decided that it would pay to cultivate the next best land which was available (grade B land), although it was not so fertile as grade A land and less advantageously located. The yield from this grade B land must have covered at least the costs of production, or it would not have been cultivated. 

The growing demand and the higher prices for wheat made it possible to cultivate grade B land. These same forces, of course, increased the economic rent which could be gained by the cultivation of grade A land. Naturally, all newcomers were anxious to get some grade A land to cultivate. Since it was all occupied, they could obtain it only by purchase or by paying a sum of money (commercial rent) for its temporary use. The amount which they were willing to pay was the amount by which the yield from grade A land exceeded the yield from the free grade B land.

The population and the demand for wheat continued to grow and before long settlers found that the returns from land of a still lower quality, grade C land, were sufficient to cover the costs of production. Therefore, this land came into cultivation. 

These same forces which made it profitable to cultivate grade C land increased the incomes (economic rent) which could be earned from grade A and grade B lands, and the commercial rent which people were willing to pay for their temporary use. 

This situation can be illustrated by the following diagram:

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Let us suppose that the cost of production in cultivating three different acres of land is the equivalent of ten bushels for each acre. The yield from grade C land will just repay the cost of production. Grade B land will yield twenty bushels, which means a surplus (economic rent) of ten bushels. A new settler would be willing to pay a commercial rent equivalent to about ten bushels for this grade B land. Grade A land yields an economic rent of twenty bushels and can command a proportionately higher commercial rent. 

We can see, then, that the rent which is earned by a particular quality of land at a given time is the difference between the value of the yield of that land and the value of the yield of the least fertile land of the same type which it pays to cultivate.* 

Rent is a differential return.

This applies not only to agricultural land but also to urban, mining, and all other types of land. In all cases, the best land will be occupied first, and as the growing demand brings the less desirable land into use, the economic (and therefore the commercial) rent of the better land will be increased. In the case of urban land, New York's great shopping center, Fifth Avenue, would represent grade A land, land in downtown Brooklyn, grade B, and land on the outskirts of the borough of Queens, grade C. Fifth Avenue land commands a high rent because storekeepers know that it is a very busy street and the thousands of people who stream along the Avenue all day long are attracted to the stores they pass. In addition to this, Fifth 

Avenue has established itself as a fashionable center for high quality merchandise and for wealthy shoppers, and stores can charge more for their goods on Fifth Avenue than they could if they were located on Sixth Avenue. These advantages are the exact equivalent of the superior fertility and nearness to market in the case of farming land. 

Rent and Prices. You will recall that, in our illustration,

Mark Overton's land yielded a higher rent when the price of wheat rose.

In other words, high prices produce a high rent; * The formulation of the law of rent was first made by an English economist, David Ricardo, and is sometimes called the Ricardian Theory of Rent.

high rent does not result in high prices. Stores located on Fifth 

Avenue do not charge high prices because they pay high rents. 

On the contrary, one of the reasons the storekeepers are willing and able to pay the high rents is that on Fifth Avenue they can obtain high prices for their merchandise. 

Extensive Margin of Cultivation. We have seen that as the demand for land grows, less and less fertile land is brought under cultivation. However, land will not be used at any given time if its yield does not at least compensate its owner for the cost of cultivating it. Land, the yield of which is barely enough to cover these costs, is known as marginal land * or no-rent land. 

Recourse to less and less fertile land until this point is reached is known as cultivation up to the extensive margin. (This term is to be compared with intensive margin of cultivation, as explained on page 54.) As the demand for wheat fluctuates, a wheat farm may be on the margin one year and below the margin the next year.

In the latter case the land will not repay the cost of cultivation and it will ultimately be abandoned. 

The same is true of urban land. You may have seen stores on the outskirts of cities which were empty one year, rented the next, empty again, and then reoccupied. This fluctuation of occupancy reflects the fluctuation of the extensive margin of land utilization.

Rent and the Value of Land. If you wanted to buy some land, you would naturally be willing to pay more for land which yields a high income than for land which yields a low income or no income at all. In general, the value of land (that is, the price which people are willing to pay for it) depends on the rent which it yields. 

Suppose that a man owns a 200-acre farm which yields him a net annual income (economic rent) of $1200. If you wished to buy this farm, how much would it cost you? Let us assume that 6% is considered the proper rate of interest for an investment with the degree of risk involved in the purchase of the farm in question. This means that the $1200 net income is *Land which does not even repay the cost of cultivation is known as sub-marginal land. The Federal Government at present is making an attempt to retire sub-marginal land from cultivation.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

6% of the value of the farm. The value, or selling price, can be calculated by the following formula: 

Net income 

Interest rate 

X 100= value of farm

If we place the correct figures in the formula, we get the following results: $1200 X 100 $20,000, or value of farm

Thus the 200-acre farm has a total value of $20,000 and a value per acre of $100.

Single Tax. Suppose that a man owns land in a city and that the economic rent (and therefore the value) of this land increases as the city grows. The value of this man's property has risen through no work or accomplishment of his own. He is the recipient of what is known as an "unearned increment. 

Some years ago, an economist named Henry George suggested that the government should take over this unearned increment by levying a tax on land to the full value of the economic rent. 

Henry George believed that this would make possible the elimination of all other forms of taxation and would lead to social justice and the elimination of poverty. This doctrine is known as the Single Tax.

At one time Henry George had a considerable body of followers, but now the number of Single-Taxers is comparatively 

There are several weak points in his argument. (1)

Land-owners often increase the value of their property by hard work and by wise investment of capital. This is an earned increment. For purposes of taxation it would not be possible to distinguish clearly between the earned and the unearned increments. (2) Unearned increments occur in fields other than land. All sorts of raw materials and finished goods as well as stocks and bonds frequently rise in price through no effort of the owner. Why should the land-owner be penalized exclusively? (3) Since land is valued only because it yields a rent, confiscation of the rent would be equivalent to confiscation of the land. 

People who had invested money in land would suffer great losses.

(4) There is considerable doubt as to whether the Single Tax, even if it could be administered, would result in the great social improvements which Henry George anticipated.

Summary.-Land is a natural factor of production, the supply of which is limited. Because of this, people are willing to pay a commercial rent for the temporary use of certain land. 

The amount of the commercial rent tends to equal the economic rent of the land, which is a differential return between the value of the yield of a given piece of land and the value of the yield of the least fertile land of that type which it pays to cultivate.

CHAPTER VI  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To discuss the meaning and importance of “labor." 

2. To explain how the supply of labor is determined. 

3. To explain how the demand for labor is determined. 

4. To indicate the causes and remedies for unemployment. 

5. To explain the meaning of "wages." 

6. To explain how wages are determined.

LABOR 

Definition.-Labor is human effort used in the creation of commodities and services. Labor may be mental effort, such as bookkeeping; physical effort, such as unloading furniture from a van; or a combination of physical and mental effort, such as grinding the valves of an automobile. 

Importance of Labor.-Labor is required to produce all the goods which man uses. When Mark Overton settled on the frontier, he labored to clear away his land, plant grain, and build himself a log cabin. Today we labor to build the great machines which we use in our large-scale production and we labor to make use of these machines after they have been built. In either type of society, the most simple or the most complex, man must labor if he is to have the things he needs in order to live and enjoy life.

The standard of life of a man or of a nation depends in great part upon the efficiency of the labor of that man or of the laborers of that nation.

Efficiency of Labor.-The efficiency of labor depends in part on the ability of the individual laborer. An efficient laborer must be intelligent, healthy, and industrious. He must be well adapted, mentally and physically, to the work he is doing, or he 64

will be dissatisfied and unable to put forth his best efforts. As a general thing he must be able to get along well with other people.

Individual efficiency alone, however, does not mean that the labor supply of a nation will be highly productive. To achieve a high level of productivity, the work of many individuals must be combined and coördinated. Raw materials, tools and machinery must be provided. Adequate transportation facilities must 

ELECTRICAL ENGINEER 

WANTED DITCH 

DIGGER 

WANTED 

SPECIAL ABILITY REQUIRED AND NEVER THE TWAIN COMPETE 

Borowol noid

Is an adequate supply of both types of workers essential to our economic well-being? be available. The work must be planned so that the highest degree of coöperation among the workers is possible. All these tasks are the function of management. If the management fails in its task, the efficiency of individual laborers will not be fully utilized. 

Supply of Labor.-By the supply of labor we mean all the human power, mental and physical, which is available to do productive work. Thus, the labor supply depends on the ac-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

tual number of workers in the country, their ability to do the required work, and their willingness to work for the wages offered.

Variations in Supply.-We can see from this that there may be an oversupply of one type of labor at the same time that there is an undersupply of another type. There may be more than enough ditchdiggers and not enough doctors, or vice versa. 

It is not possible for these men to take over each other's jobs. 

Again, there may be more than enough capable men willing to do work that pays good wages and less than enough who are willing to do a type of work that pays low wages. 

Effect of Improved Efficiency. The labor supply is increased when the existing workers become more efficient or when they train themselves to do the type of work for which there is a demand. For example, if a number of blacksmiths (for whom there is practically no demand today) learned to become expert automobile mechanics, they would, in effect, increase the labor supply.

Effect of Population Increase.—In addition, the labor supply is increased when the population of the country grows, so that more workers are available. This population growth takes place when the birth rate exceeds the death rate, or when immigration (the number of people entering the country from foreign lands) is greater than emigration (the number of people leaving the country). In the United States, we have increased the supply of labor by medical accomplishments and by comparatively good sanitary conditions which lowered the death rate. In addition our high wages and superior working conditions have attracted a great stream of immigrants. 

Immigration. During most of the nineteenth century, immigrants were welcomed into this country and even urged to come, for a new and growing country needs an ever increasing supply of labor, and a frontier readily absorbs any surplus of labor which may accumulate in the regions which are already developed. But, as the country became more thickly settled and the frontier disappeared, the natural increase in the population, together with the large number of immigrants who had already come to this country, made the supply of labor more nearly equal to the demand.

The lower standard of living of the immigrants made them willing to work for much lower wages than the American workers demanded. The laboring class, therefore, has long been in favor of restricting immigration. The industrialists, on the other hand, have been glad to have this large supply of cheap labor at hand, for it tended to lower wages and, thereby, to increase their profits.

After about 1900, a change became apparent in the character of the immigration into this country. Previous to that date most of the immigrants had come from the countries of north- DOCE OC

ETED || Why was there no vigorous opposition to unrestricted immigration one hundred years ago? western Europe; from Germany, France, Great Britain, and the 

Scandinavian countries. The physical and social background of life in those countries was similar to our own, and for the most part these immigrants adapted themselves easily and thoroughly to the life in their adopted land. 

The immigration after 1900 was predominantly from the countries of eastern and south-eastern Europe, that is, Italy, Greece, Russia, and the Balkan countries. Many of the immigrants from these countries became excellent citizens, but on the whole they found it more difficult to break off their ties with

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

the Old World and to become real Americans. They tended to concentrate in restricted areas in some large city, particularly New York, where they retained many of their national customs and very often their national language. Difficulties encountered during the World War brought these unassimilated immigrants to the attention of the American public and led us to adopt a policy which American labor had advocated for many years, that is, severe restriction of immigration. 

The Malthusian Theory. In spite of immigration and our low mortality rate, the United States has never experienced any serious or widespread overpopulation problem. In other countries, however, the population has grown so dense that at times. it threatened to become more than the productive powers of the nation could support. This was the case, for example, in 

England, during the latter part of the eighteenth century. An 

English economist of that period, named Thomas Malthus, studied the population problem and formulated what has come to be known as the Malthusian Theory. This theory states that the population has a tendency to increase more rapidly than the food supply. Unless population growth is checked by such factors as war, flood, pestilence and birth control, it will become greater than the nation can support and famine will result. 

Malthus concluded from this that it was useless to try to raise the standard of living of the common people, because the population in a highly developed country tended to "press on the means of subsistence," that is, tended to grow to the maximum figure that the nation could support. 

Criticism of the Malthusian Theory.-The Malthusian 

Theory is based on the assumption that man's productive powers do not increase to any great extent. If this were the case, there is no doubt that unrestricted population growth would cause the conditions which Malthus claimed were inevitable. This is very nearly what has happened in China today. However, in 

England and America the population has increased enormously since Malthus' day and the standard of living has also risen. 

This is because our ability to produce the necessities of life has increased even faster than the population. Land which is cultivated with modern scientific methods will yield crops many times greater than the best crops of the eighteenth century.

DEMAND FOR LABOR 

Definition.-Demand for labor is the desire to use the mental and physical efforts of other people, plus the ability and the willingness to pay satisfactory wages for those efforts. 

Changes in Demand. In this country, certain types of labor have diminished in importance at the same time that other types were increasing in importance. For example, the white-collar worker, by which we mean clerks, salesmen, bookkeepers, etc., has increased greatly in numbers and importance in the last forty years. During the same period, the farmer, certain types of craftsmen (such as cabinet makers, shoemakers, and tailors), and the machinist have declined in relative numbers and importance.

The development of the modern corporation (page 108) has created a demand for employees of managerial and executive ability who can be trusted to do the responsible work involved in administering a large business enterprise. As the large corporation grew in importance, many men who in previous years would have established their own businesses, became managerial employees of a corporation.

Thus we see that the demand for one type of labor may increase while the demand for another type decreases or remains stationary. However, it is possible for the total demand for labor to decrease. A sharp drop in the total demand for labor, or a relatively low demand for any given type or types of labor, creates a problem of unemployment.

Meaning of Unemployment.-Unemployment is a condition which exists when men and women who wish to perform productive work for wages are not able to obtain such work. 

Importance of Unemployment. The unemployed worker is deprived of his income. This means that he must suffer poverty and in some cases extreme want. Moreover, many workers suffer keenly from the inactivity which unemployment forces on them and from the humiliation of having to accept charity. 

However, not only the individual but the nation as a whole suffers as a result of unemployment.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

1. Labor creates wealth. If workers are idle, the total wealth and power of the nation are less than it would have been if they had been engaged in productive work. 

2. The unemployed laborer and his family must be supported. This creates a drain on private or public funds. 

3. The income of the unemployed laborer is cut off. Therefore, he cannot buy the products of others. This loss of purchasing power depresses business and causes more unemployment.

Causes of Unemployment.-What are the causes of this extremely serious situation? 

1. Use of Machinery.—The introduction of machinery into a particular field decreases the immediate demand for labor in that field. (This is known as technological unemployment.) 

However, the use of machinery makes possible new industries which, in the long run, compensate for this type of unemployment. 

2. Immobility of Labor.-This means that it is not easy for the working man to move from one part of the country to another. Such influences as family ties, ownership of property, lack of transportation expenses, and general disinclination to move, tend to hold him in the place in which he lives. Immobility of labor means also that the worker finds it difficult to change from one industry to another because his training is specialized. When a business or an entire industry moves to a different part of the country, or when one industry displaces another (as the automobile industry displaced the carriage industry), the immobility of labor creates unemployment. 

3. Seasonal Unemployment.-Seasonal unemployment is caused by the concentration of the activity of an industry within a certain period of the year. It is obvious that in certain seasons of the year there is a greater demand for labor in ice plants or canning factories than in other seasons. In the same way the demand for the labor of longshoremen is concentrated within the few hours of the day when ships are to be unloaded. Thus, in these fields there is unemployment during the slack periods. 

4. Personal Causes.-People are sometimes unemployed because they are not capable of holding a job or because they are not willing to work for the wages offered.

5. Cyclical Unemployment.-All the causes mentioned above produce unemployment in periods of prosperity as well as in periods of depression. When depression does come, however, many enterprises go out of business, others greatly curtail their activities, and masses of capable workers become unemployed. 

This is known as cyclical unemployment and is by far the most important cause of unemployment. 

Remedies for Unemployment 

1. Efforts of Private Enterprises.-Some American business houses have attempted to eliminate the cyclical nature of their demand for labor. For example, the Dennison Company constructs buildings and makes improvements in its plant during periods when business is slack. In this way it is able to keep its men employed and to prepare for more prosperous times when there will be a greater demand for their product. The 

Procter and Gamble Company has a similar plan whereby it guarantees its employees at least forty-eight weeks of full-time work each year. 

2. Public Works.-This plan proposes that the Government undertake great public works (construction projects, such as dams, roads, etc.) during periods of depression. In this way it will be possible to give useful work to persons who lose their jobs in private industry. The Roosevelt Administration has used public works extensively in its efforts to decrease unemployment. 

3. Shortening the Work-Week.-It is argued by some that unemployment can be greatly relieved if the number of hours in the work-week is decreased. The NRA attempted to lessen unemployment in this way. This policy has the support of organized labor. 

4. United States Employment Service.-This is an agency, created by the Roosevelt Administration, which aims to give unemployed workers accurate information about employment prospects throughout the country. In this way it is hoped that men will be guided to jobs which they are able to fill, and some of the immobility of labor will be overcome. In addition, men will learn what industries offer the best prospects of employment and they will be able to make efforts to train themselves ac-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

cordingly. A number of states have established similar agencies.

' 

5. The Social Security Act.-The Social Security Act, which is now the law of the land, contains provisions intended to meet some of the phases of our unemployment problem. 

The first part of the Act provides Federal funds to aid those who are not capable of working. Some of those whom the Government places in this class are the aged, the blind, crippled children, and poor mothers. The Federal Government offers to contribute as much money to the support of these unfortunates as any state does, up to $15 a month. For example, if a state provides these people with $15 a month, the Federal Government will contribute the same amount, so that the individual concerned receives $30 a month. (In 1939, the Federal Government raised its maximum contribution to $20 a month.) 

The second part of the Act, which is far more important, aims to set up a great system of unemployment insurance and old-age insurance for the masses of American workers. The unemployment insurance is financed by a payroll tax which is paid by employers. When the plan is fully under way, the tax will amount to 3% of the total payroll. This money will be used to set up a fund from which workers will draw money when they are unemployed. The details of the plan will depend on the various states.

The old-age insurance is financed by a tax on both employer and employees. When this plan is fully under way, the em ployer will pay 3% of his annual payroll, and the worker 3% of his wages. Workers will retire at the age of sixty-five years and they will receive pensions ranging from $10 to $85 a month, depending on the contributions they have made to the fund. 

It is believed that this Act will have the following effects: (1) It will eliminate some unemployment directly, for it will retire aged people from their jobs and make room for younger workers. (2) It will greatly lessen the sufferings which unemployment causes. (3) By sustaining the purchasing power of our people, it will tend to stabilize business conditions so that * Private employment agencies exist in many cities. These agencies serve as intermediaries between employers and employees, but they are not able to perform the services which we have mentioned above. As a general thing, their activities are confined to the local labor market and they try to provide immediate employment, for which they collect a fee.

cyclical unemployment will be avoided. (This matter will be discussed more fully in Chapter XXIV.)

WAGES 

Definition.-Wages are the price paid for the contribution made by labor to production. 

Forms of Wages.-Wages are paid in a number of different ways. The most common type is the time wage. This is a payment made for labor performed over a certain period of time, such as an hour, a day, a week, or a year. Another important type is the piece wage, which is based on the amount of work. 

For example, pressers in clothing factories are usually paid a fixed amount for pressing each garment.

All other forms of wages are combinations or modifications of the time wage and piece wage. A commission, which is a percentage of the price of a commodity which a salesman sells, is a type of piece wage. The fee of a doctor or a lawyer is paid for a specific service, so that it is really a form of piece wage. 

The salaries which are paid to business executives and to officers of corporations, the so-called "wages of management," are time wages. Bonuses may be considered a combination of time and piece wages, for they are usually based on the productivity of the worker and on his length of service. 

Real Wages and Money Wages.-Almost all wages in the modern world are paid in money. As prices of goods rise and fall, the amount of goods which a given number of dollars can purchase varies accordingly. Therefore, we must distinguish between money wages, the amount of money which the worker receives, and real wages, the amount of goods he can buy with his money. If the general price level rises and the worker's money wages remain the same, his real wages have fallen. If the price level falls, his real wages have risen, for he can buy more with the same number of dollars.

Theories of Wages.-What determines the amount of money which the average worker receives as his wages? A number of theories, or attempts at explanation, have been advanced. 

1. The Subsistence Theory. This theory was developed in accordance with the Malthusian doctrine, which states, as you

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

recall, that the population tends to press on the means of subsistence. Therefore, wages tend to remain at a level which is barely sufficient to support a worker and his family. If more than the subsistence wage is paid, the workers will raise larger families. This will increase the labor supply and tend to lower wages until the subsistence level is reached again. 

The weakness of this theory is that it assumes that the productivity of labor and the total wealth of the nation will not increase greatly. Such an increase in productivity has actually taken place, and wages in advanced countries have advanced above the subsistence level. 

2. The Wages Fund Theory. This theory holds that a cer tain definite portion of the total capital funds of a country is devoted to the payment of wages. This so-called "wages fund'' is divided among all wage earners. Therefore, if the wages of one worker are raised, another worker somewhere else will have to suffer a corresponding wage cut. The weakness of this theory is that it ignores the fact that labor is productive; it creates new wealth. Wages are really paid not out of capital funds but out of this created wealth and the wage level depends, in part at least, on the ability of labor to produce wealth. 

The wages fund theory is now generally discredited, but it was widely accepted in England during the first part of the nineteenth century and it was used to justify the extremely low wages which were paid at that time. 

3. The Marginal Productivity Theory.-The law of dimin ishing returns tells us that if a factory owner continues to add workers to his factory, he will eventually arrive at a worker who adds just enough to the total value of the product to cover the cost of his wages. The employer will not hire any workers beyond this point because, if he did, it would result in a loss of money. This last hired worker is called the marginal worker. 

The marginal productivity theory states that the wages which the factory owner can afford to pay the marginal worker establish the wage level for every man in the factory engaged in the same sort of work. For example, if a factory employs fifty men, and the employer knows that the last man he has hired, the marginal worker, produces just enough to pay for his wages, he will offer all his employees the wage of the marginal

worker. This is so because any individual worker, in relation to all the other men in the shop, is in the position of the fiftieth or marginal worker.

Although this is the best of the three theories we have examined, it does not give us a complete account of how wages are determined under modern conditions. 

How Wages Are Actually Determined. The wage of a worker falls between two fairly definite limits. The upper level, or the point past which the wage cannot rise, is determined by the productivity of the worker. This means simply that the employer cannot afford to pay a man more than the value of what he produces. The lower level, or the point beneath which the wage cannot fall, is determined by the worker's standard of living. The standard of living, in turn, is determined by the amount of goods necessary for the worker's bare subsistence, and by the comforts to which he is accustomed and which he insists upon having. In times of dire distress a worker may accept a wage which will not enable him to maintain his standard of living, but as a general thing he will not work unless he receives this minimum.

These upper and lower limits may be represented in the following way:

UPPER LEVEL. 

PRODUCTIVITY OF WORKER BARGAINING POWER OF EMPLOYER 

AND

EMPLOYEE LOWER LEVEL WORKER'S STANDARD OF LIVING 

The point on the diagonal line where wages will settle is determined by the bargaining ability of the worker as compared with the bargaining ability of the employer. 

If many men are out of work and there is a great number of men clamoring for jobs, the bargaining ability of the worker

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

is weakened and wages tend to fall. If times are good and there is a great demand for labor, wages tend to rise. Thus we see that an essential factor in determining wages is the number of men competing for a given job or type of job. Some individuals have unusual personal talents, so that there is little if any competition for their jobs. These individuals have great bargaining power and command high wages. Examples are movie stars and famous surgeons. 

MINIMUM STANDARD OF LIVING CAPITAL WAGES LABOR PRODUCTIVITY OF LABOR HOW WAGES ARE DETERMINED 

What effect does collective bargaining have on this tug-of-war? 

Other factors which give the laborer an advantage in his bargaining with the employer are labor unions and minimum wage laws and other labor legislation. 

A factor which aids the employer is the fact that he gener ally exerts a far greater influence upon legislation than does the worker. Another important influence is the fact that labor is perishable. If a laborer misses a day's work, that day's work is lost forever. Therefore, laborers sometimes feel that they should accept whatever they can get for a day's work rather than go idle. This tends to lower wages. (For further discus sion of this topic, see Chapter XX.)

Influence of Custom.-Custom sometimes plays a fairly important part in determining wages. As a result of long and widespread usage, a particular wage comes to be accepted as the "proper" or "normal" payment for a given type of work. 

For example, in many parts of the country today, $15 a week is a customary wage for a young person starting work in a business office. This figure may or may not correspond to the value of the employee's services but it is generally considered a fair wage and is often accepted by employer and employee without a great deal of bargaining. 

Law of Single Price and Wages.-The Law of Single Price 

(page 137) applies to wages as it applies to all other prices. 

Employees performing the same type of work in the same establishment feel that they should, and generally do, receive approximately the same wage. In some cases in which employers grant wage increases to favored individuals or groups, they try to conceal this information from other employees doing the same type of work. The employer knows that these workers would demand a wage increase if they were aware of the disparity.

Overpayment. This analysis of how wages are determined is true in general. We must not suppose, however, that no one is ever overpaid, that is, paid more than the value of what he produces. An employee may be overpaid because his employer over-estimates the value of what he produces or accomplishes. 

In this case, it is probable that the employer will eventually correct his false estimate and adjust the wage accordingly. 

An employee may be overpaid because he, or someone interested in his welfare, has the power to set the wage at an excessive figure. For example, employees of governmental units sometimes receive wages out of all proportion to the value of their services because they possess political influence. In some cases, officials of large corporations are paid enormous salaries because they control the policies and expenditures of the organization by which they are employed. 

All these cases, however, are comparatively rare. 

As we shall see in Chapter XX, excessively low wages are a far greater social problem than excessively high wages.

CHAPTER VII  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning of "capital." 

2. To show how capital originates. 

3. To explain the functions of capital. 

4. To discuss the nature of a good investment. 

5. To explain the meaning of "interest." 

6. To show why interest is paid. 

7. To explain how the interest rate is determined. 

Definition.-Capital is the produced instruments of further production. It is all those things which man himself has made to help in the production of goods and services. Thus we say that capital is a derived factor of production. Land and labor, on the other hand, are primary factors of production because they have their origin in natural sources. 

FORMS OF CAPITAL 

Three Aspects of Capital.-If you owned a filling station, which is a "produced instrument of further production," you might regard your capital from any one of three different aspects. You might consider it (1) the money which you saved and borrowed in order to build and equip the station; (2) the producers' goods themselves, that is, the station with its pumps, tools, etc.; or (3) the price for which you would be willing to sell your station. These three aspects of capital are called capital funds, capital goods, and capital values, respectively. 

Capital Funds.-Capital funds are the money or purchasing power which is not used for the immediate satisfaction of human wants but which is reserved for use in further production. If a man has earned $5000 in the course of a year and has spent $4000 on living expenses he will have accumulated a surplus of 80

$1000. If the man deposits this money in a bank, reinvests it in his own business, or invests it in some other business, he has built up a capital fund of $1000. In the same way, all persons who save a part of their incomes and place the money in a bank or invest it in a productive enterprise are building up capital funds. 

BUSINESS MAN FARMER SAVINGS WORKER SAVINGS, THE SOURCE OF CAPITAL FUNDS NATIONAL CAPITAL FUNDS 

For what purposes will these capital funds be used?

Some savings may take place automatically. For example, stockholders in corporations generally have some of their income saved whether they desire it or not, because the officers of the corporation turn back some of the profits into the business, rather than distribute them among the stockholders. 

Extent of National Savings.-The amount of money saved in the country is extremely important because, as we have seen,. this determines the amount of capital funds which will be available for the maintenance and expansion of the productive powers of the country. What part of their total income do the people of the country save? The table on page 82 gives this information.

It is estimated that between the years 1909 and 1918, 27 million workers (employees) saved about one-fifth of the total national savings; 8,400,000 farmers saved about one-eighth of the total national savings; and 3,300,000 business men saved about two-thirds of the total national savings. The great bulk of capital funds in the United States is created by business men and property owners.

Capital Goods.-After capital funds have been accumulated, they are used to purchase capital goods,* or commodities already produced which are to be used in further production. In the case of a manufacturer, these goods include raw materials, buildings, fuel, and transportation facilities. In the case of a * There is one case in which capital funds are wanted for themselves, rather than to purchase capital goods. Certain funds on deposit in New 

York banks are known as call money and are used for speculation on the New York Stock Exchange. This will be further considered in Chapter 

---

farmer, they include barns, silos, drainage facilities, and live stock. Retailers use capital funds to outfit their stores and to buy the goods which they will sell. Professional men, such as doctors, lawyers, and teachers, use capital funds to obtain the training necessary for their work. They spend money to buy a service (instruction) which will enable them to "produce" many other services (legal and medical advice) in the future. 

Classification of Capital Goods.—A number of classifications of capital goods have been made. One is on the basis of usefulness. Free capital includes those goods which can be used for the production of any of several things. For example, coal can be used in the production of many things, including railroad transportation, steel and electricity. Specialized capital can be used for one productive purpose only. A typewriter, for example, can be used only to type letters.

Another basis for classification is that of durability. Circulating capital includes goods which can be used only once in the same productive operation. The raw material which the manufacturer puts into his finished product and the gasoline which propels the motor truck are examples of circulating capital. Fixed capital, on the other hand, may be used a number of times in the same productive process. Motor trucks, for example, can travel many thousands of miles before being worn out.

Depreciation Costs.-Fixed capital goods, which are used many times, have a tendency to wear out. Engineers have estimated that the average life of machinery in American factories is about ten years. Therefore, business men find it necessary to set aside a part of their income each year into funds which are known as "reserves for depreciation." As the machine wears out, the reserve grows, and if the apportionment is correct the machine can be replaced or repaired, when it finally becomes necessary, without reducing the profits. 

All concerns which use considerable amounts of capital goods must make provisions for depreciation and it is often a very heavy expense.* Depreciation costs are one of the fixed charges we mentioned in 

Chapter IV.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Capital Values.-The third point of view from which capital may be considered is that of values. Capital values refer to the value of capital goods expressed in terms of money. Such values are calculated by what is known as the capitalization process. 

If a man owns a profitable business, it is valuable to him, and if you wanted to buy the business you would have to pay his price to induce him to part with it. When the owner places a price upon such a business he has assigned to it a capital value. 

Consider the case of the manufacturer whose business earns an annual profit of $5,000. What amount of money would he accept for this business? If he cared to sell at all, this is the way he would calculate its value:

His books show that the business is earning $5,000 a year for him. What amount of money would be needed to bring him $5,000 if loaned or invested at the current rate of interest? 

If investigation shows that the rate of interest at which loans are being made at that time is 5%, then he will need the amount of money of which $5,000 is 5%. In other words, $5,000 is 5% of the total value of the business. If then 5% is 1% and 100% $ 5,000 would be $ 1,000 would be or the money value of his business. $100,000

This $100,000 represents the capitalized or capital value of the business. This is not a fictitious value, for the worth of a productive good is based upon two things: first, the earning capacity of the good; second, the current interest rate for that type of investment. The capital which the successful business man has at his command is not the income of his business but is the capitalized value of that income. 

Function of Capital.-The function of capital, the reason business men are willing to spend so much money for it, is that it greatly increases our power to produce goods. In Mark 

Overton's early days on the frontier, he used very little capital. 

As a result, his productive powers were comparatively low. 

Since those days the United States has become the richest nation in the world. This development has been due in great part to

an accumulation of capital goods which have given us tremendous productive powers.

Because capital is so productive, business men have found it profitable to bring together masses of capital into single enterprises, that is, to operate on a large scale. As we saw in Chapter 

IV, large-scale, capitalist production makes possible tremendous savings, which more than outweigh the expense of producing the capital goods.

Diminishing Productivity of Capital.-Increased use of capital, on the whole, has led to greater profits, but the law of diminishing returns applies to this factor of production as it does to all others. As the use of capital for production is increased, a point is eventually reached at which the output or production does not increase in proportion to the increased use of capital. The addition of more floors to a factory building, more fertilizer to a field, more cars to run over the same right-ofway of a railroad, would rapidly become less profitable after a certain point had been reached. In the same manner, there is

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

a definite point beyond which it would not pay to invest capital in more efficient tools and machinery for a given number of workmen.

This law is effective even though the use of land and labor is also increased when more capital is added. This provides one explanation of the fact that small-scale producers are sometimes more efficient than large-scale producers.
