Does every business man have to face this problem?

Investment. We have seen that the productive capacity of the nation is determined, to a great extent, by the nature of the capital goods it uses. The nature of these capital goods is determined, in turn, by the way people invest their savings, for it is from such savings that business houses obtain the funds with which they buy capital goods. This indicates the tremendous importance of investment, not only to the individual investor, but also to the nation as a whole. 

What are the tests which the investor ought to apply when he considers investing his savings in some business enterprise? (1) The earnings of the company should be studied. (2) The

success of the company in passing through periods of depression should be examined. (3) The efficiency and honesty of the management should be investigated. (4) The nature of the product is also important. Is the company producing something which really satisfies a human want, so that the demand for it is likely to remain steady or to increase in the future? (5) The credit condition of the company should be investigated. 

Can it secure the necessary capital funds at reasonable rates? 

The average investor is not an expert financier and it is not possible for him to make elaborate investigations into all of these points. Nevertheless, an intelligent person who intends to invest funds can usually get reliable information on such matters, and his own prosperity, as well as the prosperity of the nation, requires that he do so.

INTEREST 

Definition.—Interest is the price paid for the use of capital funds.

Reason for the Payment of Interest.-For hundreds of years economists have considered the problem of why interest is paid. Many theories have been advanced. 

1. The Abstinence Theory.-Some economists have maintained that interest is a reward for abstinence. People who loan out capital funds give up the privilege of spending the funds immediately. They abstain from enjoying the use of their money and they must be rewarded for this sacrifice. 

2. The Reward for Risk.—Interest has been explained by another group as a reward for risk. Anyone who invests capital funds assumes the risk of losing all or part of his money. 

The interest payments compensate him for undergoing this danger. 

3. The Time Preference Theory.-A theory of time prefer ence was developed by a group of Austrian economists. This theory states that anything is more desirable or more valuable in the present than it is in the future. The little boy wants his candy today, not tomorrow; commuters run to catch the 5:15 in spite of the fact that there is another train at 5:27; you want to go the movies tonight, not next week. It is the same with money or capital. Would you rather have ten dollars in

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

your pocket now, or ten dollars a year from now? Something extra must be added to the ten dollars before you will choose to wait for it. Similarly, the investor who chooses to wait for his money must be compensated by the payment of interest, and the borrower who gets the immediate use of the capital funds must pay for the privilege. 

4. The Productivity Theory.-Another group of economists have advanced the theory that interest is paid because capital can be used to produce new wealth. Those who support this productivity theory point to the fact that capital funds can be converted into capital goods and that capital goods greatly increase the productivity and the profit-making powers of a business enterprise.

Truth of Theories.—There is an element of truth in all of these theories. In the cases of those loans which are made for business purposes (and almost all loans are of this type) it may be said, in general, that the risk theory, the abstinence theory, and the time preference theory explain why the investor will not supply capital unless interest is paid. He is not willing to risk his money and to give up the privilege of its immediate use, unless he receives some compensation for doing so. In other words, these theories show us why interest is necessary. 

The productivity theory, on the other hand, explains how interest is possible. The entrepreneur is able and willing to pay interest because the capital he borrows is a source of greater profit to him.

The Interest Rate. The interest rate, or the price of capital, is expressed in a different manner from most prices. It is not quoted in dollars and cents but is expressed as a percentage of the amount which is involved in the loan. It is true that interest payments are made in money, but if a prospective borrower approaches a banker on the matter of a loan of $5,000 and asks about the terms, or the cost of the loan, the banker will answer in this manner, "We will let you have this loan at 

5%." He will not say, "It will cost you $250.”

How Interest Rates Are Determined. The interest rate is the price paid for capital and this price, like all others, is set by the forces of supply and demand.

1. Demand for Capital.-The demand for capital varies in intensity with different individuals. Some individuals who want to borrow capital cannot afford to pay high interest rates; they are said to have a low time preference. Business men who cannot make high profits fall in this class. They cannot afford to pay high interest rates because the use of capital in their business will not enable them to earn high profits. Those who can afford to pay high interest rates are said to have a high time preference. Speculators and extremely successful business men, who can use capital to earn high profits, fall in this

But $10,000,000 undoubtedly would be demanded 2. Supply of Capital.-The supply of capital is subject to the same forces. The people who will not loan money except at high interest rates are said to have a high time preference. For example, successful business men will not loan out their money unless a high interest rate is paid, because these men can use their funds profitably in their own businesses. People with a low time preference (people who are willing to loan money at low interest rates) include such individuals as clergymen, teach-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

ers, and widows, who are not in a position to use their capital funds in production. Therefore, they are willing to let some trustworthy person use it, and will accept a lower price for it. 

In general, a price is set at the point where the amount of goods offered is equal to the amount which will be taken off the market. It is the same with interest. The interest rate settles at the point where the amount of capital offered by lenders is equal to the amount wanted by the borrowers. Examine the table on page 89 and you will note two things. First, as the interest rate falls, the amount of capital demanded increases. 

Second, as the interest rate rises, the amount which will be supplied increases but the amount which will be taken de- This means that lenders want a high price or rate and that borrowers want to pay only a low price or rate. Under the conditions set forth in our table the rate would be 5%, for that is the point at which the amount of capital offered by the lenders is equal to the amount wanted by the demanders. creases.

Other Factors Which Affect the Interest Rate.-The ex planation above shows how the interest rate is set by the forces of demand for and supply of capital. There are, in addition, some other factors which help to determine the interest rate. 

1. The expense factor adds slightly to the interest rate. 

Clerks' salaries, the cost of investigation of the risk, the cost of drawing up the agreement, and other expenses, such as printing and postage, make up this factor. 

2. The risk factor is important in determining the interest rate. The United States Government can borrow money at a low rate (about 22 to 3% today). There is practically no risk in loaning money to the government because it has the power of taxation, or the legal right to secure money from its citizens for its support, and this guarantees the payment of its obligations. Successful corporations, such as the American Telephone & Telegraph Company, can borrow money today at a rate somewhere between 4% and 5%. The risk of loss is greater to the lender than in the case of a government loan, for the corporation has no taxing power but must depend upon its earnings to repay the money. Individual business men are required to pay about 52% or 62% for money. Their earnings depend upon their own individual efforts and local business conditions,

and are thus subject to more uncertainty than are the earnings of great corporations. There are some unfortunate people who are forced to borrow money to meet living expenses and, since there is considerable doubt as to their ability to repay the loans, the risk of loss is great. These people have recourse to "loan sharks" and are sometimes charged as much as 36%. During the Japanese bombardment and attack on Shanghai in 1932, banks were reported to be charging 300% for loans. The risk in this case was the possibility of the destruction of property or the violent death of the borrower. 

3. The element of time affects the interest rate because it may act as one of the determinants of the supply of capital. It has been observed in the field of investment that there is a variation in the interest rates on long- and short-term obligations. 

During periods when interest rates are high, business houses who are borrowing funds by issue of long-term bonds will not be forced to pay such high interest rates as the borrowers of funds which are secured by short-term bonds. This is due to the fact that investors compete with each other to secure the long-term bonds in order that the favorable rate of interest may be extended as far into the future as possible. In other words, the supply of capital for long-term investment will be large, and the price or interest rate will fall as a result of the large supply. 

If current interest rates are low, borrowers who are issuing long-term bonds will have to pay relatively higher interest rates than borrowers who are issuing short-term bonds. This is a result of the reluctance of investors to tie up their funds for long periods of time at unfavorable interest rates. In other words, there will be a scarcity of capital for long-term investments, and long-term borrowers will have to pay a relatively higher rate of interest.

Usury. The charging of excessively high interest rates, which is known as usury, has always been unpopular and efforts have been made by some of our states to establish a maximum legal interest rate. The legal maximum in New York State, for example, is 6%, and any interest charge in excess of 6% is considered usurious.

However, all such laws are ineffective, for, as we have seen, interest rates are determined by the demand for and supply

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

of capital, and are affected by the factors of risk, time and expense. If the demand for capital should drive the rate higher than the legal maximum, both borrower and lender would conspire to evade the law. The borrower wants the loan, and the lender wants to make it. Therefore, to the interest rate of 6% they will agree to add such items as an "investigation charge,' a "service charge," a "commission," etc. These charges, which are mere subterfuges, will have the effect of raising the total interest paid far above the legal limit. The point is that when capital is really needed, the borrower will pay what the lender asks, and usury laws, as they are constituted at present, cannot prevent it.

Effects of Interest Rates on Values.—We have shown how the earnings or net income of a farm determine its selling price. 

You will remember that there was another factor which helped in determining the value of the farm. It was the interest rate of 6% which the farmer had to pay. By the capitalization formula, the value of that farm, on a basis of 6% interest, was calculated in the following manner:

Net Income 

Interest rate $1,200 

X 100 the value of the farm 

X 100 $20,000

Now, notice what happens to the value of the farm if the interest rate falls to 4%. The formula now gives this result: $1,200 

X 100 = $30,000

This means that a lowering of the interest rate actually in creases the value of property which yields a fixed income, and an increase in the rate has the opposite effect. 

In like manner, two businesses may exist at the same time, each of which yields an income of $1200 a year. One of these businesses may be a fairly safe one, in which case it will be able to borrow money at 4%. Thus, its capitalized value will be $30,000. The other business may be more speculative in which case lenders, whether banks or individuals, would demand a return of, let us say, 7%. Applying the formula, we can see

that the capitalized value of this business would be about $17,143. The common sense behind this is that if a safe business and a risky business produce the same annual income, the safe business has a greater value.

CHAPTER VIII  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To describe the function of the entrepreneur. 

2. To explain the meaning of "profits." 

3. To discuss the reasons for the payment of profits. 

4. To examine the rate of profits.

The Entrepreneur.-We have previously stated that goods are created when the various factors of production are properly combined. The person who effects this combination and sets up a going business enterprise is known as the entrepreneur, or the business man. The entrepreneur brings together and organizes land, labor, and capital in such a way that goods can be created. 

Profits. The purpose for which the entrepreneur performs these activities is to make a profit. Profits are that share of the total income of a business which is left over to the entrepreneur after he has paid his total costs of production. We say, therefore, that profits are a residual share. All the other factors of production must be rewarded before the business man can collect his return.

In many cases, the entrepreneur himself supplies the capital or labor, or owns the land on which his enterprise is located. 

The income which such a man receives from his business will include rent, or interest, or wages, as well as profits. He must deduct these items before he can determine what he has earned as profits, that is, as a reward for organizing and directing production. 

Efficiency of the Entrepreneur.-Since profits are a reward for organizing and managing a business, they depend largely on the efficiency of the entrepreneur who performs these functions. 

What are some of the factors which determine the efficiency of the entrepreneur? 96

1. Nature of Product.-The efficient entrepreneur will choose to produce a product which he knows people want and for which they will be willing to spend money. Many entrepreneurs suffer losses because they form businesses to produce goods for which there is only a temporary demand or none at all. PROFITS- 

A"LEFT-OVER'SHARE 

LABOR LANDLORD FRENT WAGES INVESTOR INTEREST PROFITS ENTREPRENEUR 

Is it possible for an entrepreneur to pay rent, interest, and wages to himself? 

2. Principle of Proportionality. This principle states that in an efficiently conducted enterprise, land, labor, and capital will be combined in their proper proportions. If too much land is combined with too little capital and labor, the business may be profitable but not so profitable as it would be if the several factors had been combined in their correct proportions. This is just as true if the amounts of labor or capital are disproportionately large. In any of these cases the maximum efficiency is not being attained. Business men must study their enterprises carefully and must experiment to discover the correct proportions.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

3. Size of the Enterprise.-The business man must discover to what extent he can increase the total size of his enterprise before the rate of profit begins to drop. An economist has discovered that in several lines of business the rate of profit begins to fall at the following points.

The point of diminishing returns varies widely among different industries and at different times. If the business man is to make the most effective possible use of his resources, he may find it necessary to determine at what figure his enterprise will reach the point of diminishing returns. 

4. General Administrative Ability.—The business man must have general administrative ability in order to meet successfully

the thousand and one problems that confront him. In the field of labor, he must know how to hire the right man for the right job and how to get fair amount of work for the wages that he pays. He must be able to judge general business conditions, so that he can decide whether it is better to expand or contract production. He must have skill at marketing his product and in anticipating any change in the tastes of the consumer. As a gen-

Justification of Profits.-The business man who has the industry and the intelligence to produce efficiently, as indicated above, is a benefactor of society. He can produce desirable merchandise of good quality and sell it at comparatively low prices. 

This is his service to society and this is the reason he deserves the reward which society gives him in the form of profits.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Other Reasons for Profits.-Profits have been earned as a result of factors other than efficiency in organization and management. 

1. Luck or Chance.-Profits may result from luck or chance.

For example, entrepreneurs have earned tremendous profits because the price for their product rose sharply after they en- 

THE EFFICIENT ENTREPRENEUR BENEFITS THE COMMUNITY ENTREPRENEUR LOW PRICED HIGH QUALITY GOODS 

How does the community reward the efficient entrepreneur? tered business. It is only fair to note, however, that entrepreneurs have often lost money because of falling prices which were beyond their control. In any case, the entrepreneur is assuming a risk and this is one of the reasons why he is entitled to a profit. 

2. Monopoly Conditions.-Profits have been earned as a result of monopoly. For example, eastern Pennsylvania is the only source of anthracite coal on the North American continent.

The men who own these mines have been able to pile up high profits, not so much because of their efficiency as because they

own the only source of supply and the public must use their product.

Profits often result from the ownership of a legal monopoly such as patents and copyrights. For example, a patent may give a manufacturer the exclusive right to produce a certain commodity, or it may enable him to produce more cheaply and efficiently than his competitors. The copyright to a "best seller" will enable a publisher to earn substantial profits. In these cases, however, it is possible that the entrepreneur has shown good business judgment or has assumed a risk in acquiring the patents or copyrights in question. 

3. Anti-Social Activities.-Profits have resulted, in some cases, from anti-social activities. Entrepreneurs have paid very low wages or have charged unreasonably high prices. In some cases they have used the natural resources of the country in a manner that brought profits to them but was extremely wasteful and destructive. They have bribed public officials to obtain government contracts and other advantages. They have marketed products of low quality and have sold them by making fraudulent claims in their advertising and on their labels. (Many of these abuses originated or became widespread with the development of the corporate form of business enterprise and largescale production. They will be considered more fully in following chapters.)

All these cases are the exceptional sources of profits, but they are sufficiently numerous to constitute a serious social problem. 

The government has made and is making determined efforts to eliminate the entrepreneur who earns profits from anti-social activities, but a great deal remains to be done before this is accomplished.

Profits, a Differential Return.-Prices tend to equal the cost of production of the marginal or the least efficient producer (page 139). Therefore, this entrepreneur does not earn profits.

The more efficient or fortunate entrepreneurs sell their product at the same price as does the marginal producer (law of single price). Therefore, they earn profits in proportion to the economies which they achieve in the cost of production. The chart on page 102 will make this clear.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

In other words, profits, like rent, are a differential return. 

They are measured by the difference between the unit cost of production of a given entrepreneur and the unit cost of production of the least efficient entrepreneur in that field.

The Non-Profit Entrepreneur.-There is a comparatively large group of business men who earn a living from their enterprises but who do not make a profit. These are usually small entrepreneurs who have land and capital invested in their businesses and, in addition, contribute personal labor. Small farmers and storekeepers are examples of this type of entrepreneur.

The income which these business men draw from their businesses may include elements of rent, wages, and interest, as well as profit. They rarely keep a complete set of books so that they themselves do not know all the facts about their financial situation. They continue in business because it enables them to earn a living, but it is doubtful, in many cases, whether they are earning any profits, that is, whether they are receiving any reward for organizing and directing a business enterprise. 

Amount and Rate of Profits. In 1928, American business men earned more than eight billion dollars in profits. 

The National City Bank* has estimated the rate of profits in certain businesses, as follows: (These figures are based upon the net value of all the enterprises in the industry in 1930.) * National City Bank of New York, Monthly Letter (April, 1931), p. 52

Department stores 14.0% 1.3% 12.3% 11.5% 4.8% 7.1% 9.7%

Shoes Telephone It is clear from this table that there is no fixed rate of profit. 

Profits vary widely from enterprise to enterprise, and from industry to industry. Moreover, the same enterprise or industry may earn different profits at different times. Profits of more than 500% have been realized in exceptional cases, and in other cases businesses have lost tremendous sums. 

The Equalization of Profits.-Although profits vary widely among different industries, there is one important force which tends to equalize them. If an industry yields an abnormally high income, other entrepreneurs will be attracted into that field. 

Competition will become more intense, prices will fall, and profits will tend to come down to a lower level. If an industry earns unusually low profits, some entrepreneurs will leave the field. 

This will result in less production and less competition. Prices and profits will tend to rise to a higher level. 

However, both these processes require many years to exert their effects and at any given time some industries earn a higher average rate of profits than other industries. 

Moreover, there are some industries in which competition does not and, for various reasons, cannot exist. In these cases, which are known as monopolies, there can be no automatic adjustment of the rate of profit by the entry of new firms into the field. This has created a serious social problem which will be more fully considered in Chapter X. 

Summary.-Entrepreneurs, in their desire to earn profits, organize and manage business enterprises which they attempt to administer with efficiency. Such efficiency enables them to sell high quality goods at low prices. This provides the justification

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

for profits, which may also be earned as a result of monopoly, chance, or anti-social activities.

CHAPTER IX BUSINESS ORGANIZATION PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To describe the different forms of business organization. 

2. To indicate the reasons for the dominance of the corporate form of business organization in the United States. 

3. To point out the advantages and disadvantages of the corporate form of business organization. 

4. To discuss the nature and the activities of coöperatives. 

Definition. A business is a productive unit composed of units of land, labor and capital, which are brought together and managed by an entrepreneur for the purpose of making profits. 

The business may be conducted by an individual entrepreneur, or by an organized group of individuals, such as a partnership or a corporation.

FORMS OF BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

Individual Entrepreneur.-Businesses carried on by a single entrepreneur (a single owner) are, for the most part, those in which a small enterprise or an enterprise of moderate size has certain competitive advantages.

A great many retail businesses, for example, are owned by single entrepreneurs. While it is true that many large chain stores and department stores exist in this country, the small neighborhood store still survives and doubtless will continue to survive. The reason for this is that people are attracted by the warm friendly relationships which come from dealing with a man who is running his own business, rather than with the salaried employees of a great corporation. Another reason is that people find it convenient to patronize the grocery stores, butcher stores, drug stores, etc., which are located in their own neighborhood. Therefore, these stores are limited to neighborhood patronage and cannot grow to a very large size. 106

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

107 Businesses which manufacture goods of extremely high quality for a limited market also may be operated to good advantage by an individual entrepreneur. The production of these articles often involves a great deal of handwork, and the mechanized, standardized methods of large-scale production cannot be applied. For example, although most men's clothing in this country is made in large factories, custom tailors still continue to run businesses as individual entrepreneurs. 

Finally, most of the farms in this country are run by individual entrepreneurs.

The individual entrepreneurship is fairly well adapted to the needs of the several businesses mentioned above. In most other businesses today, however, large-scale production is used, and large-scale production almost always demands more capital than can be supplied by a single owner. Moreover, in an individual entrepreneurship there is complete dependence on the business ability of one man. Another disadvantage is the unlimited liability of the owner; he is responsible for all the debts incurred by his firm.

The Partnership. The partnership is a business venture in which two or more entrepreneurs are engaged jointly. The responsibilities and the profits of the business are divided among the partners according to previous agreements. 

As a general thing, a partnership cannot supply great amounts of capital, but it can supply more capital than any one of the partners would be willing or able to invest if he were in business by himself. In addition to this, a partnership makes possible the application of more than one man's ability to the problems of a business. Another characteristic of the partnership is that it is easy to establish, easy to change or terminate, and rather informal in its workings. 

The partnership seems to be peculiarly fitted to the professions, such as law, medicine, and accounting. In these fields a great deal of capital is not required, and the partners contribute their personal skills and accomplishments, rather than money. 

In addition, some brokerage houses and private banking firms use the partnership form of business, because it enables them to avoid the governmental supervision to which corporations are subjected.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Within these fields the partnership serves its purpose well, but because of its inability to bring together large quantities of capital, it is not adapted to the needs of the businesses in which large-scale production is dominant. 

The Corporation. The corporation is the typical form of modern big business.* It may be regarded as an artificial person created by the state, and as such it can buy and sell property, engage in contractual relationships, and sue or be sued in the courts.

The corporation has certain characteristics which distinguish it from the other forms of business organization. In general, these characteristics are the charter upon which it is founded, the securities it issues, and its internal organization. 

1. The Charter. The legal recognition of the corporation, and the governmental permission for its existence, are provided by a document known as the charter. The charter contains, among other things, the name of the corporation, a description of the purposes for which it was founded, and a statement of the capitalization, or the total amount of money which is to be invested in the business. 

2. The Securities.-The most important of the distinctive features of the corporation is that its ownership is divided into a large number of small parts, ownership of each part being evidenced by a certificate which is known as a share of stock. 

When a corporation is organized, a certain number of these shares of stock are issued and sold to the public. The total amount of money which is paid for all the shares determines the capitalization, that is, the amount of money invested in the company. Each stockholder is a part owner of the corporation and, as such, is entitled to share in the profits, in proportion to the number of shares he owns. The profits which are paid to stockholders are called dividends.

When a share of stock is issued it usually bears a certain face * This does not mean that all corporations are big. Many of them are of moderate size or are small. However, all the great business enterprises which play such an important part in our economic life are corporations. †The organizers of the corporation (the incorporators) may themselves buy some of the stock they are issuing. In some cases (usually small corporations) the incorporators buy all the stock.

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

109 value, or par value. However, the market price of the stock, the price at which it is actually bought and sold, is rarely the same as the par value. This market price depends upon such factors as the company's profits and prospects for the future, and it may be either greater or less than the par value. 

The value of a share of stock is sometimes expressed as book value. This is determined by computing the total value of the corporation's assets (all the valuable things which it owns) and subtracting its debts to outsiders. The resulting figure is then divided by the total number of shares of stock which the corporation has issued. In this way, we find what each share of stock is worth in terms of the actual possessions of the corporation. 

In general, stocks are divided into two classes, common stock and preferred stock. Holders of common stock are usually entitled to vote on the policies of the corporation, in proportion to the number of shares they own. Holders of preferred stock usually do not have this voting privilege, but they share in the profits of the corporation on a preferred basis. This means that they are paid their dividends, usually a fixed or a semi-fixed rate, before dividends are paid to the holders of common stock. 

If this payment of dividends exhausts the profits of the corporation, the holders of common stock will not be paid anything. 

On the other hand, if profits are very high the holders of common stock will be paid unusually high dividends, while preferred stockholders will still be paid their fixed or semi-fixed rate. If a corporation needs additional capital it may borrow money from the investing public. In evidence of its indebtedness to these investors, the corporation issues documents known as bonds. Thus, bondholders are creditors of the corporation, while the stockholders are owners of the corporation. As creditors the bondholders are paid a fixed annual rate of interest which is specified in the terms under which the bonds are issued. 

In addition, these terms make provision for the repayment of the loan at some future time, the maturity date. 

If the corporation is forced to go out of business, bondholders will be paid the principal of their loans before the stockholders receive their share. Preferred stockholders are reimbursed next. 

If there are any assets left after these two payments, they are distributed among the holders of common stock.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

The bonds and stocks which a corporation issues are called its securities. We can see that of the three types of securities which we have mentioned, bonds are the most conservative and dependable investment. As long as the corporation has any resources at all, it will meet the interest charges it has agreed to pay its bondholders. At the other extreme, common stocks are a comparatively speculative investment. Dividends are paid to common stockholders only after payments on all other types of securities have been made. However, in a time of very high profits the common stockholder reaps the greatest benefits. The preferred stockholder dwells in a sort of middle ground. His investment is not so safe as that of the bondholder, nor so speculative as that of the common stockholder. 

The preceding classification of the various types of corporation securities is correct in general, but in actuality there are many exceptions and complications. For example, in a number of states, non-voting common stock is permitted. However, the 

New York Stock Exchange and Curb Market have refused to list new issues of this type, on the ground that it encourages minority control of corporations and may lead to serious abuses. 

On the other hand, common stock may bear special voting privileges, such as two or three votes per share, as compared with one vote for the ordinary common stock. Preferred stock may be divided into the cumulative and non-cumulative types. In the case of the former, if a corporation fails to pay any dividends during one or more years, the claims to the company's earnings cumulate or "heap up." Suppose that a corporation has issued cumulative preferred stock entitled to a preferential annual dividend of 7%. If this firm does not distribute any dividends for two years, but wishes to do so the third year, the preferred stockholders would be paid a dividend of 21% before the common stockholders would receive anything. In addition, preferred stock may be participating, that is, entitled to share further in the earnings of the corporation after the fixed rate has been paid. In large corporations, there may be several classes of preferred stock with different degrees of priority and participation. 

3. Internal Organization.-After the stock of the corporation has been issued, the stockholders elect a board of directors. 

This board, in turn, elects officers (president, vice-president, etc.) and the active management of the business is entrusted to these men.

In many cases, the officers, who are themselves sal-

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

111. aried employees, will hire other men to help them with the job of management. In large corporations the stockholders, who own the business, often have little or nothing to do with managing it. In other words, ownership and management are separated. Advantages of the Corporation. The corporation, as described above, has become the dominant form of modern business organization. This is due to the following advantages: 

1. Accumulation of Capital.-The greatest advantage of the corporation is that, by the sale of its securities, it can make use of the financial resources of many different people, and thus accumulate great masses of capital. Such accumulations of capital are necessary in order to buy the expensive machines and plants which are used in modern large-scale production. 

2. Limited Liability.-When an individual entrepreneurship or a partnership fails, all the personal resources of the owners may be taken over to meet the demands of the creditors. When a corporation fails, the liability, that is, the financial responsibility, of each stockholder is limited to the amount of money he has invested in the business. 

3. Permanency.-A corporation is not limited to the lifetime of any individual or group of individuals. 

4. Liquidity.-Investments in corporation securities are usually liquid. This means that the owner can convert his holdings into cash whenever he wishes. It is far more difficult to sell an interest in a partnership or single entrepreneurship. 

5. Legal Entity.-The corporation is a distinct legal entity. 

It exists apart from its stockholders and it is not necessary that the stockholders assume the burden of participating in its management.

Disadvantages of the Corporation.-The corporation has certain disadvantages which have been the cause of serious social problems. 

1. Absentee Ownership.-As we indicated above, in many cases the stockholders who own a corporation have nothing to do with managing it. Their only contact with the enterprise comes through the payment of dividends, and all that they require of the hired manager is that he administer the business so that satisfactory dividends are paid regularly. Managers know that this

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

is expected of them, and in their efforts to create profits, they have sometimes used methods which were not in the best interests of their employees, of the public, nor, in the long run, of the stockholders themselves.

Even where absentee ownership has not resulted in such abuses, it generally causes the corporation to acquire a hard impersonal character. The friendly, personal relations which a small business man can maintain with his customers and his 

THE CORPORATION SEPARATES OWNERSHIP AND MANAGEMENT 

MENUR 

GEMAL HEN MANAGEMENT DIVIDENDSE OWNERSHIP 

What social problems have resulted from this separation? employees are lacking. This sometimes leads to difficulties (for example, labor troubles) which an individual entrepreneur might be able to avoid. 

2. Exploitation of Investors.-In many cases investors have paid excessive prices for securities and later suffered severe losses. This has been due, in some instances, to watered stock. 

This means that a corporation has issued stock in excess of the value of its assets. The people who buy the stock, of course, are not aware of this. In other instances, investors have paid exces-

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

113 sive prices because speculative activity inflated the market price of the securities. In either case, prices eventually collapse, and the public loses part or all of its investment (page 151 ff). 

3. Evasion of Responsibilities.-Incorporation has sometimes been used as a screen behind which an individual or an organization has been able to operate in an anonymous manner and avoid undesirable publicity, unlimited liability, and other responsibilities and complications. 

HOW A MINORITY MAY CONTROL THE CORPORATION SMALL STOCKHOLDERS FAIL TO VOTE LARGE STOCK- HOLDERS 35% VOTING STOCK 

35% OF

VOTING STOCK SMALL STOCKHOLDERS 30% VOTING STOCK 

How do you explain the non-participation of so many small stockholders? 

4. Minority Control.-In most large corporations the stock is distributed among thousands of people who are scattered all over the country and in foreign countries. Many of these stockholders do not care to exercise their privilege of voting on corporation policies, or do not find it convenient to do so. As a result, it is often possible for a single stockholder or for a group of a few large stockholders to control a corporation, even though they own much less than 50% of the stock. This powerful minority may follow policies which are opposed to the interests and desires of the people who own a majority of the stock. 

In very large corporations, control often rests in the hands of the Board of Directors, who may own practically none of the stock. These men exercise control because they have the power

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

to send out proxies at the company's expense. Proxies are documents by which the stockholders grant permission to someone else, usually the Board of Directors or their appointees, to exercise their voting privileges. Many stockholders sign these proxies either because they have confidence in the directors, or because of sheer inertia. As a result, the directors are often able to outvote any stockholder or group of stockholders and thus may control an enterprise in which they have no investment. This may lead to serious abuses.

For example, the controlling group may own another corporation which sells materials to the first corporation. The men who control both enterprises can sell the materials at an excessive price and thus earn enormous profits. The securities of the corporation which is deliberately mismanaged in this way will probably deteriorate greatly in value and the other investors will lose heavily. Malpractices of this type were formerly common in the railroad industry, but governmental regulation has largely succeeded in eliminating them.

Coöperatives. The corporation, as we examined it above, is designed primarily to make profits for owners by producing goods through hired labor and selling these goods on the open market. However, there is a type of productive enterprise which is formed to promote the interests of large organized groups who participate in it, rather than to produce dividends for a relatively small group of stockholders. Organizations of this type are known as coöperatives. They can be divided into two general classes, consumers' and producers' coöperatives.

These 1. Consumers' Coöperatives.-The most important type of consumers' coöperative is a retail store or a system of retail stores organized and owned by a group of consumers. stores try to effect economies (and, in this way, to sell their goods at low prices) by buying in bulk directly from producers, without the use of middlemen. If the organization earns profits, they are distributed among the consumers in proportion to the amount of goods they have purchased at the coöperative during the year.

In addition, the consumers are paid a fixed rate of interest on the money they invested in the coöperative. 

Coöperative businesses of this type are extremely important in the Scandinavian countries and in England, where the famous

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION 

115 Rochdale coöperative stores have been established for almost a century. However, they have not reached an advanced stage of development in the United States. 

2. Producers' Coöperatives.-In some instances, groups of producers have sought to promote their mutual interests by forming coöperative organizations. For example, farmers have attempted to overcome the difficulties which the individual farmer encounters when he markets his product, by forming coöperative marketing organizations. Some of these enterprises, such as the Association of Wisconsin Dairy Farmers, have been highly successful. The farmers participating sell their goods directly to the coöperative and the coöperative, which has special facilities for transportation, storage, advertising, etc., sells the goods for the best possible price. Producers' coöperatives have also been established in restaurants, laundries, etc. In these cases, the workers own and administer the enterprises. 

Not all coöperatives have been successful. Some have failed because the people hired to manage the enterprise did not show the same energy and initiative as do the managers of privately owned businesses. As a whole, however, the coöperative movement has proved its worth in various fields and, in the future, may grow more important in our country. Many people believe that consumers' coöperatives, in particular, will become far more highly developed than they are at present. 

Governmental Business Activity.--In addition to the various forms of business enterprises mentioned above, our governmental units, local, state and Federal, have always engaged in productive activities to some extent. Examples are the Federal operation of the postal system and municipal operation of waterworks and power plants. However the government has engaged in these productive activities only when it appeared that some special public interest required it. By far the greater part of the production of commodities and services in this country is carried on by private business enterprises.* * It is interesting to note that various governmental and semi-governmental agencies are organized in the form of corporations. Examples are the Port Authority of New York and the Federal Deposit Insurance Cor poration (page 184). These governmental agencies are often characterized as non-profit corporations.

CHAPTER X  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning of "competition," and to discuss its good and bad effects. 

2. To explain the meaning of "monopoly." 

3. To describe the forces which determine the prices of monopolized products. 

4. To explain the attitude of the Federal Government toward monopolies.

COMPETITION 

Importance. We have stated in an earlier chapter that competition among business men, that is, their rivalry to obtain buyers for their products, is one of the basic institutions of our society. Competition is not altogether universal in this country. 

There are some fields in which there is no rivalry among sellers, because only one business enterprise exists to meet the demands of the public. As a whole, however, competition is typical of our economic life and exerts a very important influence upon it. 

There are certain desirable and certain undesirable features of this influence.

Advantages of Competition.-The great advantage of competition is that it forces the producer to make special efforts to obtain the patronage of the public. Business men try to turn out goods of high quality and to sell them for low prices, because they know that unless they do this they will lose business to their competitors. As a result of this, competition has stimulated the growth of invention. These inventions have been of two general types: (1) the development of new consumers' goods to attract the buyer, and (2) the development of improved industrial 118

methods and equipment in order to cut the costs of production and to turn out better commodities. 

Disadvantages of Competition.-In order to be able to match the low prices of competitors, business men have sometimes forced their employees to work for very low wages. In some cases, competition has led to the employment of women and children, who are generally willing to work for less than men do. 

Another undesirable effect of competition is that it sometimes creates a duplication of facilities. This means that two business enterprises are established in a field in which either one of them, by itself, would be able to fill the demand for the service or commodity they are producing. For example, you may have seen two grocery stores located in a neighborhood in which all the purchases of groceries could be easily handled by a single store. In such a case, one of the stores, the less efficiently managed, or the less advantageously located, will eventually be forced out of business. Such a business enterprise represents at least a partial waste of capital and labor, because it merely duplicates adequate facilities which already exist. (Duplication of facilities has been a particularly serious problem in the railroad industry.)

Very often, in these cases, the weaker competitor will be forced into bankruptcy. This may involve serious losses, not only to the bankrupt firm but also to the other firms and individuals who have been doing business with it. 

Competition often forces rival firms to engage in intensive and expensive advertising campaigns. Competing cigarette manufacturers in the United States offer an example of this. The tremendous cost of such advertising necessarily adds to the price of the product and the public does not benefit in any way. 

In certain fields, such as coal mining and oil drilling, competition leads to waste of natural resources. For example, an oil deposit which is being exploited by several competing companies may form a unit from an engineering point of view. 

However, each competitor will carry on operations according to his own interests. Under these conditions it will not be possible to apply the methods necessary to extract the greatest amount of oil at the lowest price.

Competition sometimes leads to various unfair and destruc-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

tive business practices. For example, a producer may attempt to drive out a competitor by temporarily selling his product at extremely low prices ("cut-throat competition"). Under the pressure of intense competition, rival firms have been known to engage in slander, bribery of employees, and even destruction of property.

MONOPOLY 

Meaning of Monopoly.-We have said that there are fields in which there is no rivalry or competition among business men. 

This condition is known as a monopoly. A monopoly may exist because there is only one enterprise engaged in a given type of business, or because the several enterprises which do exist work together in a unified manner and adopt a common price policy. 

In either case, the monopolists control the price of the commodity they are producing because there are no competitors who can offer the buyer the same commodity at a lower price. In addition, the monopolist controls the supply of the commodity, and its kind and quality. If the monopolist feels that it will serve his interests to hold the supply off the market for a period of time, he is able (within certain limits) to do so. If he wishes to market only a particular grade or quality of the product, that is also within his power. In any case, he need not fear the loss of business to a competitor.

Kinds of Monopoly 

1. Legal Monopoly.—When the government grants an individual or organization the exclusive right to manufacture and sell a commodity or a service, a legal monopoly exists. When a person writes a book, for example, the government issues a copyright, which forbids anyone to publish the book in whole or in part, unless the author gives him the right to do so. When a person develops a new invention, the government issues a patent which gives the inventor the exclusive right to produce and sell his invention. Copyrights and patents are issued for limited periods, but during the periods in which they are in effect, they create legal monopolies. There are some cases in which the government reserves for itself the right to engage in certain productive activities. For example, the United States Government operates the postal system on a monopoly basis. Some foreign

governments monopolize the sale of such products as salt and tobacco. 

2. Natural Monopoly.-One type of natural monopoly results from the possession of some valuable natural resource. The 

Kimberley diamond mines and the asphalt lake of Trinidad are examples of natural monopolies. The men who own these enterprises control practically the entire supply of the commodity. 

A natural monopoly of another sort is the monopoly of situation. Here the advantage results from the possession of an advantageous site or location for a business. Examples of such sites are buildings at busy street-corners, and the banks of rapidly flowing rivers, where hydro-electric plants can be built. 

The New York Central Railroad possesses such a monopoly in its "water level" route to the West by means of which it avoids any mountain grades.

Another sort of natural monopoly results from the possession of unusual talents by individuals. Paderewski, Charlie Chaplin, and Lindbergh are examples of such personal monopolies. Each of these men can do something which few if any other people can do and, as a result, they command high prices for their services. 

An important class of natural monopolies is composed of those businesses which are monopolistic by virtue of their inherent nature. For example, if a company which supplies electric power already has its wires running into the buildings of a town, it is almost impossible and extremely wasteful for a competing company to enter the field. The expense of installing a new system and the difficulty of winning over customers who are already being served are insurmountable obstacles. This is true, also, of enterprises which supply gas, water, etc. Moreover, in the case of such a service as the telephone, the existence of competing companies would result in great confusion and inefficiency. 

3. Capitalist Monopoly.—Capitalist monopoly results from the concentration of great quantities of capital in a business enterprise. The business men who control this capital may use it to purchase natural monopolies, such as sites, raw materials, and patent rights. They may acquire new and efficient machinery, as a result of which they will be able to enjoy all the advan-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

tages of large-scale production. They may even be able to influence the passage of favorable legislation and the granting of charters and franchises by the government. 

The result of these advantages and of others which result from similar skillful handling of capital, is to drive the less efficient competitors from the field. In some cases, the power of accumulated capital has been so great that all competition has been eliminated, and a monopoly or a semi-monopoly has been established.

Monopoly Price.-Once the monopoly has been established, whether as a result of natural conditions or of financial and industrial power, the principal advantage to the monopolist is his control over prices. The monopolist does not have to reckon with competitors who may try to attract his customers by underselling him. Therefore, unless he is restrained by some outside power, he can set his prices at the point which will yield him the greatest net return. This price is called the monopoly price. 

Monopoly price is not necessarily the highest price at which the monopolist can sell some of his products. If the price is set too high the following forces will tend to lower it: (1) The demand will drop off because some people will not be willing or able to buy the product, and the total profits of the monopolist will be decreased. (2) There will be a tendency to use substitutes for the exorbitantly priced article. For example, if aluminum, which is a monopoly in this country, were priced beyond a certain limit, people would use other materials in its place. (3) A competitor may be brought into existence, because there is often potential competition waiting for an opportunity to invade a monopolized field. (4) If the price is extremely high and the commodity is a necessity, the government may step in. 

Government action may take the form of price regulation, or the government may actually offer the same article for sale in competition with the monopolist.

In the following table you will find an illustration of the way in which monopoly price is set:

When the price is increased beyond five cents per unit, the demand drops off so sharply that the total profits are decreased. Therefore, the monopolist will set his price at five cents per unit, the price which yields the greatest net return. 

The monopolist would probably find it necessary to experiment with different prices before he discovered that the price of five cents was the most profitable.

Monopoly as a Social Problem.—As our discussion shows, the monopolist possesses great powers. Within the limits indicated, he can charge the public whatever prices he wishes. He can withhold necessary supplies from the market if he believes that this will serve his interests. He can force the public to accept goods of inferior quality. If these powers are exercised in an irresponsible manner, as they often have been, an extremely serious social problem results. In the United States, the government has found it necessary to step in and to defend the public from wholesale exploitation at the hands of the monopolists.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS MONOPOLY PROBLEM IN THE UNITED STATES 

The Trusts. In the United States, the problem of monopoly has centered about the activities and the regulation of certain great business organizations known as trusts. The term "trust" 

MONOPOLY PRICE 

RESTRAINING 

INFLUENCES ON MONOPOLY PRICE SUBSTITUTION CONSUMER GOVERNMENT ACTION POTENTIAL COMPETITION DECREASING DEMAND 

Is it always socially desirable to maintain competitive conditions?

was applied originally to a combination which was formed when the stockholders of a number of competing corporations appointed a group of men known as a board of trustees. This board. issued trustees' certificates which they exchanged for the stock of the various corporations. This stock carried voting power, so that the small group of trustees acquired control over the policies and prices of firms which formerly had been active competitors. The stockholders were willing to do this because they hoped that the monopoly created in this way would yield great profits.

This original type of trust disappeared under the fire of the courts, which held that trustees' agreements and interchange of certificates were illegal because they were not provided for in the charters under which the corporations were operating. Today the term "trust" applies to any business enterprise or combination of business enterprises which enjoys monopoly or semimonopoly powers over the price of the commodity it sells. In general, the trusts, in the sense in which we have just defined the term, have taken four forms. 

1. The Great Consolidated Corporation.-This is a corporation, organized in the regular manner, which is so large and so powerful that it dominates its field of production. 

2. The Holding Company. This is a company chartered for the purpose of buying the securities of other companies.*

In this way, it is able to obtain control of various subsidiary firms, which otherwise might compete with each other. 

3. Interlocking Directorates.-An interlocking directorate exists when the same group of men serves on the board of directors of each of several firms. This group of men can control all the corporations by appointing officers who will follow their instructions. 

4. Intercorporate Stockholding. This is a situation which exists when a small group of men control several corporations by virtue of the fact that they own large blocks of stock in each of them. As we explained in the previous chapter, men who own much less than fifty per cent of the stock can often domi- Thus, the illegality of the trustees' agreement was avoided, because holding companies received the permission of the state to hold the securities of other companies.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

nate a large corporation. In this way, corporations which might otherwise compete with each other are subject to a single unified control.

Reasons for the Growth of Combinations.-Great industrial combinations, of the various types we have examined, first appeared in this country about sixty years ago. They have developed rapidly and have become an extremely important element in our economic life.

Four factors have contributed to the development of this type of organization. (1) Business men wished to create monopoly conditions, under which they could raise prices without fear of losing business to competitors. (2) In many cases, competition had become so keen that business men attempted to drive rivals from the field by selling their products at prices under the cost of production. Such "cut-throat" methods of competition resulted in severe losses, not only to the men who were forced out of business, but also to those who were temporarily victorious in the competitive struggle. (3) Trusts, which bring together large amounts of capital, make possible large-scale production, with its many advantages. (4) Great profits were to be made in the promotion and sale of stock in various trusts. Anti-Trust Legislation. When the monopoly powers of the trusts began to manifest themselves in comparatively high prices and in the elimination of the small business man, various groups in this country clamored for Federal protection. In response to this, a number of so-called anti-trust laws were passed. 

1. Sherman Act (1890).—This Act attempted to curb the trusts by declaring that any "contract, combination in the form of trust or otherwise, or conspiracy, in restraint of trade . . . is illegal." This means, in plain language, that any attempt to throttle competition is against the law. Several trusts were ordered dissolved under this Act, but, in general, it was not very effective. 

2. Clayton Act (1914).-This law attempted to supplement the Sherman Act by prohibiting the operation of holding companies, interlocking directorates, and intercorporate stockholding, if such practices lessen competition. In addition, price discriminations and exclusive selling were forbidden, where they

tended to lessen competition. The Clayton Act tried to curb the trusts by defining, in a detailed and explicit manner, just what practices and methods of organization were illegal. 

3. Federal Trade Commission.-This Commission is authorized to investigate the activities of businesses engaged in interstate commerce. If such investigations reveal violations of the anti-trust laws, the Commission may recommend suitable readjustments in the corporations' methods of doing business. In addition, the Commission is authorized to issue orders for the 

MONOPOLY ANTI-TRUST LAWS DOMINATION OF AMERICAN ECONOMIC LIFE THE ANTI-TRUST LAWS || 

What effect did the National Industrial Recovery Act have on the 

Anti-Trust Laws? restraint of various unfair methods of competition, such as false advertising, adulteration of goods, bribery of competitors' employees, selling goods below costs in order to damage competitors, and any other practices designed to prevent active and honest competition among producers.

Famous Anti-Trust Cases.-The government sued a great many corporations for violations of the anti-trust laws. Some of these suits were successfully prosecuted, while in others the

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

courts ruled that the law had not been violated. A few of the more famous cases follow. 

1. Northern Securities Company.—In 1914, this corporation, a holding company controlling two railroads which had formerly been active competitors in the Northwest, was haled before the United States Supreme Court. There it was judged that the company was acting in restraint of trade. It was ordered to dissolve, and the two roads were placed on a competitive basis again. 

2. Standard Oil Company and American Tobacco Company. -In 1910, these two great corporations were sued by the government for violation of the Sherman Act. They were both ordered dissolved and they were broken up into the forms in which they exist today. For example, the Standard Oil Company became the Standard Oil Companies of New York, New Jersey, Indiana, etc. 

3. United States Steel Corporation. This corporation was charged with being a combination in illegal restraint of trade. 

In 1920, after many years of weary litigation, the Supreme Court decided that, even though the United States Steel Corporation had tried to monopolize its field, it had not been able to Therefore, the corporation was allowed to preserve its existing form.

Recent Trends.-Since the World War, there appears to have been a definite trend toward modification and relaxation of the anti-trust laws in various fields. By the Webb-Pomerene 

Act of 1918, American exporters are permitted to combine, provided they do not attempt any restraint of trade against American competitors in the export field. This law is justified on the grounds that American exporters are faced with powerful foreign combinations with whom they cannot deal successfully as individuals. Similar legislation has been passed legalizing combinations of railroads, and combinations of farmers for purposes of coöperative marketing. The anti-trust laws were relaxed in all these cases because it was believed that the prosperity of all the groups involved required it, and the general public interest would not be harmed.

The National Industrial Recovery Act gave further evidence that the country is not so much interested in competition, in

itself, as it is in efficient and reasonably profitable production. 

Under the terms of this law, all business enterprises which agreed to operate under the codes of fair competition were to be exempted from the anti-trust laws. The NRA went into the discard when the Supreme Court found it unconstitutional (1935). 

In 1938, Congress created a Temporary National Economic Committee (TNEC) to make a thorough investigation of every phase of the monopoly problem. In July, 1939, this body issued a preliminary report which recommended certain amendments to the Clayton Act and suggested new legislation to control the use of patents. It is believed that the intensive study being made by the TNEC will add a great deal to our understanding of the monopoly problem, as it exists today. 

Natural Monopolies and the Government.-As we indicated above, certain businesses, such as gas, electric, and water supply companies, railroads, etc., are natural monopolies. In all these cases, it is either undesirable or impossible to maintain competitive conditions. In order to prevent consumer exploitation, the state and Federal governments subject enterprises of this type to very extensive regulation, particularly with regard to the rates which they charge.

Where competition is impossible, government action must supply the control of prices which is ordinarily performed by competition.
