CHAPTER XI  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning of "value." 

2. To explain the difference between “value” and “utility.” 

3. To indicate the relationship between "value" and "price." 

4. To show how prices are established.

Meaning of "Value."-As we survey the various fields of economics, we see men making all sorts of efforts to produce all sorts of things. What accounts for this multitude of efforts? 

Why are men willing to spend all this time and energy at work? 

It is true, of course, that many people enjoy their work, but the basic reason for our productive efforts is that we want the things we can produce by our labor. We want them and are willing to undergo some pain and privation to get them because we know that they will yield us pleasure and satisfaction. In other words, these things have value for us. 

Definition.-Value is the importance or worth which we attribute to a thing. This importance or worth is indicated and measured by the fact that the thing in question can be exchanged for other desirable things.

There is a distinction between value and utility. There are numerous free goods (page 21), such as air and water, which are of the greatest utility but ordinarily have little or no value, that is, cannot be exchanged for other desirable goods. On the other hand, there are many things, such as automobiles and diamonds, which we can very well do without but which have great value.

The factor that accounts for this discrepancy is scarcity. 

Air and water are useful, but because they are so plentiful they have no exchange value. When water becomes relatively scarce, as is the case during a drought, it may acquire a high exchange 132

value (page 22). Fountain pens are not nearly so useful as air and water, but because of their relative scarcity they have more value.

Thus, there are two factors involved in value-utility and scarcity.* How does this factor of scarcity affect the value of a thing? 

Law of Diminishing Utility.-Suppose that you enter your house on a hot day and that you are very thirsty. You go to the faucet, where you can get all the water you want, and you drink one glass of water, which gives you a great deal of satisfaction. You drink a second glass, which you find somewhat less satisfying. Then you drink a third glass, which slakes your thirst completely and provides you with very little satisfaction. 

You may use a fourth glass to give a drink to your dog and a fifth glass to water a plant which stands upon the window sill. 

This final glass gives you practically no satisfaction, and after it is used you have no further immediate use for water. 

Now, note that the consumption of each succeeding unit gave you less satisfaction than did the preceding unit. In short, the utility of a given commodity diminishes as you consume or possess a greater number of units of that commodity. This is true of the consumption of all types of goods and is known as the Law of Diminishing Utility.

Marginal Utility.—The final and least important or satisfying glass of water which you consumed in the example given above is known as the marginal unit. The satisfaction which was derived from the consumption of this final glass is called marginal utility.

It is this marginal utility which determines the value of a commodity. Men do not value water by the satisfaction they derive from drinking one glass or two glasses on a hot day. 

This is so because under ordinary circumstances water is so plentiful that all men have enough to cover these vital needs. 

They do value water by the marginal utility because this indicates the satisfaction that new acquisitions of water will yield 

Note that all valuable things must have some usefulness. However, not all useful things are valuable.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

them. Since water is so plentiful, its marginal utility and its value are practically zero. 

Now, let us apply this idea to fountain pens. Fountain pens are comparatively scarce. Most of us have one, or perhaps two of them. If you were to lose your only fountain pen, you would be greatly inconvenienced.* In other words, the satisfaction which you derive from your last (oldest) or least satisfying pen (that is, the marginal utility of fountain pens) is high. 

Therefore, people are willing to give something desirable in exchange for fountain pens and fountain pens have a fairly high value.

Scarcity. From the above account, the importance of scarcity in determining values is evident. However, it may occur to you to ask, "What determines the relative scarcity of different things?" There are several factors involved in this. 

1. The relative scarcity of raw materials is determined by the extent and accessibility of the natural supply. For example, there are vast natural deposits of coal and iron. Gold is less plentiful. Ivory and jade are very rare. Finished goods made from these raw materials are correspondingly scarce. 

2. The scarcity of some goods is affected by the nature of the work it takes to produce them. Goods which depend on some rare personal skill are necessarily scarce. Examples of this are great works of art or the services of a famous surgeon. 

Goods which require less extraordinary ability are more plentiful. 

3. The scarcity of goods is affected by the cost involved in producing them. Why are Fords more plentiful than the very expensive automobiles? The materials of which automobiles are made are plentiful, but the cost of forming these raw materials into a Rolls Royce is so great that comparatively few of them are made. Fords, which can be made more cheaply, are produced in far greater numbers. 

Values and our Economic Life. To a considerable extent, values guide and set the character of our economic life. In the *Note that the loss of a fountain pen would cause you less distress if you owned several pens than if you owned only one. In the former case the marginal utility of the last or least satisfying unit is lower.

first place, the worth or importance we attribute to things plays an essential part in determining what things we will choose to produce. For example, our great automobile industry has grown up because we value pleasant and rapid transportation. 

The publishing industry exists because people value the instruction and the pleasure they can derive from reading books. 

Therefore, one of the basic problems of the producer is to anticipate or to interpret the value which people attribute to things. In the days of Mark Overton, this was comparatively simple, for he produced only for himself or for his neighbors. 

The modern entrepreneur, however, produces in anticipation of demand and for a widespread market. If he misinterprets the value which other people attribute to goods, he will not be able to sell his products and he will not earn profits. 

However, not only what we produce, but also the manner in which we produce it, is an indication of how we value things. 

You will recall that in Chapter I we discussed the development of the complexity of our economic structure. This development took place because people wanted (attributed worth or value to) more and better goods than they could produce under the simple conditions of frontier life. Therefore, people tried to devise methods of production and distribution which would enable them to raise their standard of living. As a result, they developed large-scale production, railroads, banks, etc. 

PRICES 

Definition.-Price is an evaluation of something expressed in terms of money. The price of any good is the amount of money that is exchanged for that good.

Nature of Prices.-Prices come into existence when a buyer and a seller agree to exchange certain goods for a certain amount of money. Suppose that a man owns a horse which he does not care to sell for less than $250. If a friend offers him $200 for the horse, no price has been established, for the buyer and the seller have not reached an agreement. However, if another customer were to appear and offer $250 for the horse, the owner would sell it and the price would be established at that figure.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Suppose that the owner of the horse met a man who was willing to pay $300 for the animal. In this case, there would certainly be a sale, but no one can say definitely what the price would be. There is an overlapping of evaluations to the extent of $50 and the price would fall somewhere between $250 and $300. Whether it would fall closer to the former figure or to the latter would depend on which of the two men could drive a better bargain.

If all three offers for the horse had been made at the same time, the owner would have chosen the highest price, that is, $300. On the other hand, if any one of the purchasers had found several men who were willing to sell horses of equal quality, he would have chosen the horse which was being sold at the lowest price.

Prices in a Competitive Market.—In all the instances given above, prices are determined between a single seller and a single buyer, or between a single seller and several buyers, or between a single buyer and several sellers. However, it is generally the case that many sellers are offering goods and many buyers are purchasing goods. This is known as 

This is known as a competitive market; sellers compete for the buyers' money and buyers compete for the sellers' goods. 

How are prices set under these conditions? Let us consider the case of a single commodity-tomatoes. Many farmers have tomatoes for sale and many housewives wish to purchase them. 

Now, if the price for these tomatoes is set at a high figure, many farmers will bring their crop to market and offer to sell them, that is, the supply (the total amount of a commodity available for sale at a given price) will go up. On the other hand, housewives will be less willing or able to buy the tomatoes at this high price. Therefore, the demand (which is the willingness to buy a commodity at a given price plus ability to pay) will fall. A low price for the tomatoes will have just the opposite effect. Demand will rise and supply will fall. 

The following chart will show you how supply and demand vary at different price levels.

You will see from this chart that a rise in the price for tomatoes results in an increase of the supply and a decrease of the demand. A fall in price results in a decrease of the supply and an increase of the demand.

TABLE OF PRICE MOVEMENTS The Demand for Tomatoes 

(in pounds)

The Supply of Tomatoes 

Price per Pound (in pounds) 100,000 20¢ 10,000 80,000 19¢ 20,000 75,000 18¢ 30,000 70,000 17¢ 35,000 60,000 16¢ 45,000 50,000 15¢ 50,000 40,000 14¢ 60,000 35,000 13¢ 65,000 30,000 12¢ 70,000 25,000 11¢ 80,000 20,000 10¢ 90,000 

Notice what happens when the price reaches fifteen cents per pound. The demand and the supply at this point equal each other. In other words, at this price the total demand for tomatoes will be met and the total supply will be purchased. If the price were set higher than fifteen cents the supply would exceed the demand and a number of farmers would not be able to sell the tomatoes which they sent to market. If the price were set lower than fifteen cents, the demand would exceed the supply, and a number of customers who wished to purchase tomatoes would not be able to obtain them. Therefore, fifteen cents, the price at which demand equals supply, is the point at which the market price will actually be set. 

In a competitive market, prices are set at the point where the supply of a commodity is equal to the demand for it. 

Law of Single Price.-Although there were many buyers and many sellers of tomatoes in our example above, all the tomatoes were sold for fifteen cents per pound. This illustrates an important fact about prices which is known as the Law of

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Single Price. This law states that the forces of demand and supply tend to establish a common price for all goods of the same type which are sold in a given market at a given time. A seller may cut prices in an attempt to attract buyers, but he will eventually be forced to raise prices again or his competitors will be forced to reduce prices to his level. 

Elasticity of Demand and Supply. We have seen that changes in prices cause changes in supply and demand. The responsiveness of the supply and demand of a commodity to such price changes is known as elasticity.

Demand. There is a certain amount of elasticity to the demand for all commodities. However, the demand for such articles as bread, salt and other necessities will not vary a great deal if the price varies. People must continue to consume these things if they are to live and although very poor people may vary their consumption somewhat according to price, the total change will not be very great. On the other hand, the demand for luxury goods, such as jewels, theatre tickets, and expensive clothing, is highly elastic. People do not need fixed amounts of these commodities in order to live, and they will vary their consumption of them sharply as the price rises and falls. 

In the case of luxury goods, an influence known as "perverse elasticity" sometimes comes into effect. Some wealthy people derive satisfaction from buying expensive goods, not so much because these things are useful or beautiful, as because their expensiveness is widely known and will reflect some credit or distinction on the owners. If the price of these goods were to fall, the people in question might buy less rather than more of them. The goods would no longer be "exclusive" and would not be able to satisfy the "snobbish' impulses of wealthy purchasers.

Supply. The elasticity of the supply of a commodity is determined in part by the elasticity of the demand. Under modern conditions, if people are willing to buy greater amounts of a commodity, it will, in most cases, be supplied. This is particularly true if the commodity in question is produced by machines. In these cases it is usually possible to expand the output greatly in a short time. However, in the case of commodities and services which are produced by hand (such as paint-

ings, surgical operations, and watch repairing), it is sometimes not possible to increase supply sharply, even though the demand has increased. This is true also of agricultural products. The output of a farm usually cannot be increased with the same facility as the output of a factory.

Normal Price.-Under free competitive conditions, prices tend to hover about the cost of production of the least efficient, or marginal producer. (Note that all competitors, the least as well as the most efficient producers, charge a single price for the same goods.) If prices fall below the costs of production of the marginal producer, he will be forced out of business. On the other hand, if prices rise above this point, other and less efficient producers will be able to enter the field. These newcomers will then become the marginal producers. 

A price which is about equal to the costs of production of the marginal producer is known as the normal price. The forces of competition operate in such a way that in the long run normal price tends to equal the market price, that is the price for which goods are actually sold. (It is important to note here that the costs of production, which determine the normal price, include whatever margin of profit is necessary to induce the producer to remain in business. If these profits are not paid, the producer will ultimately cease production, that is, go out of business.)

If prices rise far above the cost of production in any business activity, that business becomes highly profitable. Competitors will be attracted by the prospects of profits, and production will be increased as each additional competitor puts new factories into operation. When more goods than the consumers can readily use are thrown upon the market, prices drop back toward the cost of production. If prices fall below the cost of producing the articles, some of the producers will curtail production or may even close their plants to avoid losses. Curtailed production will diminish the supply of the goods, and prices will begin to mount again toward the cost of production. 

However, you must realize that what we have just described is only a tendency for prices to equal the cost of production. 

There are many exceptions. A notable example is the automobile industry. The prices of automobiles were so much higher

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

than the cost of production that unusually high profits were possible over a period of many years. Many other examples of the same general type might be given. 

Producers' Surplus. Since market price hovers about the cost of production of the marginal producer, more efficient or more fortunate producers (whose costs are less than the market price) will be able to earn profits. This profit is sometimes known as a producers' surplus. Suppose that the marginal producer of a particular grade of shoes in a given market has production and sales costs of $5 per pair. This, by definition, will be the market price. Thus, a manufacturer whose costs are $4 per pair will earn a producers' surplus of $1; a manufacturer whose costs are $3.50 will earn $1.50.

Consumers' Surplus.-When a commodity is offered for sale, there will probably be a group of purchasers who consider the article to be worth just about the market price. If there is any rise in price, they will refuse to buy, for they will feel that they can obtain "better value" for their money if they spend it on something else, or save it for future expenditure. Purchasers of this type are said to be marginal consumers. (Note that the market price is equivalent to the marginal consumer's evaluation of the goods.) On the other hand, there will probably be purchasers who would be willing, if necessary, to pay more than the market price for the commodity in question. Since these purchasers buy at the same price as the marginal consumer, they are said to receive a consumers' surplus. 

Normal Price and Market Price. If perfectly free competition prevailed (which is an ideal rather than an actual condition) market price would always be approximately equal to normal price. However, in our society, certain forces operate which interfere with the free working of competition and tend to prevent the two prices from coinciding. 

1. If monopoly or semi-monopoly conditions exist in an industry, prices will be fixed at the level which yields the highest profits, as described in the previous chapter. In this case, the market price will probably be well above the cost of production of the firm or firms in the field. However, government regulation may have the effect of forcing prices down toward normal price.

2. If an industry in which monopoly conditions do not prevail is earning exceptionally high profits, it may be some time before new firms will enter the field. The reason for this is that the various factors of production are relatively immobile; it takes time for the entrepreneur to decide to go into business and then to organize land, labor and capital into a productive enterprise. This is particularly true of industries in which largescale production is dominant and large amounts of capital and an elaborate technical organization are necessary. 

Therefore, the market price of particular commodities may be well above normal price for a considerable length of time before added competition will force prices down. 

3. In all those industries in which fixed charges (page 49) are high, it may pay the entrepreneur to sell his product at prices below the cost of production rather than suspend operations altogether. This is because fixed charges must be met whether production continues or not. If the prices for which the business man can sell his product bring in enough to cover his operating expenses and at least part of his fixed charges, it will pay him to continue producing. In such a case, then, market price may be below normal price. Of course, a firm cannot continue losing money indefinitely; sooner or later the losses will force it into bankruptcy. 

4. In some cases, a customary or habitual price exists which has been established by usage and is accepted by both buyers and sellers. For example, five cents is the customary price for an ice cream cone, a shoe shine, and for many other commodities and services. This price is relatively stable and may be far above normal price. 

5. A sudden change in market conditions may set the market price far above or far below normal price. For example, fashion may make a certain type of cloth extremely popular and the producers who happen to have a stock of this cloth will be able to sell it at very high prices. On the other hand, a sudden shift in demand may force producers to sell their goods at prices below the cost of manufacture, that is, below the normal price. 

Of course, if the changed demand appears to be relatively permanent, new firms will probably enter the field or old ones will be forced out of it, depending upon the circumstances.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Summary.-Value, or the importance or worth which men attribute to things because of their utility and scarcity, is one of the essential factors in determining the goods which will be produced to satisfy our needs. The prices at which these goods are exchanged are the monetary expressions of the values which society attributes to the goods. 

QUESTIONS 

1. Italy is a much smaller and poorer country than the United 

States, yet it has many more opera houses. Explain how this fact illustrates the statement that the different values which we attach to things play an essential part in determining what we produce. 

2. (a) Can a thing be valuable if it is not useful? (b) Are all useful things valuable? (c) Are all scarce things valuable? Explain. 

3. Why is bread, the "staff of life," less valuable than caviar? 

4. A number of years ago there was a short-lived fad for midget golf-courses. When the popularity of this form of recreation waned, many business men who had invested in them lost money. Show how this was a result of misinterpretation of values. 

5. (a) Define price. (b) Will a price be established if you are willing to sell your pen-knife for one dollar and a friend offers you fifty cents; if you are willing to sell it for fifty cents and a friend offers you one dollar? Explain. 

6. (a) What is meant by a competitive market? (b) In what ype of market does your family buy most of the goods it uses? 

7. (a) Explain the following statement: "If society wants a product, it must be prepared to pay enough to produce it." (b) Explain why profits are considered one of the costs of production. 

8. (a) During what part of the year does your family use most tomatoes? Explain. (b) During what part of the year does a millionaire use most tomatoes? Explain. 

9. (a) Why do prices tend to hover around the costs of production of the marginal producer? (b) Does a rise in prices necessarily mean that new producers will be attracted? Give reasons. 

10. (a) Explain the fact that the supply of shoes is more elastic than the supply of oil paintings? (b) Under what circumstances can the supply of agricultural products be elastic? 

11. Do you think that the demands of rich people are more elastic than the demands of poor people? Explain.

12. (a) Explain the fact that if a man owns one suit of clothing he will take greater pains to preserve it than if he owned six suits. (b) If everyone owned six suits of clothing, what would happen to the value of clothing? Explain. 

13. (a) What is the Law of Single Price? (b) Have you ever seen or heard of any exceptions to this law? (c) How do you account for these exceptions? 

14. Look through a newspaper or magazine and find an advertisement which you think is trying to take advantage of the "perverse elasticity" of demand. Explain how this is done.

CHAPTER XII  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain what is meant by "market" and "middleman." 

2. To describe the functions of the middleman. 

3. To describe the nature of the great organized markets. 

4. To explain the purpose of the SEC. 

Definition. In everyday speech, when we speak of "going to market," we generally mean a particular store or group of stores. When we speak of a market in economics, however, we refer to the total opportunities which exist for buying and selling a commodity or a service at a given time and place. Thus we speak of the "Chicago wheat market in 1930," and the "New York labor market in 1936." A market, understood in this sense, is not a store or a building but rather a set of conditions resulting from the play of many economic forces, such as the weather, supply and demand, transportation costs, etc. 

The middleman is the human element in the mechanism of the market. His function is to transfer the output of the producers to the hands of the consumers. Wholesalers, storekeepers, and brokers are examples of middlemen.

Services of the Middleman.-The middleman is a type of entrepreneur and, as such, he earns profits. What services does the middleman perform for society which justify these profits? 

1. Financing.-A considerable period of time generally elapses between the production of goods and their sale to the ultimate consumer. Producers would find it a serious hardship to wait for their money until this final sale was made. However, most producers sell, not to the ultimate consumer, but to middlemen and, as a result, they are paid for their merchandise long in advance of the final sale. 145

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

2. Grading, Sorting and Packing.-Many commodities must be classified and separated according to size, quality, model, etc. 

The performance of this important task is one of the functions of the retailer. For example, when a man enters a hat store and asks for a gray fedora, size 712, he expects the retailer to show him such a hat within a few moments. This adds quantitative utility to the goods (page 24). 

3. Storage. Many commodities, particularly agricultural products, are seasonal in their production. The supply for the entire year is sent to the market at one time, and it must be 

THE SERVICES OF THE MIDDLEMAN II 

CONSUMER MIDDLEMAN CREDIT PACKING STORAGE GRADING SORTING SELLING PRODUCER 

Is it possible to "eliminate the middleman''? cared for until it is gradually used up by the ultimate consumers. Middlemen perform this function of storage until the final distribution takes place. This adds time utility to the goods (page 23). 

4. Selling. The middleman sells the goods which the factories and workshops of the nation produce. This means that the middleman finds the consumer and effects the exchange of the product for money. Advertising is a part of this selling activity. This adds ownership utility to the goods (page 23).

5. Transportation.-Middlemen serve the public by transporting goods from the producers to the ultimate consumers. 

For example. a great store like R. H. Macy of New York assembles merchandise from all over the world from which its customers may make a selection. After the goods have been sold, they are delivered to the homes of the purchasers. This adds place utility to the goods (page 23). 

Evaluation of the Middleman.-In spite of these valuable activities, some people claim that the charges which the middlemen make are out of all proportion to the services they perform. 

For example, it has been pointed out that one mutton chop served in the dining room of a New York hotel has at times cost more than a Western rancher received for an entire sheep. 

Part of this tremendous increase in cost went to the various middlemen who handled the sheep before it reached the hotel kitchen. Therefore, it is maintained, the numbers of the middlemen and the extent of their activities should be sharply decreased. 

At the present time, there appears to be a trend toward a reduction in the numbers of middlemen. Mail-order houses buy directly from producers and sell directly to consumers through the mail, thus eliminating the use of wholesalers and of stores. 

Department stores and chain stores, which usually buy goods in great quantities, are often able to dispense with the services of wholesalers and buy directly from producers. In addition, coöperatives (page 114), which may become far more highly developed in the future, lessen the numbers of the middlemen. 

The elimination of middlemen in these instances may result in savings which should be passed on to the consumer. 

The Chain Store Problem.-We indicated in a previous chapter (page 106) that retailing is one field in which the individual entrepreneur still survives in large numbers. In recent years, however, there has been a great development of the chain store system, that is, a number of retail establishments owned and operated by a single corporation. Some chain store systems with which we are all familiar are the A. & P. Stores, the Woolworth 5 and 10 Cent Stores, and the United Cigar Stores. The chart on page 148 will give you some idea of the importance of the chain stores in 1932 in the fields in which they have aitained their greatest development.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS Type of Store CHAIN STORES Chain Percentage of Total Number of Stores in Country Chain Percentage of Total Sales in Country Food

What accounts for the rapid growth of the chain store? The principal reason is that chain store organizations can buy their goods in bulk, often directly from the producer. This results in savings which often enable the chains to undersell the single entrepreneur. Another reason is that the chains are expertly managed and often carry a greater variety of goods and display them more attractively than do the small, independent retailers. 

On the other hand, there are a considerable number of people who do not think that the growth of the chains is socially desirable. The following criticisms are made. (1) The chain store eliminates the small independent business man and replaces him with the salaried employee of a corporation. This is opposed to the traditional American individualism and tends to produce a greater concentration of wealth. (2) The chain stores do not bear an equitable share of local taxation. (3) In small towns and cities, complaints are made that the chain stores do not employ local labor and that they do not patronize local bankers and other local business men.

For these reasons, there is some vigorous opposition to chain stores and a number of states have passed laws intended to handicap the chains and to benefit the small independent merchants.

Installment Buying.-Another comparatively recent development in retailing is the great growth of a form of consumer credit known as installment buying. This means that the consumer is given a commodity in exchange for a partial payment

and a promise to pay the remainder in the future. The amounts and the dates of the payments are specified in the terms of the sale.

The advantage of installment buying is obvious. A man with a moderate income and with no savings can acquire such a commodity as an automobile or an electric refrigerator and can enjoy the use of it while he is paying for it. However, there are certain dangers inherent in widespread installment buying. 

People find it easy to buy when they have to make only a small cash payment. For a limited period, therefore, business may be greatly stimulated by installment buying. Business men may increase their production in response to what appears to be a permanent expansion of demand. However, these purchases have mortgaged the future incomes of the consumers. 

In the weeks to come, they will find it necessary to restrict their buying in order to pay the installments as they fall due. As a result, the total demand for goods ultimately will fall and business will suffer.

When great numbers of people are carrying one or more installment accounts, these conditions may cause serious economic disturbances. It is widely believed that installment buying in the late 1920's had gone further than was desirable. 

ORGANIZED MARKETS 

Nature of an Organized Market. A market, as our definition indicates, exists whenever and wherever there are opportunities to buy and sell goods. If you should call a friend on the telephone and arrange to buy some radishes which he has raised in his garden, such an arrangement would be considered a market.

In certain cases, however, it has been found desirable to organize special facilities for trading, and organized markets have been created. These organized markets, such as the New 

York Stock Exchange and the Chicago Board of Trade, are associations of middlemen who buy and sell particular commodities. 

The members of the New York Stock Exchange for example, deal in the stocks and bonds of corporations, and the members of the Chicago Board of Trade handle agricultural products, such as wheat, corn and oats. In addition such capital goods

time. The current prices of these securities are printed in the daily newspapers and the investor can tell at a glance how much he can get for his stocks and bonds or how much he will have to pay for them. The middlemen who carry out this buying and selling for investors are known as brokers. They are paid commissions for their services.

In this manner, the brokers and the exchanges perform important functions in our economic life. They aid the flotation (sale) of securities. They make long-term capital investments liquid, that is, readily convertible into cash. They facilitate the investment of funds by people who have accumulated savings. 

They furnish a market for shifts in investments, that is, they make it possible for individuals or groups of individuals to shift their capital funds from one enterprise to another enterprise which they consider more promising. They aid the transfer of liquid funds to countries and regions where they are needed. 

In short, the exchanges greatly facilitate the transfer of capital funds and capital goods which is absolutely essential to the functioning of our economic system.

In the public eye, however, these legitimate and constructive activities of the organized markets are often overshadowed by a more spectacular activity known as speculation. 

Speculation.—A good part of the transactions which take place in the organized markets are made because the investor hopes that he will be able to profit from a rise or fall in the price of the commodity. As we have seen, storekeepers also buy in the hope of selling for a higher price. However, storekeepers add place utility, time utility, ownership utility and quantitative utility to the goods they handle. This gives their activities a positive social value. Speculation, on the other hand, is buying to sell again without creating any added utility. 

1. Buying "Long."-This is the practice of buying in the hope that the price of the commodity will rise in the future. If this happens, the speculator will be able to get more money for the commodity than he paid for it. If the price falls, however, the speculator will lose money. 

2. Selling "Short."-This is a speculative transaction in which the investor profits from an anticipated fall in the price of the commodity concerned. Following is a typical example.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

A speculator authorizes his broker to borrow a number of shares of stock from another broker. This borrowed stock is sold for $1000. The broker from whom the stock was borrowed, will probably require that this $1000 be left with him as security. Suppose that within a period of several weeks the market price for the stock drops to $750. The speculator can repurchase the stock for this amount, return it to the broker from whom it was borrowed, and receive the $1000 which was left as security. In this way, he has gained a profit of $250. (Both brokers who are concerned in this transaction would be paid commissions, so that the profit would actually be less than $250.)

On the other hand, if the price of the stock in question had risen above $1000, the speculator would have had to "cover," that is, repurchase it at the higher price and would have lost money. Hedging. Consider the case of a miller who must buy wheat to convert it into flour. If the price of this wheat drops after he has bought it, he will lose money, for the prices of wheat and flour rise and fall together. Thus, his high-priced wheat will have to be sold in the form of low-priced flour. To protect himself against a drop in prices between the time at which he bought the wheat and the time at which he will sell the flour, the miller engages in a hedging transaction. Hedging refers to a simultaneous purchase and sale of a commodity for the purpose of avoiding losses resulting from changes in the price level. The following illustration gives some idea of the nature of hedging as practiced by a miller. 

On August 1, the miller buys wheat at 97¢ a bushel to be made into flour, At the same time he sells the same amount of wheat and guarantees to deliver it to the buyer on the first day of September at 98¢ a bushel.† On September 1, his flour, Speculators who anticipate a rise in the price of securities are known as "bulls"; speculators who sell "short" in the hope of a fall in prices are called "bears."

The price paid for a commodity which is to be delivered at some time in the future is called the "future" price. In the case of wheat, the "future" price is generally about one cent a month higher than the "spot" price (the price charged if the wheat is to be delivered immediately). 

This one cent covers the cost of storing the "future" wheat until it is delivered.

which was made from the wheat, is ready for sale, but he finds that the best price he can get for the flour is only 90¢ a bushel. 

It would seem that he must take a loss due to the drop in prices but notice how the hedging transaction of August 1 helps him. 

He has guaranteed to deliver wheat on September 1, but having no wheat he must buy it to make his promised delivery. He goes into the wheat market and buys it at 88¢ a bushel and delivers it. His purchases amounted to $1.85 a bushel; his sales to $1.88 (wheat and flour). In spite of the fall in prices, the sales exceed the purchases by three cents for each bushel of wheat. One cent of this amount pays for storage and the other two cents provide for the cost of producing the flour and also for the miller's profit. Thus, the miller has avoided a serious loss.

On the other hand, if the price of wheat had risen during the month of August, the miller would have gained on his "spot" purchase but would have lost on his "future" purchase. In either case, he is protected against a great loss. Thus hedging is a sort of commercial insurance, as contrasted to buy. ing "long" and selling "short," which are purely speculative activities. 

Buying "on Margin" and Call Money.-Investors who speculate on the exchanges frequently do not have enough money to make outright purchases of the articles in which they are speculating. In this case, they supply a part of the cash needed (for example, forty per cent) and their brokers borrow the rest of the money for them. This practice is known as buying "on margin. 

Usually, these speculative transactions are completed within several days and the money which is borrowed is wanted only for that period. How does it happen that banks have funds available which they are willing to lend to brokers for such short periods of time?

It has been the custom for banks from all over the country to keep a part of their funds on deposit in the banks of several large cities, particularly New York City. The New York banks have been faced with the problem of investing these funds in such a way that cash could be raised immediately, in case the out-of-town banks wished to withdraw their deposits.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Therefore, the practice has arisen of loaning these funds to stock exchange brokers, who provide collateral for the loans in the form of stocks and bonds. The funds used for this purpose are known as call money and the loans themselves are referred to as call loans. As a general rule, these loans are made for one day or are subject to call (can be terminated) on any day after they are made.

Dangers of Speculation.-Speculation has often resulted in great losses to investors. For example, during the great “bull” market of the late 1920's, thousands of people in moderate circumstances gambled in Wall Street in the hopes of "earning" some of the enormous profits which, it seemed, could be had for the asking. Many of them bought their securities "on margin." 

In this way, they were able to buy and sell greater amounts of stock than if they had been required to pay in full for the securities. When the "crash" (the great fall in security values) came in October, 1929, many of these people lost their life's savings. The securities which they had bought on margin declined in value, the banks demanded repayment of the call loans and the investors were "sold out" at prices which were often insufficient to cover even the amount of the loan. 

Moreover, the frenzied speculation had a most unhealthy effect on general business conditions. The great "boom" of the 

1920's, in which security prices were inflated far above their true value, was one of the leading factors in bringing on the depression which swept over the country in 1929. The prices at which securities were bought and sold bore little or no relation to the physical assets of the corporations which had issued them or to the ability of those corporations to produce wealth and profits over any extended period of time. 

Securities and Exchange Commission.—In an effort to prevent a recurrence of such a blind and destructive speculative frenzy, the Roosevelt Administration established the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) in 1934. This Commission has the power to regulate practically everything concerned with the buying and selling of stocks. Some of the outstanding provisions of the laws which the Commission is to enforce are: 

1. All stockbrokers, stock exchanges, and firms issuing securities to be sold in interstate commerce must register with the Commission.

2. There is to be adequate publicity relating to the organization and the financial status of firms issuing such securities. 

As a result of this, investors will be able to base their purchases of stocks and bonds on intelligent judgments, rather than on rumors and publicity. 

3. The Commission has the power to control all artificial manipulations of market prices. This means that powerful groups (pools) of "bulls" and "bears" will not be able to force prices up and down, as their interests require. 

GET-RICH-QUICK PANIC AND DEPRESSION SEC THE SEC INTERVENES SPECULATOR AMERICAN BUSINESS 

How may the S.E.C. help to prevent another speculative boom? 

4. Trading on margin is to be strictly regulated. The Stock

Exchange Act of 1934 gave the Federal Reserve Board power to set the minimum proportion of cash a buyer of securities must furnish. Early in 1936, this was set at 55%; in October, 1937, it was cut to 40%. At the same time, the Board adopted the first Federal margin requirements on "short" sales (50%).

These latter moves, it was believed, were intended to counteract the severe slump in stock prices which occurred late in 1937. 

The laws establishing the SEC contain few hard and fast rules,

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

but the Commission has ample powers to regulate the great organized markets in the interests of the nation as a whole. 

Summary.-Producers sell their goods to middlemen, who bring these goods into the hands of consumers. 

In certain cases, middlemen find it advisable to establish great organized markets, which are so designed and administered that they provide a continuous market for certain commodities.

CHAPTER XIII  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning and functions of money. 

2. To discuss the effects of changes in the value of money. 

3. To describe the various types of money standards. 

4. To explain the meaning and functions of credit. 

Need for Money.-Jones has plenty of sugar and Smith has more tea than he can use.

The two men might make a direct exchange of sugar for tea. Such a direct exchange of goods is known as barter. Barter has been much used in the past, and is used, even today, by primitive peoples. 

However in our complex modern society, where so many thousands of articles are exchanged, barter cannot be used extensively. Suppose that Jones wished to exchange his sugar for an automobile. An automobile is worth many thousands of pounds of sugar. Therefore, Jones would have to find a man with an automobile for sale who had some use for a great quantity of sugar. It is not likely that he would find such a man. 

Suppose, however, that there is a third commodity, of which almost everyone possesses at least a small quantity, which is accepted universally in exchange for all goods and services. 

Let us see how this will help Jones get that automobile. 

Jones will offer to exchange his sugar for this third commodity at a certain rate-so much sugar for so much of the third commodity. People who want sugar, in small or in large quantities, will come to Jones and make the exchange. When Jones has accumulated enough of the third commodity, he will be able to exchange it for the automobile. The man who sells the automobile, can use the third commodity to acquire as much sugar as he needs, and, in addition, the many other commodities and services which he needs. 158

This transaction between Jones and the automobile owner is not a product of our imagination. It is essentially the same as the thousands and thousands of transactions which take place in our country every day. And, as you have probably already guessed, the "third commodity" which is used to effect all these exchanges is known as money.

MONEY-THE MEDIUM OF EXCHANGE 

FARM 

PRODUCTS INDUSTRY 

AGRICULTURE INDUSTRIAL PRODUCTS 

Is exchange possible without the use of money?

Definition. Money is any article which is used freely as a medium of exchange and which is accepted without reference to the credit of the person who offers it. 

Functions of Money. Thus, we see that money is the instrument by means of which we can exchange goods and services freely and conveniently. We say, then, that the primary purpose of money is to serve as a medium of exchange. As an outgrowth of this primary function, money has come to assume several other functions: 

1. Money appears in the form of units, such as the American dollar or the French franc. Because almost everything is bought and sold for money, we find it convenient to express the value of these things in terms of the number of units for which

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

they are exchanged. We say that an automobile cost $1,000, or that sugar costs $.05 a pound. In this way we establish a certain value for automobiles and sugar which can be compared directly with the values of all other commodities, which are also expressed in terms of money units. Thus we say that money serves as a standard of value. 

2. If goods are bought on a credit basis or if funds are borrowed, the payment is deferred to some time in the future. 

In all such cases, however, arrangements have been made that provide for the payment of so many dollars and cents at a specific time. The person who has loaned the money, or sold the goods on time payments, knows how much he will receive in payment of the debt. Thus money serves as a standard of deferred payment. 

3. We have said that money is a medium of exchange by means of which we can acquire the things that we want, things which have value to us. Now, suppose that over a period of years you have been able to store up $10,000 in a bank. You have really stored up not so many slips of green paper, but an instrument by which at some time in the future you will be able to acquire valuable things, such as an automobile, clothing, and a college education. By storing up money you have really stored up these valuable things for yourself. Money is a storehouse of value.

Effect of Price Changes on Functions of Money. Thus we have summarized the four principal functions which money serves in the modern world: a medium of exchange, a standard of value, a standard of deferred payment and a storehouse of value. It can be seen from this that if money performed its job perfectly it would be a commodity which people were willing to accept freely in exchange for all other commodities, at rates or prices which, on the whole, did not vary greatly from time to time. If people are not willing to accept money in exchange for other commodities, it does not fulfill its function of a medium of exchange. On the other hand, if the rates at which people exchange money for goods (that is, the prices we pay for goods) vary greatly from time to time, money fails at least partially to fulfill its functions of a standard of value, a stand-

ard of deferred payment, or a storehouse of value. Let us see why this is so. 

1. Effect of Rising Prices.-Suppose that prices as a whole are rising rapidly. This means that we can get less for a dollar, or that the value of money is going down. How does this affect people who have stored their savings in a bank or loaned it out to the government or to a corporation? Obviously their bank accounts or investments are worth less than formerly. 

When they withdraw their savings from the bank, they can buy less with these savings than they could have bought when they first deposited their savings. Money, in this case, has failed to function perfectly as a storehouse of value. When the government redeems its bonds (that is, repays the investors the money which they have loaned to it) the money handed back is worth less than the money which the investor gave the government. 

Money, in this case, has failed as a perfect standard of deferred payment.

PRICES WAGES LAG BEHIND RISING PRICES 

111 

WAGES 

What effect would inflation have on the great masses of wage earners?

In the same way, people who are living on a fixed income will suffer from a rising price level. For example, the depositors and investors in government bonds who draw a fixed amount of interest will find that they can buy less with their incomes. 

The same is true of people who draw fixed salaries, such as teachers, postmen, and employees of private enterprises who are working under contract. Even when wages are not fixed, as in the cases above, a rising price level generally is harmful. 

This is so because when prices go up, wages will probably follow eventually, but they tend to follow more slowly, that is, to lag behind. Wage earners, on the whole, suffer from a rising price level.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

On the other hand, middlemen and bankers who have accumulated stocks of goods of various sorts derive certain immediate benefits from a rising price level. The value of their goods, in terms of money, is increased. Similarly, manufacturers with stores of raw materials also benefit, for they can turn their raw materials into finished products and sell them at a comparatively high price. However, a sharply rising price level may cause certain disturbances in the economic structure which ultimately will more than outweigh the temporary ad vantages (Chapter XXIV). 

2. Effect of Falling Prices.-On the other hand, a falling price level means that the value of money is rising, since a dollar can buy more goods than it could previously. Therefore, this has effects exactly opposite to those of a rising price level. 

Debtors, that is, individuals and organizations who owe money, are harmed since they must pay back to their creditors dollars which are more valuable than the ones which they borrowed. 

People who draw interest from bonds and bank accounts and people who live on fixed salaries benefit because the fixed number of dollars which constitute their incomes have greater purchasing power when prices fall. Business men with accumulated stocks of goods suffer.

In either case, whether the price level rises or falls, money is not performing perfectly the functions which we have described above. Of course, slight variations in the price level, which occur almost continually, are not very serious. However, a considerable rise or fall in prices works great hardships on various groups and is a major disturbing force in our economic life.

Index Numbers.-Index numbers provide a method of determining and expressing general variations in the price level. 

An index number of prices is generally stated as a percentage which gives the relationship between prices at some particular time and prices in a base year, which is used as the standard of comparison. The prices in the base year are regarded as 100%. 

Therefore, if prices in the year which is being compared with the base year are higher, the index number will be over 100%. 

If prices are lower, the index number will be less than 100%. 

In computing an index number, we select a representative list of commodities, such as food, clothing, steel, fuel, etc. We

then determine the average prices for which these commodities were selling in the base year, which, as stated, we consider 100%. Then we determine the prices of the various commodities in the year which we wish to compare with the base year. 

We compare the prices in the two years for each of the commodities involved, and this gives a price ratio for each commodity. The average of all the price ratios for one year is considered a general index number. The following illustration will make this clear.

INDEX NUMBER 

Price in

Base Year 

(100%) 

Price in year 19Price Ratio for Commodity 

Commodity A $1.50 $2.25 150 

Commodity B .90 1.17 130 

Commodity C 1.00 1.20 120 

Commodity D 3.50 3.50 100 4)500

General Index 

Thus we see that the index number for prices in the year 19 (as compared with the base year) is 125. This means that prices, on the average, are 25% higher in the year 19than they were in the base year. 

It has been computed that the index number for 1934, as compared with the base year of 1926, was 85.* This means that in 1934 prices were 85% as high as they were in 1926. If you are interested in determining how much more you could get for your money in 1934 than in 1926, divide 100% by 85% and multiply the answer by 100, thus: 100% 85% =1.17 

1.17 X 100% = 117% *In the computation of this figure, many more commodities were used than we indicated in our simplified illustration. In addition, the method of computation was more complicated than that used in the illustration. 

The basic principle, however, is the same.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

This indicates that your dollar in 1934 was worth 117% as much as in 1926. We can express this in terms of purchasing power by saying that a dollar in 1926 bought 100 cents' worth of goods, but a dollar in 1934 bought 117 cents' worth of the same goods.

Index numbers are used to measure not only price changes but also changes in wages, employment, bank check clearings, volume of production, and many other important economic conditions.

Qualities of a Good Money.-From all this discussion we begin to get some idea of the tremendous importance of this tool we call money. Now, in order to do its job well, any tool must possess certain definite and peculiar qualities. Money is no exception. Thousands of years of experience have taught men that a good money possesses the following qualities. 

1. It is generally the case that the stuff of which money is made, or on which its value is based, is useful for other purposes. Gold and silver, for example, are used for jewelry. (It is theoretically possible to have a good monetary system which is not based on a valuable commodity, but such a system presents serious practical difficulties which we shall consider later.) 

2. Money is easy to carry and handle. Our paper money and coins possess this quality. 

3. Money is either extremely durable or quickly and cheaply replaceable. Our metal coins last for a long time. Our paper bills, on the other hand, are not very durable but when they wear out it costs comparatively little for the government to replace them. 

4. All the units of a monetary system are uniform. This does not necessarily mean that all the units will look alike, but that they will have equal buying power. We can have a dollar in several different forms (for example, a silver dollar and a paper dollar), but each of these has the same buying power and acceptability. 

5. The units of the monetary system are easily divisible into a number of smaller units. For example, our monetary system provides an adequate supply of small change (pennies, nickels, dimes, etc) to facilitate buying and selling.

6. Money is easy to recognize. For example, there is very little danger that any one will confuse a five dollar bill with a cigar store coupon or a worthless scrap of paper. In addition, our money is very difficult to imitate and we have severe laws to punish counterfeiters, who attempt to make such imitations. 

7. The supply of money is sufficiently elastic to adapt itself to changes in economic conditions. This means that the supply of money will expand and contract as the need for money (to be used in business) increases and diminishes. However, it is extremely undesirable that the supply increase or diminish beyond the needs of the people. 

Monetary Systems.-What particular devices or methods have men used to secure the qualities which have been described above? This question can be best answered by examining the principal monetary standards or systems which have appeared in the modern world. These are the gold standard, the silver standard, the inconvertible paper standard, and bimetallism. 

1. The Gold Standard.-A country is said to be on the gold standard when the following conditions prevail. (a) The monetary unit is defined as a particular quantity of gold. For example, prior to March, 1933, the dollar was defined as 25.8 grains of gold, 10 fine. (b) Gold is freely coined at this fixed price. If you brought 25.8 grains of gold to the United

States Treasury prior to March, 1933, the Government would give you a dollar for it. (c) All other money in the country is maintained at equal value with gold and is convertible into gold upon request. (d) Gold money is full legal tender within the country. This means that all debts, public and private, can be settled in gold. (e) Gold can be exported and imported freely and used for any legitimate industrial purpose. 

In most modern countries which have used the gold standard, there was actually little gold in general circulation. For example, our country was on the gold standard prior to March, 1933, * If a government makes a charge to cover the expense of coining the metal, the charge is known as brassage. If the government charges more than enough to cover the cost of the coining (in order to make a profit) it is known as seignorage. The United States Government has never made seignorage charges, but at various times has levied a small brassage charge.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

yet one rarely saw a gold coin. This permitted more effective utilization of our gold reserves and saved the expense of coining the metal.

All the important countries of the world used the gold standard until the difficulties of the period following the World War caused most of them to abandon it. Economists are somewhat divided in opinion as to the advantages and disadvantages of the gold standard. One important advantage which it did have, however, is that, whether justifiably or not, a great many people had confidence in it. This attitude, which was widespread and deep-rooted, was of the greatest importance, for no money can work well as a medium of exchange unless the people who use it have perfect confidence in it. 

2. The Silver Standard.-The silver standard would be defined in the same way as the gold standard, with the word "silver" substituted for "gold." There are no countries in the world today which employ a full silver standard, but China,

Mexico, and Ethiopia use modified versions of it. In all these countries, silver is the standard of value but there is no free movement or coinage of the metal. 

3. The Inconvertible Paper Standard.-A country is said to employ this standard when (a) the monetary unit and standard of value is a paper unit; (b) the paper unit is full legal tender; and (c) the other money of the country is convertible into the standard paper money. In general, a paper standard has been used only in emergencies. Its great weakness is that it does not provide a limitation on the amount of money that can be issued. 

Under the gold standard, for example, the issuance of money is limited by the supply of gold. Under the inconvertible paper standard, however, there is no such check and a government which is in desperate circumstances may try to solve its financial problems by issuing great quantities of "printing press" money. 

Such an expansion of the supply of money, which will result in sharply rising prices, is known as inflation. Inflation has disastrous results on the economic life of a nation (page 177). 

Paper money, as described above, which is not based on any valuable commodity, such as gold, but merely on the credit of the government that issues it, is known as fiat money. The 

United States Government issued a limited amount of fiat money

("greenbacks") during the Civil War. Later, a gold backing was provided for these notes. 

4. Bimetallism.-At various times in the past, the United 

States, as well as other nations, have been on a bimetallic standard. Under this standard, the dollar (or whatever the monetary unit may be) is defined as a definite amount of either gold or silver. There is free coinage of both metals and an exchange ratio is set up between them. This means that their relative value is established by law. For example, when our national monetary system was established in 1792, the exchange ratio was set at 15 to 1; that is, 15 ounces of silver were worth one ounce of gold.

However, a difficulty appeared in this instance, as it has on many other occasions when bimetallism has been tried. Gold and silver both have a price on the open market, which varies in response to supply and demand. Soon after Congress established the ratio of 15 to 1, it was found that the ratio of the market values of gold and silver was 15½ to 1. In other words, the government was offering more gold for silver than was being offered in the open market. It was overvaluing silver.

People soon found that it was into silver at the goldsmiths' 

For example, suppose that a 

What was the result of this? more profitable to turn their gold than it was to sell it at the mint. man had 1 ounce of gold which he wished to convert into the largest possible number of dollars. He knew the government would give him 15 ounces of silver for it. However, at the goldsmith's he could sell his 1 ounce for 15½ ounces of silver, which was the market price; 15½ ounces of silver were worth more at the mint than 1 ounce of gold, for the mint ratio was 15 ounces of silver to 1 ounce of gold. As a result of this, comparatively little gold came into the mint. Moreover, people found it profitable to melt their gold coins, sell them to the goldsmith for silver, and then convert this silver into dollars at the treasury. Upon various other occasions when the United States attempted to establish a bimetallic standard, essentially the same thing happened. The overvalued metal tended to drive the undervalued metal out of circulation.

Gresham's Law. The results of bimetallism, as explained above, are an example of an important generalization about

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

money known as Gresham's Law. This law states that when there are several different types of money in circulation, all of which have the same legal status and value but different values as commodities, the cheaper money will tend to drive the dearer money out of circulation.

Gresham's Law holds true not only in the case of a bimetallic standard, but also when a great deal of unsecured and inconvertible paper money is issued. In this case all metallic money tends to disappear, because people will hoard what gold and silver they have and use the paper money, in which they have little confidence, to make their purchases. This is what happened in Germany after the Great War, when the government issued huge amounts of "printing press" money. In a short time almost all gold and silver and sound foreign money disappeared from circulation.

We can sum up Gresham's Law by saying that bad money tends to drive good money out of circulation. 

Monetary Standard of the United States Today.—In regard to international payments, the United States is today on a gold bullion standard. One dollar is defined as 15521 grains of gold, 10 fine.* Another way of expressing this is that one ounce of gold is worth $35. Americans can purchase gold at this price in order to ship it abroad in payment of foreign trade balances. (Such purchases and shipments of gold are subject to stringent regulation by the government.) The value of the dollar in terms of foreign currencies is determined, to some extent, by the abovementioned legal ratio between gold and the dollar.

As far as our internal affairs are concerned, however, the United States is now on what is virtually a paper standard. The great bulk of the money in circulation is paper money which is not convertible into gold. Moreover, no citizen is allowed to hold gold bullion, but must sell it to the government. 

This monetary system was developed in an attempt to with- * Previous to March, 1933, when the United States was on the full gold standard, the dollar was defined as 25.8 grains of gold. In January, 1934, the value of the dollar was fixed at 15 5/21 grains of gold, a reduction of about 41%. This is what is meant by the expression, which is sometimes used, that we now have a "59¢ dollar." The President was given the power to devalue the dollar further to 50% of its pre-1933 gold content. 

In 1937, this power was extended until 1939, and in 1939 until 1941.

stand the tremendous financial strains of the long depression period. It is almost universally admitted that it is not a thoroughly satisfactory system and that the future will witness great changes in our monetary policies. It remains to be seen whether this will involve a return to the pre-depression gold standard or whether we will attempt to develop a new standard, better suited to modern conditions.

Money Used in the United States. In the United States today, we use nine different forms of money. 

1. Gold Certificates.-This is a form of paper money which is backed, dollar for dollar, by gold deposited in the United

States Treasury. When the United States went off the gold standard this money was withdrawn from general circulation, and at present is held only by the Federal Reserve Banks. 

2. Silver Dollars.—A comparatively small number of dollars made out of silver circulate in the United States. The silver in these coins is worth less than one dollar, but people are willing to accept them at their face value because they have confidence in the credit of the United States Government. Coins of this type, whose value as bullion is less than their face value, are known as token money. 

3. Silver Certificates.-This form of paper money is backed by silver on deposit in the United States Treasury. 

4. United States Notes.-This is a comparatively rare form of paper money ("greenbacks") which is backed, although not dollar for dollar, by gold on deposit in the United States Treasury. 

5. Subsidiary Coins.-This group includes dimes, quarters, and half dollars. The metal of which these coins are made is worth less than their face value, so that they are a form of token money. 

6. Minor Coins.-This name refers to pennies and nickels. 

These are also a form of token money. 

7. Federal Reserve Notes.-This is a form of paper money issued through the Federal Reserve Banks. The notes are backed by gold certificates to at least forty per cent of their value. The remaining sixty per cent is covered by government securities or by short-term notes which business men issue when they borrow money from a bank. This is the most important single form of

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS (Federal Reserve money in use in the United States today. 

Notes will be considered more fully in Chapter XV.) 

All these forms of money are full legal tender. However, as we have seen, none of this currency is now redeemable in gold. 

Money Retired from Circulation.-Within the last few years, the Government has decided to retire two forms of money from eirculation: (1) a comparatively rare type of currency known as 

Federal Reserve Bank Notes; and (2) the far more important 

National Bank Notes, which were issued by national banks, and were backed by Government bonds and by a five per cent redemption fund of lawful money. Of course, it will require some years to remove all these notes from circulation. 

CREDIT 

Purpose of Credit.-In our roundabout method of produc tion, a considerable period of time elapses between the purchase of raw materials and the sale of the finished product. In the making of furniture, about thirty days are required to bring a piece through the processes of finishing (staining, varnishing, rubbing, etc.). Now, the entrepreneur who is manufacturing this furniture may not have enough cash to buy his raw materials and to pay all his expenses during the thirty-day period of manufacture. However, he knows that when he sells the finished product he will have enough money to meet all these expenses. 

What will the entrepreneur do in this case? He will try to get the raw materials in exchange for a promise to pay for them after he sells the finished furniture. Furthermore he will try to borrow cash with which to pay his labor and incidental expenses. 

This, too, he will promise to repay after he sells the furniture. 

In short, the entrepreneur will try to get something in exchange for a promise to repay in the future. He will try to get credit. 

Definition.-Credit is a medium of exchange by means of which commodities, services, or money are provided in the present in return for a promise to pay in the future. 

We can see from this that credit is a substitute for money. 

Our furniture entrepreneur, in the illustration used above, had no money. Yet by use of credit he was able to buy raw materials and pay his expenses exactly as though he possessed the necessary amount of cash.

We may, in general, distinguish two types of credit, personal credit, and bank credit.

Personal Credit.-Personal credit is extended when an individual or an organization obtains goods or services in direct exchange for a promise to pay in the future. If you have a charge account at a department store you are receiving personal credit. You take the merchandise now, and you give nothing but your promise to pay at some future date. In many cases business firms take merchandise and do not pay for it immediately. This is also personal credit. Another instance may be 

BRIDGES TIME BETWEEN CREDIT PRODUCTION CONSUMPTION WOOLEN MILL CLOTHING FACTORY 

AND

RETAIL 

CLOTHING STORE 

CRE IT 194 JUNE 1 

BOND ISSUE BANK CREDIT CHARGE ACCOUNT 

Did Mark Overton make extensive use of credit in his early frontier days? Explain. seen in the case of the laborer who is paid once a month. He is extending credit to his employer, for during the entire month he is giving his services, and he receives only a promise to pay. 

Bank Credit. In almost all cases the ability to obtain personal credit is very limited. The entrepreneur, whom we have considered above, who wants to get the raw materials for his furniture on credit, may be an honest and capable man. However, the business house which is selling the raw materials may

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

not know him personally. Besides this, their own financial position probably requires that they limit the amount of the credit which they extend. Because of these limitations of personal credit, an institution known as bank credit has arisen. 

In the first place, the bank will use its own credit to obtain funds. Depositors give money to the banker, and receive only his promise to repay upon request. Then the bank is ready to do its business, which is to extend credit to business men. In most cases the bank does this by giving the borrower an account at the bank against which he can write checks. This means simply that the borrower is permitted to order the bank to pay sums of money to other people. (The paper on which the borrower records this order is the check.) 

Let us suppose that our furniture manufacturer has obtained bank credit. He will pay for his raw materials by writing checks on his bank. The check itself is an instrument of credit, for it is not money, but merely an order to pay money. However, the firm which supplies the raw materials is willing to accept it, for it has confidence in the bank against which the check is drawn. Thus we see that by the issuance of bank credit, the credit of the bank has been substituted for the credit of the individual.

Instruments of Bank Credit.-In the issuance of bank credit a number of instruments may be involved. We will examine the more important of these. 

1. Promissory Note.-This is by far the most common of the various instruments which are used in this country in the issuance of bank credit.* It is a written promise which the borrower gives the bank to repay the amount involved on the date arranged. In most cases the bank will ask the borrower to get another business man to act as endorser. The endorser signs the note, and is legally responsible for the debt if the borrower does not pay it when it falls due. If the borrower cannot provide an endorser, the bank will probably ask him to furnish some property, such as good securities, which can be held as collateral and sold if he fails to repay. *In all the cases of bank credit the bank charges a fee (interest) out of which it pays its expenses and derives its profits.

2. Bill of Exchange.-In many cases, a man who has sold goods to another business man cannot collect his money for some time. (Thirty days is a common interval.) The seller may find it necessary or desirable to have his money immediately. The bill of exchange is an instrument by means of which he can get his money before the buyer pays him.

Let us suppose that you have sold me goods worth $5000 for which I agree to pay in thirty days. You will make out a document in which you order me to pay this amount at the date arranged. This order to pay is the bill of exchange. 

Now, how can you use this bill to get your money before the thirty days have elapsed? You can sell it to a bank, which will pay you the $5,000 for it, minus, of course, its interest charges. $85.00

The bank is willing to pay you the money for the bill because they know that at the end of the thirty days they will be able to present the bill to me and demand that I pay it. 

Actually, there are several variations of the bill of exchange. 

In many cases the man on whom the bill is drawn (that is, the man who is ordered to pay), will be asked to accept the bill after it has been made out. This means simply that the debtor acknowledges that he owes the money. In token of this he writes "accepted" across the face of the bill, and signs his name. In this case the bill is sometimes called a trade acceptance. 

In some instances the bill of exchange is not bought by a bank.

It may be bought by another business man who owes money to the individual on whom the bill is drawn. In such a

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

case this other business man will buy the note because he can use it to discharge his debt. We might represent the situation in this instance by the following diagram:

In this case you may word the bill in such a way that I will be ordered to pay the third party, rather than yourself. In either case, however, the third party can use the bill to discharge his debt. (Note that in this transaction the use of a bank is not involved, so that it is really not bank credit, but a type of personal credit.) 

3. Check. The nature of the check has been indicated above. 

It is a document in which a depositor orders his bank to pay money to another person or to himself. 

4. Bank Draft.—A bank draft is a check drawn by one bank on another bank in which the first bank has funds deposited. 

Suppose that A in New York owes a certain sum to B in Los Angeles. He can effect payment by going to a New York bank and buying a draft which will order a Los Angeles bank, in which the New York bank has funds deposited, to pay the sum involved to B. The draft is sent to B, who cashes it at the bank. 

Bonds. A bond (see Chapter IX, Business Organization) 

is an instrument for obtaining long-term credit. If a corporation wishes to construct new buildings, for example, and does not have the necessary funds, it can borrow this money from indi-

viduals, from banks, and from other corporations. In evidence of this indebtedness it will issue bonds, which are promises to repay the principal of the loan and a stipulated annual interest. The corporation may pledge some of its assets (for example, its real estate) as security (mortgage bonds). The term of credit may run for a period of twenty years or more. In addition to private enterprises, governments and the agencies of governments issue bonds, which may likewise be bought by individuals, banks, corporations, and other governments.

Credit and the Monetary System.-Credit in itself is not money. You will recall our definition that money is "accepted freely in exchange for goods and services." In contrast to this, acceptance of a promise to repay depends on our confidence in Pay to the order of Wilbur Jones and Company 00
