Nevertheless, credit does do some of the work of money because it functions as a medium of exchange. Individuals who have no money are enabled to carry on business activities exactly as though they possessed the money. Because of this, credit does. exert a very important influence on our monetary system. When we consider the virtues and defects of that system, and possible ways of improving it, we must remember that about 90 per cent of the business transactions which take place are effected not by the actual transfer of money, but by the use of the instruments of credit which have just been described.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

The Quantity Theory of Money. We have seen above that the value of a unit of currency varies, that is, that prices go up and down. What is the cause of such variations in the value of money? One of the attempts to explain this is the quantity theory of money.

This theory states that, other things being equal, the value of a unit of the accepted medium of exchange decreases when the supply of that medium increases, and that it increases when the 

ENTREPRENEUR 

1099 

BUSINESS ACTIVITY BANK CREDIT = BANK CREDIT FLOATS THE SHIP 

What happens if the water dries up? supply decreases. If the supply of money in a community were to increase, while other factors remained unchanged, the value of the medium of exchange would fall, that is prices would rise. 

If the supply of money were reduced, the reverse effect would follow.

We have seen, however, that the money actually in circulation is not the only medium of exchange. Credit also performs that function. Therefore an increase in the amount of credit extended to business men will tend to raise prices, and a decrease in credit to lower them.

Another important factor involved in the quantity theory of money is the rapidity of circulation. If a dollar (in the form of either money or credit) changes hands five times in one day, that is, if it is used to effect five different business transactions, that dollar is obviously doing as much work as five dollars which change hands only once. If all the money in a country increases the rate of its circulation, the effect is as though the amount of money in circulation had been increased. 

The quantity theory considers all these three factors, money, credit, and rapidity of circulation, and states that if, on the whole, they increase while other things remain equal, prices will rise, while if they decrease, prices will fall. 

Most economists believe that the quantity theory of money expresses a basic relationship existing between money and prices. 

However, the several factors involved (the amount of credit, rapidity of circulation, etc.) are very complex and vary constantly. Because of this, it is extremely difficult to determine with what degree of precision the theory can account for the price changes which occur.

Inflation. Suppose that a nation (in which the volume of goods produced remains fairly constant) experiences a sharp increase in (1) the amount of money in circulation, (2) the amount of credit issued, (3) the velocity of circulation of these media of exchange, or (4) a combination of any of these several factors. What will happen? In accordance with the quantity theory, there will be a sharp rise in the general price level. 

This process is known as inflation.

Our discussion above (page 161) indicates that inflation works great hardships on all people living on a fixed or semifixed income, and on the masses of wage-earners. Moreover, if the rise in prices is sharp enough, money will cease to function effectively as a medium of exchange, people will become panicstricken, and a great part of all productive activity will be paralyzed.

This is substantially what happened in Germany several years after the Great War. The government, which was in desperate straits, issued great quantities of "printing-press" money. 

Prices rose until the mark (the German monetary unit) was almost worthless and a complete collapse of German economic

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

life threatened. Germany's monetary system had to be thoroughly overhauled to remedy the effects of this disastrous inflation.

In the prosperous 1920's, there was a great expansion in the volume of loans granted by the banks of the United States. 

This resulted in a credit inflation. This inflation of course was not so extreme nor so disastrous as the German currency inflation, but it had a number of unfortunate effects and was a factor in bringing on the depression. 

Deflation. The process of inflation cannot continue indefinitely. Sooner or later there must be an adjustment. In some cases, the economic system may adapt itself to the new and permanently higher price level. In other cases, the adjustment may take the form of a contraction of purchasing power (money, credit, or velocity of circulation) and a sharp fall in prices.

This process is known as deflation. 

Falling prices have an adverse effect on profits and discourage business activity. Therefore, a period of deflation usually means bad business conditions and widespread unemployment. After the crash in 1929, the United States went through a long deflationary process but the worst of this painful adjustment now appears to be over.

Summary.-Money is a commodity which is accepted freely in exchange for all other commodities. Credit is not money, but it performs the same function as money, for it is used as a medium of exchange. Money and credit together form our monetary system. It is desirable that the buying power of the unit of the monetary system should not vary greatly from time to time.

CHAPTER XIV  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning and the functions of a bank. 

2. To distinguish between the different types of banks. 

3. To explain a bank statement and to show how it is affected as the bank conducts its business.

Meaning of a Bank. To begin with, a bank is a type of business enterprise. The best way to indicate the nature of any business enterprise is to tell what it does. For example, we would say that a shoe factory makes shoes, and that a publishing house produces books. What are the functions of a bank that distinguish it from other businesses? In general, banks deal in money and credit. More specifically, their functions are deposit, discount, issue, and collection.

Not all banks exercise exactly the same functions, but those that we have mentioned are the important activities of the banking business. Let us see what they mean.

Deposit. When people have money which they do not intend to spend immediately, they want to deposit it, or leave it for safekeeping, in a place where they know it will be secure. 

Banks provide such a place.

In the exercise of this function, the banker finds that deposits are of two sorts. Demand deposits are those which the depositor can withdraw from the bank at any time he wishes. The ordinary checking account is of this type. An account of this sort may show a balance of $1,000 on one day and only $100 the next day. Because these deposits can be withdrawn so quickly, a fairly large sum of money must be kept on hand to meet the demands for cash. The basic reserve requirements of banks belonging to the Federal Reserve System range from 7% to 13% of the total deposits, depending on the location of the bank. 

These basic requirements may be increased by as much as 100% (page 199). 182

DISCOUNT 

Time deposits are made by depositors who do not intend to withdraw their money immediately, and surrender the legal right to do so. For example, depositors may be required to notify their bank thirty days before withdrawing funds. In this period. the banker has an opportunity to obtain the money needed by selling securities or other assets. Because of this, there is not so much need for cash reserves behind time deposits, and banks which are members of the Federal Reserve System are required to maintain a basic reserve ratio for time deposits of only 3%. 

FIRST NATIONAL BANK DEPOSIT 

ISSUE 

COLLECTION THE FUNCTIONS OF A BANK 

Do all banks perform the same functions?

It may be that in certain localities the banker has found from experience that he needs a reserve larger than the legal requirements. In such a case he will make the reserve ratio as large as he considers advisable. Such discretionary reserves may be as high or higher than 30%.

Insurance of Deposits.-In an effort to safeguard the savings of depositors, the Federal Government established a permanent * A similar organization was set up on a temporary basis in 1933.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS FDIC (Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation) in 1935. This organization includes all banks which are members of the Federal Reserve System and it is open, with certain restrictions, to banks which are not members of the Federal Reserve System. 

The banks which join the FDIC are to create a common insurance fund by paying annually one-twelfth of one per cent of their total deposits. If any of these banks becomes unable to meet its obligations, the insurance fund will be used to reimburse all the depositors of that bank up to the amount of $5000 for each depositor.

Discount. The banker has found from experience that, under normal circumstances, all of his depositors will not want to withdraw all of their deposited funds at any one time. For example, if a total of $1,000,000 has been deposited in a bank by 100 different people, withdrawals and deposits will be made continually, but the total demand for cash on any one day will be much less than the $1,000,000 on deposit. Therefore, the banker can, with perfect honesty and safety, loan a part of the deposited funds to responsible borrowers. The banker charges interest on the loans, and from these interest charges he pays the expenses of his business and makes his profit. 

Illustration of Discounting.-Let us examine a typical case of discounting. A business man needs $5,000 to finance the manufacture of several hundred suits of men's clothing. He believes that within sixty days he will be able to complete the suits and sell them. Therefore, he feels that if he can borrow $5,000 for sixty days, he will be able to repay the loan and make a profit for himself.

He goes to his local bank, explains the situation, and asks for a loan. The banker makes a thorough investigation of the character and the business status of the applicant, and decides that it will be a good risk to make the loan. Before the funds are made available to the business man he must give the bank a written promise to repay the loan within the period arranged, which in this case is sixty days. This document, as we have seen, is called a promissory note.

The bank, however, will not give the business man the full $5,000. They will discount the note, or subtract the interest for sixty days at their rate of interest, which is, let us say, 6%.

The interest charges in this case amount to $50 (6% of $5,000 $300. $300 is the interest for one year. The interest for 

60 days is % of this, or $50). Our business man, therefore, receives only $4,950 from the bank. Instead of taking this money in cash he will, in all probability, be given a deposit account of $4,950 at the bank, against which he can draw checks as he sees fit. The business man uses this account to buy his woolens, and to pay his rent and labor. At the end of sixty days he sells his completed suits and repays the bank the $5,000. 

Notice that, since our business man took his loan in the form of a checking account, one effect of the loan was to establish a new deposit in the bank. A great part of the deposits in the banks of this country are the results of such loans. 

Discounting and Values.-In the exercise of the function of discount the primary problem of the banker is the recognition of values. When business men apply for loans it is for the purpose of producing more things to be sold. The banker must decide whether the business man will be able to turn out the goods and sell them at a reasonable price. If this seems fairly certain, the banker will be justified in granting the loan, the business man will be able to make profits, and society will have more goods for its use and enjoyment. Thus, the banker's ability to recognize values affects not only his own prosperity but also the prosperity and welfare of the community as a whole. 

Issue. This function of banking refers to the issuance of paper money which has legal status as a medium of exchange. 

In this country the National Banks and the Federal Reserve Banks are authorized by the Federal Government to issue such money.

Collection. Most business men receive a considerable part of the payments which are made to them in the course of business activities in the form of checks which may be drawn on *When the banker subtracts his interest in advance from the face value of the note, as in this case, the transaction is known as a discount. When the borrower receives the full face value of the note and then is required to repay this amount plus the interest (in this case, a total of $5050), the transaction is known as a loan. Bankers usually prefer to extend credit in the form of discounts rather than loans, because the discount is slightly more profitable to the bank.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

various banks. Suppose that you are a depositor in bank A and receive a check drawn on bank B in the same city. You present this check at your own bank, and your account there is credited with the amount of the check. Your bank is willing to do this because it knows that it can obtain that amount from bank B. 

In this way you are relieved of the necessity of calling at bank B and then returning to bank A to make the deposit. Your bank has collected the money for you.

What effect may the banker's decision have on the community as a whole?

This is how bank A acquires a claim on bank B. In the course of business activity, however, depositors in bank B will receive checks drawn on bank A, which they will present to bank B for collection. In this way bank B acquires a claim on bank A. You can easily see that if the claims of these two banks are in equal amounts, they cancel each other, and there is no need for any payment; or if B's claim on A is $1,500, and A's claim on B $1,400, only $100 need be paid (from A to B). 

This is the principle which banks use in adjusting accumu-

lated claims against each other. In large cities an institution known as the clearing house is established in order to facilitate payments between banks. All the banks in the city bring to the clearing house their checks drawn on other banks in the city. 

These checks, or claims, are balanced against each other, exactly as in the simple case we have given above. Transfer of cash is made only to cover the excess of one claim over another. 

Minor Functions of Banks. In addition to these four major functions the banks perform various minor services, such as renting safe-deposit boxes, providing advice on financial matters, administering estates, and supplying change. 

Types of Banks.-When we began our discussion of banking functions, we said that not all banks exercise exactly the same functions. As a result of these differences, we can distinguish four types of banks, the commercial bank, the savings bank, the investment bank, and the trust company. 

1. The Commercial Bank.-The commercial bank is interested chiefly in the extension of loans to business men; its primary function, then, is discount. Loans run for short periods, generally for thirty, sixty, or ninety days. Deposits in commercial banks are generally of the demand type. 

2. The Savings Bank.-Savings banks receive time deposits of small sums of money. With these funds they make long-term loans and investments. Because their deposits represent the small savings of great numbers of people, many of them in very moderate circumstances, savings banks are required by the law to follow an ultra-conservative banking policy. For example, they do not discount short-term commercial paper. 

3. The Investment Bank.-The investment bank is interested chiefly in long-term financing operations. It is through such banks that corporations float their stock and bond issues, that is, sell them to the public. Suppose, for example, that the X corporation wishes to float $500,000 in bonds in order to purchase new machines. This may be a perfectly sound investment, but it is probable that the X corporation does not have the facilities to bring its issue to the attention of the investing public. In this case, the corporation will try to sell its securities through an investment bank. If the bank considers the securities a good investment, it will underwrite them, that is, guarantee their sale

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

by a certain date at a stipulated price. The bank then uses newspaper advertisements, circulars, personal interviews, etc., to bring the issue to the attention of potential investors. When the securities are sold, the bank retains a certain percentage of the receipts as its commission or fee. 

4. The Trust Company.-Trust companies act as trustees and administrators of estates. They are given authority to invest funds and, in general, to manage the financial affairs of estates. 

Today, more than one type of banking is often performed by a single firm. The National City Bank of New York, for example, has both trust and savings departments, and the Cleveland Trust Company, in addition to acting as trustee and administrator of estates, performs the functions of commercial banking. 

The Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 provides that a bank belonging to the Federal Reserve System may not perform the functions of both commercial and investment banking. 

THE BANK STATEMENT Meaning of the Bank Statement.-Banks regularly issue documents, known as statements, which are simply reports of the conditions of their financial affairs. Now that we have gained some idea of the functions and operations of banks, let us examine such a statement and see how it reflects the principles which we have been studying.

Resources. The resources in this statement include everything which the bank owns or which is owed to the bank.* 

1. Loans and discounts refer to the funds which borrowers owe to the bank. This is considered a resource of the bank because the loans will eventually be paid back, or the bank will retain the collateral which was deposited as security for them. 

2. Overdrafts refer to the money which careless depositors owe because they have written checks for more money than they had on deposit. The bank has cashed these checks and will require the depositors to pay back the amount involved. 

3. United States bonds and other securities are evidences of indebtedness to the bank. The bank can sell these securities for cash in the open market or it can wait until the bonds mature and are redeemed by the government or by the organization which issued them. 

4. The banking house, or building, and other real estate are considered among the resources because they are valuable things which the bank owns and can sell, if it so desires. 

5. Cash and due from other banks refers to the money which the bank has in its own vaults and to money owed by other banks which it can obtain on relatively short notice. 

Liabilities. The liabilities are the obligations of a bank, what it owes to its depositors, stockholders and other people. 

1. This bank is a corporation and when it was organized it issued capital stock. The bank is indebted to the people who purchased this stock. 

2. Surplus and profits refer to the money which the bank has earned and which it owes to the stockholders, who are the owners of the bank (page 108). * Previous to the retirement of National Bank Notes (page 170), two other items would have appeared in a typical bank statement. The re- sources would have included a redemption fund, referring to the money which the bank was required by law to deposit with the United States Treasury. This money, which belonged to the bank, was used to redeem any notes presented for payment. The liabilities would have included circulation, referring to the notes which the bank had issued, and which were circulating all over the country. These notes were really promises to pay, and were considered obligations of the bank to the persons or organizations holding them. Note also that circulation entered into the calculation of a bank's reserve ratio.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

3. Deposits refer, of course, to the money which people have left with the bank for safekeeping and which the bank must return upon request.

$100,000 485,000 600,000 600,000 $110,000 495,000 610,000 610,000

Notice that the bank has increased its deposits without increasing the cash in its vaults. This results, of course, in a decrease in the reserve ratio. If you apply the formula, you will find that the reserve ratio has been reduced from 25.8% to 25.3%.

Since the extension of credit results in a fall in the reserve ratio, the reserve requirements provide an automatic check to the amount of credit that a bank can issue. 

Solvency and Liquidity. If the assets, or valuables, of a bank are sufficient to cover its liabilities, that bank is said to be solvent. The bank in our illustration was solvent, as the figures in its statement indicate. Suppose, however, that this bank had invested funds in securities which declined greatly in value, and had discounted notes for business men who were not able to make repayment. A bank which sustained these losses would probably become insolvent (that is, its liabilities would exceed its resources) and it would ultimately fail.

In order to be successful, however, a bank must be not only solvent but liquid. This means that the assets of the bank are in such form that it will be able to meet all immediate demands of depositors. A bank can keep itself in liquid condition by maintaining adequate cash reserves † and by making investments which can be turned into cash quickly, if necessary. For example, government bonds and short-term commercial paper are highly liquid. On the other hand, real estate and certain types * For purposes of simplicity, we disregard the interest which the bank would take from the business man in the form of a discount. 

As we explained above, a bank may find it necessary to maintain a higher reserve ratio than that required by the law.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

of securities are not considered liquid investments because they generally cannot be sold on short notice, unless the owner is willing to accept a heavy loss.

A bank which has invested heavily in non-liquid assets may be thoroughly solvent and yet unable to meet a sharp increase in the demands of its depositors. Such conditions would probably cause the failure of the bank.

The Government and Banks. From all that we have said about banks, we can see that they are definitely bound up with the public interest. The manner in which the banks exercise their various functions affects the well-being of all of us. The banks control the flow of credit which, in turn, plays a large part in determining the volume and nature of business activity. 

If this great power is used recklessly or only with thought of personal enrichment, economic disaster is almost inevitable. 

Moreover, through their investments, banks often acquire controlling interests in other business enterprises. If this power is used only with consideration of the bank's opportunities for gain, other people (such as the small investors in the corporations controlled by the bank) may suffer. 

For these reasons the banks of the country have always been regulated, more or less closely, by the government. In the course of the government's attempts to regulate the banks in the public interest certain problems arose. These problems will be considered in the next chapter.

CHAPTER XV BANKS AND THE FEDERAL RESERVE SYSTEM PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 1. To describe the organization of the Federal Reserve System. 

2. To explain the functions of the Federal Reserve System. 

3. To show how the Federal Reserve System has improved our banking system.

Creation of the Federal Reserve System.-Economic condi tions in the early years of the twentieth century indicated clearly that our monetary and banking systems required drastic reforms.

The National Banking System, which had existed since the Civil 

War, had the following defects: (1) the volume of currency could not be expanded and contracted in response to the varying needs of business; (2) a general lack of coördination prevailed; (3) reserves were scattered among the many small banks and could not be used effectively; (4) costs of collecting out-of-town checks were excessive. To remedy this, the Federal Reserve System was created in 1913. It introduced a centralizing and coördinating influence into our banking structure. 

Of the 16,000 banks in the United States in 1935, approximately 6400 were members of the Federal Reserve System, but these member banks contained about 65% of the country's total bank deposits.

Organization of the Federal Reserve System.-The country is divided into twelve districts, with a Federal Reserve Bank in each district. Each Federal Reserve Bank is financed by the sale of its stock to the member banks of its district. (Member banks include all national banks and any state banks which wish to join the system and are able to meet its requirements.)

Each of the twelve Reserve Banks is governed by a board of directors of nine men. Three of these men, known as class A 195

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

directors, are elected by the member banks to represent them. 

Three, known as class B directors, are elected by the member banks to represent the business interests of the district. Three, known as class C directors, are appointed by the central governing agency of the entire system, the Board of Governors, which sits at Washington. One of these class C directors serves as 

President of the Board of Directors and also as Federal Reserve Agent. In general, he acts as intermediary between his Federal 

Reserve Bank and the Board of Governors. 

The Board of Governors is composed of seven men, all appointed by the President of the United States. As indicated, this Board is the central coördinating and directing agency of the entire system. The Governors are chosen so that equitable representation is given to industry, agriculture, and other important economic groups. The Board of Governors is assisted by an Advisory Council made up of twelve men, one chosen by each of the Reserve Banks.

This description indicates that the System is so organized that it introduces a measure of centralization into our financial structure and, at the same time, allows considerable power to representative local authorities. This arrangement is not unlike the division of powers between the Federal Government and the states.

Functions of the Federal Reserve System.-We have stated above that the various member banks own the Federal Reserve Bank located in their district. (This ownership results from their purchase of the stock which the Reserve Banks issue.) We can see then that the Federal Reserve System is a system of bankers' banks.

Each Reserve Bank performs much the same functions for the member banks of its district as the member banks perform for their own customers. These functions, as we saw in the previous chapter, are discount, deposit, issue, and collection. We shall consider, in addition, two other functions which the Reserve Banks perform, international financing, and open-market operations. 

1. Rediscount.-In the Federal Reserve System the function of discount, with which you are familiar, is called rediscount. 

This term is used because the Reserve Banks discount again

the commercial paper which the member banks have already discounted for their own customers.* The following example will give you a clear idea of how this works. 

Mr. Jones, a business man at X, wishes to borrow $5,000 from his bank at Y. The bank cannot make the loan, for it does not have enough money at that time. The bank accepts Mr. 

Jones' promissory note (commercial paper) and takes it to the Federal Reserve Bank at Z. The arrows below show the path which Mr. Jones' commercial paper has followed. 

Mr. Jones

A Federal Reserve Bank 

→ Z The Federal Reserve Bank at Z loans $5,000 in the form of Federal Reserve Notes, a form of paper money which the Reserve Banks are authorized to issue (page 200) to the local bank at Y. 

Mr. Jones' note serves as security for this loan. The local bank then loans the $5,000 to Mr. Jones at X. The arrows below show the path which the $5,000 has followed. 

Spent for some business purpose (for production) Mr. Jones The local bank A Federal 

Reserve Bank

You will notice that more money to the extent of $5,000 has been put into circulation at O in answer to the legitimate demand of a business man. The currency has been expanded. 

When Mr. Jones repays his loan to the local bank, that bank repays the loan it had obtained from the Federal Reserve Bank. 

The arrows below show how the $5,000 is removed from circulation and sent back to the Federal Reserve Bank when it is no longer needed by the business man.

The currency has been contracted, for the $5,000, which is no longer needed by the business man, has been taken out of circulation. * If conditions require it, one Reserve Bank may rediscount commercial paper for another Reserve Bank.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

A money system which expands and contracts in response to the needs of business is said to be elastic. One of the primary purposes of the Federal Reserve System was to establish an elastic monetary system. 

OTHER FEDERAL RESERVE BANKS REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT THE FEDERAL RESERVE SYSTEM FEDERAL RESERVE BANK OF NY REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT RATE OPEN MARKET POLICY BOARD OF GOVERNORS REDISCOUNT FEDERAL RESERVE BANKS REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT REDISCOUNT COMMERCIAL BANK

Does the Board of Governors have absolute control over the operations of a New York commercial bank?

Just as Mr. Jones must pay the local bank a certain interest rate (discount) for the use of the money, so the local bank must pay an interest rate (rediscount) to the Federal Reserve Bank. 

The rediscount rate is set below the rate which the local bank charges Mr. Jones, in order to allow the local bank to make a profit.

Thus we see that when a member bank finds it necessary to rediscount commercial paper, the rediscount rate controls the discount rate. If the rediscount rate is raised, the local banker must raise his rate or his profit will be decreased or eliminated. 

If the rediscount rate is lowered, the local banker will be able to lower his rates and thus attract more customers without any decrease in his margin of profit.

The rediscount rate for each district is set by the board of directors of the Federal Reserve Bank in that district. The 

Banking Act of 1935 provided that the Board of Governors by a majority vote may change the rediscount rate of any district. 

2. Deposit. The funds on deposit at the various Reserve 

Banks come from two sources. (a) The United States Government deposits its funds with the Reserve Banks. (b) As we indicated in the previous chapter, all banks are required by law to maintain certain minimum reserves against the funds which their customers have deposited with them. Member banks deposit these reserves with the Federal Reserve Bank of their district. 

Thus we see that the reserves of all member banks, as well as certain funds of the Federal Government, are centralized in the possession of the Federal Reserve Banks.* This money is available for investment and for issuance of credit to member banks. 

The basic reserve ratios prescribed for Federal Reserve member banks are: Demand deposits-in central reserve cities (New 

York and Chicago), 13%; in reserve cities (smaller cities, as 

Peoria, Illinois), 10%; in all other cities, 7%. Time deposits— 

3% in all cities.

By the Banking Act of 1935, the Board of Governors is empowered to increase the reserve requirements for any district, but it cannot make them more than twice the basic requirements, nor can it reduce those requirements. We have seen that when a bank makes a loan, it lowers the ratio of cash reserves to deposits. Therefore, the effect of raising reserve requirements is to decrease the amount of credit a bank can extend. 

The Board of Governors first used this power in 1936, when it raised all basic reserve requirements by 50%. Early in 1937, another increase of the same amount was decreed by the Board. 

Thus, the reserve ratios were set at their maximum level, that is, twice the basic figures indicated above. At the time, these steps seemed necessary to check a possible credit inflation. However, the business recession of 1937-38 soon led to a reversal of policy. The Board felt that it was necessary to stimulate credit and, in April, 1938, lowered the reserve ratios to 2234%, 172% and 12% on demand deposits, and 5% on time deposits. *The Federal Reserve Banks themselves are required to maintain in their own vaults a thirty-five per cent reserve against all money deposited with them.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

3. Issue. As we have seen, the Federal Reserve Banks issue a type of money known as Federal Reserve Notes. The law requires that these notes be backed by forty per cent of their value in gold certificates. The remaining sixty per cent is covered by the commercial paper which business men issue when they borrow from their local banks, or by government bonds. 

These Federal Reserve Notes furnish the "elastic" element in our monetary system. As business expands, more eligible commercial paper comes into existence, and more Federal Reserve Notes can be issued. When business activity diminishes, there is less commercial paper eligible for rediscounting (for fewer business men wish to borrow capital funds) and the supply of Federal Reserve Notes in circulation contracts. 

4. Collection.-Checks are continually passing from one town to another in payment for goods and services. This means that banks are accumulating claims on each other. Prior to the Federal Reserve System, the settlement of these claims often required the shipment of gold to various parts of the country. It cost a great deal of money to transport gold because it had to be heavily insured and guarded. 

The Federal Reserve System has provided a more efficient method of making payments between different sections of the country. The method used is basically the same as that employed by local clearing houses, which we considered in the previous chapter. The Federal Reserve Bank acts as a clearing house for the banks of its district. Each bank sends to the Federal 

Reserve Bank the checks it has received which are drawn on other banks. The Reserve Bank balances these claims against each other, and, just as in the case of the local clearing house, provides for the payment of the differences only. These differences are paid by transferring the funds on deposit at the Reserve Bank from the credit of the bank which owes the difference, to the credit of the bank to which the difference is owed. 

Thus, actual money payments inside the district are avoided, and the bankers are saved the expense of the physical transfer of funds. This is an important function, except in large cities where local clearing houses have been in operation for many years. 

The same service is provided on a national scale by the gold settlement fund at Washington. Each Reserve Bank has a cer-

tain fund on deposit at the United States Treasury. Debts between Reserve Banks are adjusted by a transfer in title of these funds. The expense and danger of transferring funds are thus avoided. 

5. International Finance.-The Federal Reserve Act author izes the organization of banks for the sole purpose of engaging in international financing operations, and it also authorizes American banks to establish foreign branches. Smaller banking 

OPEN-MARKET OPERATIONS 

OPEN-MARKET PURCHASES SURPLUS OPEN MARKET. 

SALES SURPLUS FUNDS FUNDS BOOM PERIOD DEPRESSION PERIOD 

How did the Banking Act of 1935 affect the control of these operations? institutions are permitted to buy stock in these banks. Thus, it became possible for foreign trade to be financed through American banks. Actually, this development of international banking has not been very important. Foreign branches do operate today, but their primary function is to serve American tourists abroad. The British banker continues to provide the means of payment for the bulk of international trade. 

6. Open-Market Operations.-Open-market operations refer to the purchase and sale of government securities and other eligible securities by the Federal Reserve Banks. These operations

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

are important because they influence the amount of money in circulation and the amount of credit which can be issued. For example, if the Federal Reserve System sells considerable amounts of securities to individuals and to banks, this tends to drain the money market of its surplus funds and to decrease the amount of money available for credit purposes. The purchase of securities by the Federal Reserve Banks has just the reverse effect. More money is put into circulation and more credit can be issued.

The open-market policy (the decisions whether to buy or sell securities) is determined by a committee composed of the seven members of the Board of Governors and of five representatives of the Reserve Banks. Thus, the balance of power in determining open-market policies is held by the agents of the government, for the seven members of the Board of Governors are appointed by the President.*

Influence on Business as a Whole.-It is desirable that business be stable. Stability in business means that there will be no periods of feverish activity and expansion (booms) and no periods when business is almost at a standstill (depressions). 

Instead, there will be steady production and employment, and business will expand in a careful and reasonable way. The 

Federal Reserve System was designed to assist in the main. tenance of such stable conditions in business. 

Instruments which are used for this purpose are the re discount rate and the open-market operations. For example, if an unhealthy or too rapid expansion of business is taking place, the rediscount rate may be raised. This increase, presumably, will result in higher interest rates for loans, and business men will tend to borrow less from their banks. As we have explained, this will lead to the retirement of Federal Reserve 

Notes. At the same time, the Reserve Banks may sell securities on the open market. This will absorb the surplus funds of banks and individuals, and less cash will be available to invest and to serve as a basis for the extension of credit. In addition, the Board of Governors may raise the reserve requirements of 8. * The power to determine open-market policies was lodged in the hands of this committee by the Banking Act of 1935.

member banks, thus decreasing the amount of credit they can extend.

As a result of all these steps, there is less money circulating, the banks are not able to issue so much credit and, because of the high rates, business men are not so eager to use the bank credit which is available. The effect of these conditions, presumably, will be to check business activity. 

Similarly, if business is slow, the Federal Reserve System will attempt to stimulate it by lowering the rediscount rate and A MUCH-NEEDED 

STIMULANT 

FEDERAL RESERVE SYSTEM 

LOW

INTEREST RATES BUSINESS IN TIME OF DEPRESSION 

Can this stimulant effect a complete cure? the reserve requirements (if they are higher than the basic requirements) and by buying government securities. This will increase the amount of money in circulation and will presumably stimulate the use of credit.

By adjusting the rediscount rate, the Federal Reserve System also tends to level out inequalities in production costs among various sections of the country. This is so because interest charges are an important item in the cost of production and, under the Federal Reserve System, the rate of interest for business loans is fairly uniform all over the country.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

The Federal Reserve System and the Depression.-It may occur to you to ask why the Federal Reserve System did not prevent the country from sinking into the great depression and why it did not make possible a rapid recovery. There are several answers to this: (1) The control of the Federal Reserve 

System over the nation's credit is only partial. (2) Some economists believe that the rediscount rates were not raised quickly enough and sharply enough during the too-rapid expansion and speculative activity of the late 1920's. (3) The optimism of business men and the investing public was so great during that period that even higher interest rates could not curb the feverish activity. (4) The causes of depression are highly complex. Wise control of our banking system, however important it may be, is in itself not enough to prevent depressions or to cure them. The Federal Reserve System is unquestionably a valuable instrument but is not, and can never be, a cure-all.

CHAPTER XVI INTERNATIONAL TRADE PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the meaning and nature of international trade. 

2. To point out the reasons for the existence of international trade. 

3. To discuss the nature and importance of the foreign trade of the 

United States. 

4. To show how payments are made for the goods involved in international trade. 

5. To explain the present condition of international trade. 

Definition. International trade is business activity which crosses national boundary lines. It is to be distinguished from domestic trade, which takes place within the bounds of a single country.

Nature of International Trade. The goods which are involved in international trade can be divided into two general classes, visible items and invisible items. 

1. Visible Items.-Visible items are tangible, concrete things which are recorded when they enter a country. Such articles as wheat, machinery, live-stock, and iron and steel are examples. 

2. Invisible Items.—Many transactions which take place in the course of international trade do not involve the transfer of material goods, such as those mentioned above. For example, if an English firm insures an American ship, no material commodity, which would be recorded upon entry, is transferred from one country to the other. A transaction of this type is known as an invisible item. Other invisible items result from investment in foreign countries, interest payments on bonds, expenditures of tourists in foreign countries, remittances of funds between individuals (such as money orders or letters of credit sent by immigrants in the United States to their relatives in Europe), payments for shipping facilities, and bankers' commissions. 207

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

In all these cases, no material, visible commodities are exchanged, but an international debt is contracted and an international payment must be made exactly as though such an exchange had taken place.

Why International Trade Takes Place.-Why do merchants choose to exchange goods with foreigners instead of producing and using all necessary goods at home? The reason for this is that certain commodities and services can be produced more efficiently and cheaply in some countries than in others. example, in the United States we cannot produce silk, rubber, coffee, linens, and many other things as cheaply and efficiently as they can be made in other nations. Therefore, we import these articles and in exchange we offer those things in the production of which we excel, such as automobiles, wheat, cotton cloth, etc. (The reasons why certain nations excel in the production of particular articles have already been considered in our discussion of the geographic division of labor (page 43).

We have already seen that there are certain man-made barriers, known as tariffs, which tend to prevent specialization in the field or fields for which a nation is best fitted. In the United 

States, we have been committed to a high protective tariff for many years. This has exercised a definite check on the volume of international trade. Nevertheless, international trade, although much smaller than our internal or domestic trade, is a fairly important element in our economic life. In the first place, it enables us to obtain essential products which are not or cannot be produced in this country (rubber, tin, quinine, etc.). 

In the second place, it provides a market for our surplus production of various commodities. The volume of these surpluses varies from year to year. If they can be disposed of in foreign markets, internal trade is stabilized because prices will not be forced down by an excess supply of goods or by the fear of such an excess.

Balance of Trade. The chart on page 209 will give you some idea of the nature and volume of the trade which the United States carries on with the rest of the world. 

During this year, we sent the other nations of the world visible and invisible items which were worth $4,090,000,000. 

However, the goods which we bought from the other nations of

INTERNATIONAL TRADE 

209 the world amounted to less than this. Therefore, we say that we have a favorable balance of trade. (Nations which buy more goods than they sell are said to have an unfavorable balance of trade.)

In order to cover the amount by which our sales exceeded our purchases, the other nations of the world sent us money (in the form of gold) amounting to $1,303,000,000. This is the only money which actually came into the country in payment for the things we exported. The rest of the exports were "balanced" by our imports.

The mechanism by which we exchange our exports for imports from foreign countries is known as foreign exchange. 

Let us see how this operates.

FOREIGN EXCHANGE 

An International Transaction.-Let us suppose that you are a merchant living in New York and that I am a merchant living in London. I sell you a shipment of marmalade for which I charge £1014. (£ is the symbol for the pound, the English monetary unit.) Now, as an Englishman, I want to be paid in

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

pounds, but you, as an American, do all your business in dollars and have no pounds with which you can pay me. This is a serious difficulty, but we must solve it if that marmalade is to be sold.

Next door to your office in New York there is a man who has manufactured a number of typewriters and who has sold them to a dealer in London for $5000, which at present is equivalent to approximately £1014. It is easy to see that the same difficulty will arise here. Your neighbor wants to be paid in dollars

EUROPE 

Is a young agricultural nation more likely to export large quantities of visible items or of invisible items? (he has no use for pounds) but the London dealer has no dollars to send him. You and your neighbor talk over your problems, and it occurs to you that both difficulties will be solved if you pay your neighbor the $5000, and the typewriter importer in

London pays me the £1014. In this way, both your neighbor and I will receive payment, and we will receive it in the forms which we require, that is, in dollars and pounds respectively. 

Then you think matters over further and you decide that

INTERNATIONAL TRADE 

211 the most satisfactory method of making these two payments is by the use of a bill of exchange, the credit instrument which we explained in Chapter XIII, Money and Credit. Your neighbor will draw a bill for £1014 on the London trader who owes him the money for the typewriters. He sells you this bill for $5000 (remember we said that this is equivalent to about £1014). You send this bill to me in payment for the marmalade. 

I present it to the typewriter importer who pays me the £1014 which the bill calls for. 

If you understand this simple transaction thoroughly you understand the essential element in foreign exchange.

THE PRINCIPLE OF INTER- NATIONAL PAYMENTS 

Do transactions of this type eliminate entirely the need for shipments of gold?

Exchange Market.-Now, in actual everyday life it is extremely improbable that you, as a New York merchant, will have for a neighbor a man who is owed exactly as much by one London merchant as you owe to another London merchant. 

However, there are thousands of importers in New York who, in the course of a year, contract debts to London merchants. 

Similarly there are thousands of exporters who are owed money by London merchants. Because of this, it is desirable to concentrate the available supply of bills of exchange drawn on foreign merchants, and offer them for sale to business men who

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

need them to pay foreign debts. In other words, an exchange market must be created.

This is what is actually done. The agencies which buy up the available exchange and offer it for sale are the banks. As a general thing, they coöperate with a particular foreign bank which is known as the correspondent bank. The banks serve as go-betweens for exporters and importers, but they do not alter the basic facts of the simple illustration which we have given above. 

Banks in Foreign Exchange.—Let us return to the example outlined above and see how the banks would help us. Your neighbor, the gentleman who has exported the typewriters, will draw up a bill of exchange on his London customer. However, instead of trying to find another merchant who can use this bill he sells it to a bank which deals in foreign exchange. This bank will send the note to its correspondent bank in London, which, in turn, will collect the amount involved from the typewriter importer on whom the bill is drawn. The New York bank, which paid for the bill in the first place, is credited with the £1014 collected. However, I must still be paid by you for my marmalade. You effect this payment by turning over the correct amount to the New York bank, which orders its London correspondent bank (with whom it has built up a credit of £1014) to pay this amount to me.

So, we see that the use of banks does not alter the fact, which we previously discovered, that international exchange is essentially a process by which the value of imports is balanced against the value of exports in such a way as to avoid the shipment of gold.

Rate of Exchange.—In our discussion above we made reference to the fact that a certain number of dollars is worth a certain number of pounds. In the same way a certain number of 

French francs is worth a certain number of dollars, and a certain number of Italian liras is worth a certain number of francs. 

All national currencies have a particular value relative to other national currencies. How are these values, the so-called rates * When banks buy or sell foreign exchange they charge a fee which represents their charge for the service. For purposes of simplicity we will ignore the fee here.

INTERNATIONAL TRADE 

213 of exchange, determined? Let us consider the case of the pound and the dollar.

Why is the pound worth a certain number of dollars? In the first place the pound and the dollar have something in common. 

They are both worth a certain amount of gold. Before the 

World War the pound represented about 4.86 times the amount of gold that the dollar represented. This ratio settled the basic or normal value of the pound at $4.86. $4.86 was the so-called par of exchange. Since then the gold content of the dollar has been changed. At present the pound is equivalent to 8.2397 times as much gold as the dollar, so that the par of exchange is now $8.2397.*

It is important to understand that the rate of exchange (the rate at which you can actually buy foreign money or a claim to foreign money, that is, a bill of exchange) is not always the same as the par of exchange. It may be higher and it may be lower. The rate varies with the supply and demand of bills of exchange.

Suppose that we are importing a great deal from England and, at the same time, are exporting very little. There will be a great demand for bills of exchange drawn on English merchants and banks to pay for those imports. We have seen that these bills can be created only by exports from us to England. 

If, as we have stated, such exports are very small, the supply of suitable bills of exchange will also be small. Now, bills of exchange are like other commodities. If a great many people want to buy them and the supply is small, the price will go up. 

That is exactly what does happen under the conditions we have described. The value of the pound in terms of dollars will rise above the par of exchange. On the other hand, if we were ex- 

* The par of exchange is computed as follows: The United States will give you one dollar for approximately 15 grains of gold. England will give you one pound for approximately 125½ grains of gold. Thus, one pound is now equivalent to about 84 times as much gold as one dollar /125/2 

15148%). Hence, the par of exchange is $8.2397. Before President Roosevelt devalued the dollar, the par of exchange between the pound and the dollar was $4.86, since the United States was then willing to give you = 4.86 one dollar for 25% grains of gold (254 1252 .86).

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

porting a great deal to England at the same time that we im. ported very little, the value of the dollar would rise in terms of pounds. 

The Gold Points. Is there any limit to the amount by which the rate of exchange can rise or fall? Let us answer this question by returning to the example we used above. However, let us suppose that the transaction took place in 1914, because, as we shall shortly see, conditions were more typical or normal then, than they are now.

Remember that the English merchant, whom you must pay for the marmalade, wants to be paid in pounds, or in something which is equivalent to and exchangeable into pounds. That "something" is gold. Therefore, as an American importer, you have your choice of buying a bill of exchange, or of buying the proper amount of gold and of shipping it across the Atlantic. 

The cost of this latter method is fixed because both the pound and the dollar are equivalent to a certain amount of gold. The question then becomes (disregarding, for the moment, the cost of shipping), "For how much, that is, for how many dollars, can we purchase the amount of gold which is equivalent to a pound?"

We have already given you a name for this amount. We called it the par of exchange. Thus we can see that if the rate of exchange becomes higher than the par of exchange plus the cost of shipping gold (which is about two cents for the amount of gold equivalent to a pound), it would pay an American to ship gold to pay his debts to Englishmen. The same is true of an Englishman who must pay an American creditor. When the cost of buying the proper type of bill of exchange becomes greater than the cost of buying and shipping gold, he will naturally prefer to ship the gold.

These two limits to the rise and fall of the rate of exchange beyond which it becomes profitable to ship gold are known as the gold points. Of course, this rule does not apply only to the 

United States and England, but to any two nations dealing with each other whose currencies are convertible into gold. We might restate our rule by saying that whenever one currency becomes so dear in terms of another that it pays to ship gold, the debtor will ship gold. Note that shipments of gold take

INTERNATIONAL TRADE 

215 place when bills of exchange are relatively scarce, that is, when a nation's imports exceed its exports. Therefore, the shipments of gold serve to cover (pay for) a nation's unfavorable balance of trade.

Exports Must Pay for Imports.-It is hoped that the discussion above has made clear one extremely important fact about which there is much popular misunderstanding. International trade is essentially a process of exchange of goods. If a nation wishes to sell commodities and services in foreign countries, it must purchase commodities and services from foreign countries. 

For a limited period, a nation may sell more than it buys and import gold to cover the favorable" balance. If it imports a great deal of gold, however, this will tend to raise prices (cost of production) which, in turn, will put the gold-importing nation in an unfavorable position in international trade. It will not be able to sell its high-priced goods in foreign markets and its own citizens will be eager to buy the comparatively lowpriced foreign goods. Thus, with all other factors remaining equal, the balance between exports and imports would be restored.

The Present Situation.—Prior to the Great War, the situa tion which we have described above was in operation and gold moved freely from nation to nation. Since then, however, most of the great commercial nations (France was the last to take this step) have adopted policies by virtue of which their currencies are no longer freely convertible into gold and the export of gold is severely restricted. In the United States, the Roosevelt Administration put this policy into effect in April, 1933. 

One of the purposes of this action was to increase American exports by making them cheaper abroad. Suppose, for example, that an American is manufacturing a product which he must sell for $4.96 in order to make a profit. If he had attempted to sell this in England in March, 1933, he would have had to charge slightly less than 1/2 pounds, in order to get the equivalent of $4.96, since one pound at that time was worth about $3.43. After the devaluation of the dollar (which meant an increase in the dollar-value of the pound) he could sell his product in England for one pound, since one pound is now equivalent to about $4.96.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Failure of International Trade to Revive. For various reasons, this move did not have the hoped-for effect of reviving our international trade. (1) Other nations took comparable steps to devalue their currency in terms of foreign currencies. 

(2) Various other policies of the Roosevelt Administration 

(AAA, NRA, etc.) tended to raise the domestic cost of production. This prevented American producers from selling cheaply in foreign markets. (3) Various foreign nations (e.g. Italy) are making every possible effort to become self-sufficient, that is, to produce all necessary commodities at home and thus to eliminate the need for imports. (4) The world-wide economic depression affects foreign trade just as it affects internal trade. (5) The dropping of the gold standard by most of the great trading nations has lessened the confidence of international traders and made them somewhat reluctant to buy and sell large amounts of goods. They know that their currency and the currencies of the nations with which they are dealing are no longer convertible into a common standard of value at a fixed rate. (6) Finally, and possibly most important, high tariff barriers throughout the world have cut off the free flow of international trade.

It may be said that we are now living in an era of economic nationalism, in which primary emphasis is placed on trade within national borders. 

Rate of Exchange under Present Conditions.-The rate of exchange between two nations which have abandoned the full gold standard can no longer be determined solely by the relative gold content of the two currencies and the supply and demand of bills of exchange. This is so because while the two currencies are nominally equivalent to a certain amount of gold, they cannot be converted into gold and gold cannot be shipped freely from nation to nation. Therefore, the rate of exchange is not fixed, within narrow limits, by a single invariable factor. Several other forces may influence it. 

One of these influences is the relative purchasing power of the two currencies. The pound in England and the dollar in the United States are no longer convertible into a fixed amount of gold, but at any given time, they are both convertible into a particular quantity of goods of all sorts. The index number

INTERNATIONAL TRADE 

217 between the average purchasing powers of the pound and the dollar provides an objective basis for measuring the relative values of the two currencies. The rate of exchange tends to approach this purchasing power par. 

Another important influence on the rate of exchange at the present time is artificial manipulation by governments. The 

United States has set up a so-called stabilization fund of about $2,000,000,000 which is used to buy and sell foreign exchange. 

The object of these purchases and sales is to influence the value of the dollar in terms of foreign currencies. For example, the purchase of large amounts of English exchange would tend to raise the value of the pound in terms of dollars (or, we might say, to lower the value of the dollar in terms of pounds) because it would leave a smaller supply of exchange available to meet the demands of American business men. As we have seen, such conditions tend to raise the price of any commodity. 

The government attempts to carry on this manipulation of the rates of exchange in such a way that our national interests are protected and promoted. Other nations have set up similar funds and carry on like operations.

CHAPTER XVII PUBLIC FINANCE PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain what is meant by "public finance." 

2. To examine the sources from which the governmental units derive their incomes. 

3. To determine the purposes for which governmental units spend their incomes. 

4. To describe the characteristics of a good tax.

6. To consider the present problem in public finance.

Introduction. In previous chapters we have come into repeated contact with the part which the state* plays in our economic life. We have seen that there is no phase of the production, distribution and consumption of commodities and services which is not in some way influenced or controlled by our various governmental units. 

It may have occurred to you that most of these manifold activities require the expenditure of money. If the Interstate 

Commerce Commission, to mention only a single example, is to perform its function of regulating the railroads, it must rent offices, pay salaries, purchase supplies, and meet a host of incidental expenses. 

If you consider that the Interstate Commerce Commission is only one agency of the state, and a comparatively small one, you will begin to get some idea of the enormous sums required to keep our governmental machinery moving.

The term "state," as used here, refers in a general sense to all governmental organizations, including the Federal Government, the governments of all the states, and the governments of the smaller local divisions, such as cities and counties. 220

*This represents governmental spending under ordinary or normal conditions. Since 1933, because of the emergency relief and recovery program, the size and the relative importance of Federal expenditures have greatly increased.

PUBLIC FINANCE 

223 This table shows that the Federal Government, in 1929, spent most of its money for protection against foreign enemies, for payments on the national debt, and for regulating and fostering industry. The states spent the greater part of their incomes for highways, education, and social welfare. The bulk of the expenditures by local governments, as shown by the table on page 221, is also for education and social welfare.* 

Sources of the Money Received by the State.-In general, the government obtains the money to meet its expenses from two sources, taxation and public borrowing. The governmental units may also derive funds from the sale of the public domain, and from fees, such as the charge for a passport or a marriage license. However, loans and taxes provide by far the greater part of the public revenues. 

TAXATION 

Definition. A tax is a compulsory payment which an individual, or an organized group of individuals, makes to the government.

Amount of Taxation.—The table on page 224 shows the amounts which have been collected in taxes in this country over a period of forty years.

It is apparent from this table that the tax burden of the country has been increasing steadily since 1890. Total tax collections in 1930 were almost twelve times as great as in 1890. 

The population of the country almost doubled in the same period, so that tax collections per capita (that is, the average amount of taxes paid by every person in the country) increased about six-fold.

This increase can be attributed to a rise in the general price level (the government must pay more for the things that it buys), and also to a great expansion in the services and functions of government. The government has entered new fields (for example, old-age pensions), and it has greatly increased 

*The emergency expenditures of the Roosevelt Administration since 

1933 have greatly increased the relative importance of Federal expenditures for economic development and social welfare.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

and improved its facilities in fields in which it has long been engaged, such as education.
