This table shows that the backbone of the state and local tax systems is the general property tax, which furnished more than three-fourths of their total tax collections in 1929. 

General Property Tax.-Property can be divided into two general classes, real and personal. Real property refers to real estate, that is, land and the buildings which stand upon it. 

Personal property refers to all other types of possessions, such as clothes, securities, automobiles, furniture, books, etc.

We can readily see that if an individual wishes to conceal his personal property, it is generally easy for him to do so. This makes it impractical to levy taxes on personal property. On the other hand, it is impossible to conceal ownership of land. 

Because of this, the great bulk of general property taxes are levied on real property. 

The govern- 

The tax is applied in the following manner. mental unit in question determines how much money it will need for the coming year. Then it estimates or assesses the total value of all the real estate to be taxed and computes the rate of taxation which will yield the needed revenues. For example, if a city needs $5,000,000 in revenues and the assessed value of all the property in the city is $100,000,000, the tax rate will be $5,000,000 or 5%. Then the value of the property of each in- $100,000,000 dividual is assessed and the tax rate is applied uniformly.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Sales Tax. Within the last few years, many governmental units, including New York City, have adopted a sales tax. This is a tax levied on all retail purchases, usually with the exception of food. In New York City, the tax is two per cent, so that if you bought a pair of shoes for five dollars, you would have to pay a sales tax of ten cents.

The Tax Bill of 1936.-In June, 1936, Congress passed a tax bill which radically revised previous methods of corporate taxation. As we have seen (page 81), many corporations do not distribute all their earnings as dividends but retain part of them to build up an "undivided surplus.' These funds were formerly taxed at the same rate as other corporate earnings (12½ to 15%). The tax bill of 1936, however, provided for a sharply graduated tax (ranging from 7 to 27%) to be levied on future undistributed corporate income.

The supporters of the tax claimed that it would (1) encourage the distribution of corporate profits, thus creating greater mass purchasing power, (2) prevent wealthy stockholders from avoiding the heavy surtaxes on personal income by having their share of a corporation's earnings reinvested directly in the enterprise, and (3) encourage the financing of industrial expansion by floating securities (which subjects proposed investments to the consideration and judgment of the financial community) rather than by accumulated surpluses, which are controlled by the individuals managing the corporation. 

The tax was bitterly criticized by its opponents on the grounds (1) that undivided surpluses serve as a "cushion" which enables business enterprises to withstand depressions and other emergencies, and (2) that small corporations would be injured, for they have particular need of surpluses in order to expand. Critics held the tax largely responsible for the severe business recession in 1937. In 1938, the tax was greatly modified, and in 1939 completely abolished. 

Direct and Indirect Taxes.-In considering the social desirability of various types of taxes, it is important to note that many taxes are not really paid by the people on whom they are levied. For example, internal revenue taxes, such as those placed on tobacco and playing cards, are not borne by the manufacturer from whom the government collects the tax. The manufacturers buy the stamps which they are required to place on

PUBLIC FINANCE 

227 their products, but they shift the tax to the retailer in the form of higher prices, and he, in turn, shifts the tax to the consumer, in the same manner. A tax which is shifted in this way is called an indirect tax. On the other hand, a tax which, by its very nature, must be paid by the person on whom it is levied, such as an income tax, is called a direct tax. 

The term incidence of taxation is used to designate the point where the tax burden actually falls. For example, in the case of the tobacco tax, we would say that the incidence of taxation is on the consumer. In the case of income taxes, real property taxes (on private homes), and estate taxes, the incidence is on the individual on whom the government lays the tax. The incidence of taxation explains why it would not be socially desirable to place an excise tax on a commodity such as bread. The tax would fall on the consumers and would work a great hardship on many of them.

Characteristics of a Good Tax.-It is obviously to the interest of all of us that taxation be administered as efficiently as possible. The question arises, then, "What is a good tax?”

We may outline the characteristics of a good tax in the following manner:

The 

1. A good tax produces adequate amounts of revenue. object of taxation is to raise enough funds to meet governmental expenses, and a sound system of taxation will be so designed that it does this. 

2. A good tax is based on the ability of the taxpayer to pay. 

This is known as the "ability theory" of taxation, as opposed to the "benefit theory," which holds that the person who is benefited by the expenditure of the tax should pay it. 

The "benefit" method of taxation may be justifiable in some particular case. An instance of this is a tax on gasoline which is used to build and maintain highways. It seems reasonable that the motorists who use the highways should pay for their upkeep and replacement. As a general thing, in the modern world, however, it is socially desirable that taxes be placed according to ability to pay. If a millionaire contributes heavily toward the cost of government, he will still be able to maintain a high standard of living. In the case of a poor man, even small

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

contributions may mean the loss of modest comforts or necessities.* 

The income and inheritance taxes, which, as we have seen, are so important in our present system of taxation, are based directly on the ability-to-pay theory. This is particularly true because the rates imposed by these two taxes are progressive or graduated. This means that the higher the income or inheritance, the higher the rate which is applied. 

For example, the basic rate of taxation under the Federal Tax Law of 1935 is 4%. This rate is applied to annual incomes beyond $1000 for single men and incomes beyond $2500 for heads of families. However, the government levies surtaxes, or additional taxes, on incomes beyond $4000 a year. These surtaxes are sharply graduated and reach a maximum of 73% on annual incomes between $1,000,000 and $2,000,000. Thus, a person with an annual taxable income of $2,000,000 would pay a total tax of $770,000 (4% regular tax plus 73% surtax) on his second million alone. 

3. A good tax is certain. This means that the government can depend on the tax to yield certain revenues which can be estimated with fair accuracy in advance. 

4. A good tax cannot be avoided. It should be so designed that the individuals or organizations on whom it is levied must pay it, whether they want to or not. 

5. A good tax is economical to collect. If a tax is expensive to collect, the amount which the government gets for its own use is correspondingly small. It is estimated that it costs the Federal Government about three cents to collect one dollar in customs duties. Thus, import duties can be considered an economical tax. 

6. A good tax is convenient to pay. This means that the tax should put as little strain as possible upon the taxpayer. For example, the State of Indiana makes its general property tax comparatively convenient by collecting it in two installments, one in May and one in November.

A perfect tax would have all the features which we have *This is a good illustration of Engel's Law (page 37). The poor man spends so great a part of his income on necessities that any reduction of his income through taxation may deprive him of necessities.

PUBLIC FINANCE 

229 mentioned above. However, almost any tax which has ever been levied possesses defects in one respect or another. For example, the income tax is levied in accordance with ability to pay and it cannot be shifted, but it is expensive to collect and many attempts are made at evasion. A sales tax raises large revenues but, in the opinion of many, it places a deplorable burden on those whose incomes are barely sufficient to provide the necessities of life. On the whole, however, a good tax will conform fairly well to the requirements we have explained. 

Criticism of Real Property Taxation.-As we have explained, state and local governments derive the bulk of their revenues from taxes on real property. The use of this form of taxation has been severely criticized for the following reasons: 

1. It may penalize the owner of real property as compared with the owner of personal property. A man who owns a home worth $50,000 often pays more in taxes than a man who has securities which are worth the same amount. 

2. Frequently, the nominal owners of real property, on whom the tax is imposed, are really only part owners, because other individuals hold mortgages on the property. The mortgage is considered a form of personal property, so that the mortgagee (the person who owns the mortgage) is not required to bear a proportionate share of the tax burden. 

3. The assessment, or evaluation, of the property, on which the tax is based, involves great difficulties. In the first place it provides opportunities for corruption, that is, for bribery of the public officials who make the assessments, and for use of political influence. Even if the assessors are scrupulously honest, some property is bound to be over-assessed and some under-assessed. 

For all these reasons, it is claimed, the tax on real property is not based on ability to pay, and therefore should be radically reformed or supplanted in part by a different tax which is better adapted to modern social needs.

Effect of Taxation on Business.-We have seen in an earlier chapter that business could not be carried on without the many services which government performs. Therefore, the taxes which business pays to support governmental activities are for its own benefit, and are included in the calculations of the entre-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

preneur as part of his costs-as payment for one of the necessary factors in production. 

On the other hand, taxes which can be shifted, such as an excise tax or a sales tax, cause prices to rise. If the business man had to pay the tax himself, it would reduce his profits, and he naturally prefers to have the public pay it. Higher prices, as we have seen, discourage buying, and this hampers business. 

Therefore, while taxes cannot be eliminated, business men wish them to be kept as low as possible. This can be done by efficient and economical administration of our various governmental units.

Tax-Exempt Property. Such institutions as schools, hospitals, orphan asylums, etc., whether publicly or privately owned, are generally tax-exempt in this country. The reason for this is that institutions of this type are not profit-making enterprises but exist to perform services which, directly or indirectly, benefit the entire community. The government encourages their valuable activities by exempting them from taxation.

In addition, the income derived from bonds issued by the states and municipalities and from certain Federal bonds is exempt from the Federal income tax. The Federal Government exempts the bonds of states and municipalities because it does not have the constitutional power to tax these securities, and it exempts its own bonds in order to attract investors. 

PUBLIC BORROWING How Public Loans Are Made. The second important method by which the governmental units raise money is public borrowing. Governments borrow money in much the same manner that private corporations do. They issue bonds, which may be purchased by banks, by individuals, or by other governments, foreign or domestic. Like private businesses, governments must pay interest on their debts although, as we have previously said, they can usually float their loans at comparatively low interest rates because investors feel that such investments are safe.

Purposes of Loans.-We have seen that the governmental units within our country collect great revenues from the various

PUBLIC FINANCE 

231 taxes which they levy. Why, then, do they find it necessary to borrow funds? What is this additional money used for? Are such loans justifiable?

We may, in general, distinguish four purposes for which public loans are made: (1) to meet extraordinary situations or emergencies, (2) to establish revenue-yielding property, (3) to establish non-revenue-yielding property, and (4) to meet ordinary expenses not covered by regular income. 

1. Emergencies.-In case of emergencies which are unforeseen and temporary, borrowing by a governmental unit is clearly justified. For example, cities often borrow money after a great natural catastrophe, such as a fire or an earthquake, in order to relieve human suffering and to aid in repairing damage. The bonds which the Federal Government sold during the World War were also considered emergency loans. The need for them arose from an extraordinary and temporary situation. Similarly, the Roosevelt Administration has borrowed for the last several years to meet the demands of a different sort of emergency-depression and unemployment. 

2. Revenue-Yielding Property.-Loans for the establishment of revenue-yielding property are also clearly justifiable, for the public benefits from such enterprises and the revenue can be used to repay the loans. Examples of such enterprises are canals, tunnels, toll roads, city-owned transit lines and power plants. 

3. Non-Revenue-Yielding Property. By this we mean such property as schools, hospitals, roads, court-houses, etc., which are necessary for the public welfare, but from which the government derives no income, as it does from a power plant. 

No one denies that these things should be built, but there is some dispute as to whether they should be paid for out of loans or out of current revenue. On the one hand it may be said that payment out of current revenue would put too great a strain on any one year's tax yield. On the other hand, it is pointed out that property of this sort produces no income, so that, in any case, it must eventually be paid for out of taxes. Therefore, say those who are of this opinion, it is best to accumulate the money before it is spent, rather than to place such a burden on our future income.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

There is some truth in both these points of view. There are certainly particular cases, when a school or hospital is needed immediately, in which a governmental unit is justified in borrowing. On the other hand, too free borrowing of this sort should be discouraged because it creates a debt which must be paid out of future taxes. In any case, provision should be made in the budget to amortize the loan, that is, make provisions to pay it back gradually.

4. Ordinary Expenditures.-Finally, it can be said that, under normal conditions, loans which are made to meet ordinary expenses which are not covered by current revenues, are thoroughly bad. The governmental unit must adjust at least its ordinary expenses so that they remain within its income. If it does not, and borrows to meet the deficit, it is headed for bankruptcy.

Amount of Borrowing.-In 1929, the outstanding indebtedness of the Federal Government was $16,742,800,000. The indebtedness of all state and local governments was $13,452,141,000. 

Since 1929, however, the Federal debt has risen by leaps and bounds, due largely to the recovery and relief expenditures of the Roosevelt Administration. By 1940, the debt amounted to about 42 billion dollars. 401 35 WPA

The Rise of the National Debt (in Billions of Dollars). 

The growth of the public debt in a period of depression is, in itself, not a matter for serious concern. Indeed many economists believe that, when economic conditions are bad, it is advisable for the government to spend more than it raises by taxes. Such a deficit-spending program necessitates borrowing, thus creating additional bank deposits (deposit currency). This greatly stimulates business. Some economists hold that the budget should be balanced not annually but over the length of the business cycle. 

Ultimately, however, the budget must be balanced. There is a limit to the amount which even the most powerful government can borrow. If it exceeds this limit, it will find that its credit is exhausted. Such a situation would probably lead to inflation and to a disastrous economic collapse.

PUBLIC FINANCE 

235 The Federal Budget and Rearmament.-The financial problems of the Federal government were greatly complicated in 1940 by international developments. The course of the European war made it clear that it was vitally necessary for the United States to embark on a huge program of rearmament. 

This meant, particularly, building huge numbers of airplanes, tanks, machine guns, warships, etc. 

It was evident that the cost of this program would run into many billions of dollars. How were the costs to be met? Congress voted increases in many types of excise taxes; proposals were made to raise income tax rates, and to lower the exemption level. It seemed, however, that unless tax increases were made far more drastic, they would not meet more than a small part of the armaments bill. Some observers maintained that the 

American people would have to pay an unprecedented proportion of their national income in taxes, the only alternative being a Federal budget completely out of balance for an indefinite period of time. On the other hand, it was also pointed out that the armaments program should greatly stimulate business, thus making possible greater tax revenues at given rates, and smaller expenditures for the relief of the unemployed.

CHAPTER XVIII  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To describe the nature of the farm problem. 

2. To indicate what the government has done to help the farmer. 

3. To discuss the factors upon which a thorough-going solution of the farm problem depends. 

Nature of the Present Problem.-When Mark Overton be gan to cultivate land in frontier days, his problem was to raise a good crop on his farm. If he succeeded in doing this, he was prosperous. If he did not raise a good crop, he suffered hardships and perhaps went hungry.

The agricultural problem faced by the American people today is not the necessity of raising good crops in order that famine may be avoided. There have been crop failures, but the worst that has resulted from them has been a loss of money to the farmer and higher prices to the consumer. Famine has been practically unknown. Indeed, the farmer's problems for many years have been caused in great part by the surplus which he has produced, and by the low prices which result from such surpluses.

In other words, the American farmer of today has solved the problem of production which beset Mark Overton, but he finds that he cannot sell enough of his produced goods at prices which will enable him to earn a living.

Farmer's Credit Problem.-In addition to the difficulties resulting from the surplus, the American farmer has been faced with a serious credit problem. Most farmers have found it necessary to borrow from 40% to 50% of the money they pay for their farms. These loans, which are secured by mortgages on the land, usually run for from three to five years. 238

In general, it has been necessary to pay high interest rates in order to obtain this long-term credit. As a result the farmer has been forced to assume a burden of mortgaged indebtedness. 

He found that this burden was one of his greatest handicaps in his efforts to earn a living by cultivating his land. 

Federal Farm Loan Act. In an effort to ease this burden of mortgaged indebtedness, the Federal Farm Loan Act was passed in 1916, during the Wilson Administration. This Act established twelve Federal Land Banks which were authorized to loan money to farmers, on the security of their land, at interest rates of 6%. The duration of the loans was set at forty years. In the first four years of its operation, this system loaned more than 212 billion dollars.

The World War.-The World War greatly increased the de mand for agricultural products. In addition to the normal demand of the civilian population of the world, the purchasing agents for the armies of Europe scoured this country for foodstuffs, for horses, and for cloth. When the United States entered the war the demands of our army of four million men were added to this. 

As a result, prices of farm products sky-rocketed. Wheat, corn, cotton, tobacco, and wool all sold at unheard of prices. 

The farmer was becoming rich! His land was rising in value because it produced such high-priced crops. Farmers decided that the bonanza days would last forever, and were eager to buy more of the profit-producing acres. In order to obtain the funds to do this, many farmers mortgaged their original holdings heavily. In addition to this, worn-out lands which had formerly been used for grazing, were placed under cultivation and later sold for high prices. 

Post-War Difficulties.-Agricultural conditions began to change for the worse soon after the close of the war. The prices of farm products began to fall behind the prices of other commodities. This process continued quite steadily, and by June, 1933, the return from a given amount of crops would buy the farmer only $60 worth of goods, while the return from the same crops in 1914 would have bought him $100 worth of goods. 

Compared with 1914, his purchasing power, or prosperity, had been reduced by 40%. In addition, the mortgaged indebtedness

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

was becoming more and more burdensome. The farmer had borrowed money at high interest rates, when he was prosperous, and had to repay it at a time when the prices for his own products were falling rapidly. Many farmers were not even able to meet the interest charges on their debts, and lost their farms through foreclosure.

The Federal Farm Board. In 1929 these conditions were already very serious, and the Hoover Administration established the Federal Farm Board. This Board attempted to raise the prices which the farmer received for his products. To this end, it encouraged the organization of agricultural coöperative marketing associations, and bought up surpluses of wheat and cotton, which it held off the market for some time. Millions of dollars were spent in the purchase of these surpluses, but the attempt to boost prices was not successful.

The AAA. After our economic life sank into the great depression, the farmer's condition became even worse. It became clear that some effective measures would have to be taken to help him. It was believed, moreover, that if the farmer's purchasing power could be raised, it would benefit industry also, because the farmers, as a group, are the best customers of industry. 

In response to this need, the Roosevelt Administration passed the Agricultural Adjustment Act in 1933, and set up the Agricultural Adjustment Administration (the AAA) to administer the provisions of the law. In accordance with this legislation the farmers were paid subsidies in return for withdrawing part of their land from cultivation. For example, in 1933, the following provisions were made for wheat farmers: 

1. A subsidy of 30 cents a bushel (less administrative expenses) was paid to any farmer who would reduce his acreage devoted to wheat production by 15% of his average acreage between 1930 and 1934. 

2. The land withdrawn from cultivation was to lie idle or be used for crops which were not sold, but were intended to restore the fertility of the land. 

Cotton, corn and tobacco acreage were restricted in the same general manner. In addition, the government bought hogs at high prices and slaughtered them. The meat was used as fertilizer, or was given to relief agencies for free distribution.

The idea behind this acreage reduction and subsidized hog slaughter was to reduce the surplus, and thereby to raise prices. 

The Administration announced that its aim was to restore basic agricultural products to the average price level of the period from 1909 to 1914.

To an extent, this effort was successful; at least the prices of agricultural products did rise. However, the prices of industrial products, which the farmer must buy, rose at the same time, so that he did not get the full benefit of the rising prices. 

Processing Taxes.-The money which was used to pay the farmers' subsidies was obtained by the so-called processing taxes. 

This tax was imposed on the processors, that is, the manufacturers who use the agricultural products as their raw materials. 

For example, every time a mill (the processor) bought a bushel of wheat to manufacture flour, the Federal Government taxed that mill thirty cents. Then, the farmer who had agreed to restrict his acreage was paid his subsidy with that money. 

The same general plan was used for the other products whose production was restricted.

Processing taxes were criticized on the grounds that they raised prices, and thus retarded business activity. 

Soil Conservation and Domestic Allotment Act.—In Jan uary, 1936, the AAA was found unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. Several months later, the Soil Conservation and Domestic 

Allotment Act was passed. This law provides that the Federal 

Government shall pay farmers bounties for withdrawing acreage from the staple crops, such as wheat and cotton, which have the effect of depleting the soil of essential elements. This land is then to be planted in "nurse" crops, such as alfalfa, peas and beans, which build up or enrich the soil; and in trees and grasses, which bind the soil together and help it to resist erosion. This program is now administered by the states, although financed by the Federal Government (about 500 million dollars annually). 

AAA of 1938.-Large surpluses of staple crops appeared in 

1937 and 1938. Congress felt that further action was necessary, and passed the Agricultural Adjustment Act of 1938. This law represents an ambitious, many-sided attempt to control agricultural production and prices by enlisting the voluntary coöperation of farmers. Following are the main provisions :

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 1. Conservation and Parity Payments.-Payments authorized by the Soil Conservation and Domestic Allotment Act are to be continued. (Only farmers participating in this program will be eligible to share in the benefits of the new law.) Moreover, if funds are made available, the Secretary of Agriculture may decree additional "parity" payments, when he considers these necessary to restore the 1909-14 ratio of farm income to total national income. 

2. Acreage Allotments.-The Secretary of Agriculture is to announce annually the total national acreage allotment for each of five basic crops (wheat, cotton, tobacco, corn and rice). These allotments are to be divided among states, then among counties, and finally among individual producers. 

3. Marketing Quotas.-If the above measures fail to prevent overproduction, the Secretary of Agriculture is authorized to propose a national marketing quota for each of the crops mentioned. 

These quotas are also to be divided among states, counties and individuals. However, a quota cannot go into effect until approved by two-thirds of the farmers concerned, in a national referendum. 

Any farmer who markets produce above his quota may be fined. 

4. Crop Loans.-An agency known as the Commodity Credit 

Corporation will extend loans to farmers on the security of their crops. The loans will be set high enough to prevent prices from falling to unduly low levels. Crops acquired by the CCC will be stored until market conditions warrant their release. 

5. Crop Insurance. The Act establishes a system of insurance 

("ever-normal granary") for wheat farmers. In years of good crops, farmers will pay a premium (in wheat or cash) to an agency known as the Federal Crop Insurance Corporation. This wheat will be held off the market, thus helping to prevent sharp declines in prices. In years of poor crops farmers will be paid either wheat or cash, in proportion to their earlier contributions. Thus, it is hoped, farm income will be sustained and the public will be protected against high prices.

Financial Measures.-The Roosevelt Administration also took steps to reduce the farmer's burden of mortgaged indebtedness. 

The general fall in prices which accompanied the depression has made this burden more oppressive than ever. As we have already seen (page 162), a falling price level means that the value of money is increasing. Thus, farmers who had borrowed

99 money prior to 1929 were required to pay interest and amortization charges in "dearer, or more valuable dollars than those they had borrowed. This aggravated an already serious situation and it became evident that some measures would have to be taken to relieve the farmers. 

In 1933, farmers who had received loans from the Federal Land Banks had their interest rates reduced from 6% to 412%. 

In addition, 2 billion dollars in government-guaranteed bonds

These bonds paid only 4% interest, but many banks and individuals were glad to receive them in exchange for mortgages which were supposed to pay much higher rates, because incomes from such mortgages were uncertain and, in many cases, non-existent. When these exchanges had been made, the farmers' interest rate was reduced to 42%. If the reduced interest payments are made regularly, the farmer will not be required to make any payments on the principal of his loan until 1938.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

In addition, the Farm Credit Act of 1933 created new facilities to provide the farmer with short-term credit. 

Frazier-Lemke Act (1934).—This Act was intended to aid those farmers who, in spite of the legislation explained above, could not pay their debts, and faced the loss of their farms. 

The law provided for an extension of time in payments due on mortgages, and in some cases for an "adjustment" (reduction) of the amount to be paid. In April, 1935, the United States 

Supreme Court held the Act unconstitutional, because it violated property rights guaranteed by the Constitution. 

In 1935, Congress passed a new Frazier-Lemke Act which provided that farmers facing foreclosure on their property may resort to a modified form of bankruptcy in a Federal Court. 

The court may stay (postpone) the foreclosure for three years, during which period the farmer can retain the use of his property by paying a fair rental. At the end of three years, the property is to be re-appraised by the court and the farmer must settle his obligations or the farm will be sold at auction. Early in 

1937, by a unanimous vote, the Supreme Court found this law constitutional.

Farm Tenancy.-One of the more serious problems confronting American agriculture today is the steady increase in farm tenancy. Although at one time practically all our farmers owned the land they cultivated, it is now estimated that more than 40% of the farms within the United States are being worked by tenants. Opinion is almost unanimous that this is a highly undesirable trend. Tenant farmers naturally do not enjoy the same economic security as landowners. Many of them (e.g., the Southern "share-croppers") live in abject poverty. Moreover, tenants do not have a strong incentive to cultivate the land in such a way as to preserve soil fertility. 

Demands have been made in recent years that the Federal Government take steps to remedy this condition. In 1937, Congress passed the Bankhead-Jones Act, which authorizes the Secretary of Agriculture to make loans, at favorable terms, to tenant farmers for the purchase of farms. Although the appropriations allowed for this purpose are relatively small, the Act may foreshadow a far more extensive legislative attack on the problem of farm tenancy.

Conclusion.-Despite these attempts at relief, the farmer is still in difficulties. The level of agricultural prices in relation to the prices of other commodities, is still considerably lower than it was in 1914. This means that the farmer can buy less now with the returns from a given crop than he could before the war. He still finds the burden of mortgaged indebtedness heavy. 

It seems probable that a final solution for the farm problem can be achieved only when a sound prosperity is reëstablished in the country as a whole. When this occurs the city worker will be able to buy more farm products and to pay better prices for them, and the farmer's income will be raised. This interdependence of city and country reflects the fact that, under modern conditions, our nation is a single, unified whole. The welfare of any group is dependent on the welfare of all the rest of us. As 

President Roosevelt expressed it in a recent address, all the varied phases of our economic life form a "seamless web."

CHAPTER XIX CONSERVATION PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To describe the background and general character of the modern conservation problem. 

2. To explain how natural resources are wasted. 

3. To discuss what has been done to repair damage and prevent further loss, and to indicate what still remains to be done. 

4. To show how modern science is making possible the more effective use of our natural resources, and to indicate what general changes may be expected in the future.

Nature of the Problem.-The United States has been lavishly endowed with natural wealth. Our country has been favored with tremendous areas of fertile farm land, with great mineral deposits, with navigable and swiftly flowing streams, with dense and extensive forests, in short with almost all the materials which man needs in order to live and to live well. 

When the pioneers first came to this country, they probably thought that the abundant natural wealth they found here was practically inexhaustible. For many years this attitude was typical of the American people. They were anxious to exploit the vast resources of the new continent and they proceeded to use farms, mines and forests in whatever manner they found most convenient and profitable. This was not due primarily to selfishness. It was simply a failure to realize that all natural wealth is limited and that its misuse affects the well-being of future generations.

In the latter years of the nineteenth century, there was some recognition of the fact that our natural resources were being used up rapidly, and attempts were made to introduce reforms, particularly in the treatment of our timber lands. However, it was not until the presidency of Theodore Roosevelt that the problem received adequate attention. The President published information concerning the extensive damage which had already 247

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

been done and formulated a program for the wise and effective use of our natural resources. Under his leadership, many constructive steps were taken. In spite of this, the conservation problem has become even more serious since that time. 

What are the causes of these difficulties? In the first place, the rapid growth of the population and the need of modern large-scale industry for power and capital goods have resulted in a great drain on our natural resources. However, the real source of our troubles is not that we have used our natural resources extensively, but that we have done so without the proper care and efficiency. We have neglected science and common sense.

Today the nation is finally awakening to the need for a farreaching conservation program. In the following pages, we shall consider the manner in which our various natural resources have been misused, the steps which have already been taken to repair the damage and to prevent future loss, and the many steps which still remain to be taken. 

AGRICULTURAL LAND 

Erosion. The conservation problem in the case of agricultural land centers about the prevention of erosion. Erosion refers to the loss of the rich, fertile top-soil through the action of wind, rain, and other natural forces. Erosion seriously impairs the fertility of land and, in its extreme form, will make land incapable of bearing any crop. 

How far has erosion gone in the United States? Statistics issued by the Department of Agriculture indicate that the situation is extremely serious. Out of 385 million acres of crop land in the country, 35 million acres have lost all top-soil and are unable to bear crops; 115 million acres have lost most of the topsoil, 100 million acres have lost from 25% to 75% of the top-soil, and only 35 million acres show little or no erosion. These figures tell their own story. The productivity of our farming land, our basic natural resource, has already been gravely impaired and, unless something is done, will suffer even greater ravages in the future.

Nor is loss of fertility the only evil effect of erosion. Erosion tends to bring on floods. The rich, spongy top-soil can absorb a great amount of water, which it releases gradually in dry

CONSERVATION 

249 weather. If the top-soil has been washed away, water from rains and melting snows flows directly into rivers. When rains and snows have been unusually heavy, as in the winter and spring of 1936, this may swell rivers and other bodies of water beyond their usual size and cause devastating floods. Another serious effect of erosion is dust storms. When the rich top-soil has been washed away, the remaining soil is comparatively dry and sandy. During a period of drought, this fine, sandy layer is picked up by the wind and is swept over considerable areas in the form of blinding dust clouds. This is what happened in our 

Middle-Western states in the summer of 1936. As a result, considerable damage was done to property and millions of people suffered intense discomfort. Eroded soil is carried into rivers. 

If the erosion is extensive, the rivers may be rendered unnavigable, their courses may be changed, and harbors at the mouths of rivers may be clogged up by the deposits of soil. 

Prevention of Erosion.-What can be done to prevent erosion? 

1. Contour Farming.-Erosion is particularly rapid and thorough on hills or slopes. Farmers can check this by contour farming, that is, by planting crops with the rows running across a hillside, rather than up and down. Under these conditions, it is desirable to employ a device known as strip-cropping. This means that the farmer plants alternate belts of close-growing crops (such as wheat and oats) and cultivated crops (such as corn and potatoes). The close-growing belt will prevent the top-soil in the other belt from being washed down the slope. If the hill is very steep, terraces may be built horizontally across the slope. If erosion continues in spite of these measures, the land should be retired from cultivation and certain coarse grasses or shrubs planted to prevent further damage. 

2. Trees and Grasses.-One of the most effective preventatives of erosion is the presence of trees and of certain coarse grasses, such as the buffalo grass which once covered a great part of the Mid-Western regions of the United States. These plants have the effect of binding or of holding together the soil so that it cannot be easily scattered by the wind and the other agents of erosion. In addition, trees act as a safeguard against floods, which do great damage to the soil whenever they occur.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

The destruction of a great proportion of our forests (which we shall discuss below) and the disappearance of grasses and shrubs have deprived the land of a valuable protection against erosion. Reforestation and the replanting of the proper grasses and shrubs will help to prevent further damage. 

3. Depletion of Vital Elements.-When certain crops (wheat, tobacco, etc.) are planted year after year, the soil becomes exhausted of valuable elements, such as phosphates and nitrates. 

This has the effect of making the top-soil comparatively dry and sandy, so that it can be easily carried away by wind and rain. 

The farmer can avoid such conditions by devoting "worn out" land to the cultivation of certain crops (such as alfalfa, peas, and clover) which rebuild the soil, that is refurnish it with the valuable elements it has lost.

Recent Measures against Erosion.--In 1933, Congress established the Civilian Conservation Corps. Under the law which was passed several hundred thousand unemployed young men have been put to work at various forestation and conservation projects. The CCC has planted trees over extensive areas and has taken steps to prevent forest fires. Grasses and shrubs have been planted in regions in which extensive erosion threatens or has already occurred. Various other steps have been taken which, directly or indirectly, will help to combat erosion. 

The government has purchased several million acres of submarginal farm land (page 59) and is devoting a large portion of it to reforestation. Other public land is also being replanted with trees. If this Federal and state program is carried through successfully and on a large enough scale, it will have very beneficial effects in checking erosion. 

In April, 1935, the Soil Erosion Act was passed by Congress and signed by the President. This law established the Soil Conservation Service which will give the farmers practical advice and assistance in meeting erosion problems. Farmers will be taught what to do to check erosion and every effort will be made to impress them with the need for prompt and effective action. 

In Chapter XVIII, The Farm Problem, we discussed the Soil Conservation Act, which was passed early in 1936. As we have seen, this Act is intended not only to prevent the production of ruinous surpluses of staple crops, but also to encourage farmers

CONSERVATION 

251 to devote part of their land to the cultivation of trees and of the several soil-building crops mentioned above. If the law receives widespread coöperation from the farmers, it should have definitely beneficial results in checking erosion and in maintaining and improving soil fertility. 

Forests. Forests are among the most important of the natural resources of a nation. The discussion above has indicated the importance of forests in checking erosion. In addition to this important function, forests help to prevent floods by absorbing great amounts of water which they liberate gradually in the warm weather. Finally, forests are the source of lumber, tar products, and many other important raw materials. 

For these reasons, the waste and destruction of our great virgin forests is one of the most deplorable phases of our national history. When the pioneers arrived in the New World, over forty per cent of what is now the United States was covered by forests. Today, only about twenty-five per cent of the total area of the country is forest land and less than half of this contains trees from which lumber can be obtained. 

Waste of Forests. In what ways have our great forests been wasted? (1) Trees have been cut down in a thoughtless and destructive manner. Extensive areas were cleared completely of trees and for many years practically no replanting was undertaken. (2) Carelessness resulted in a number of great forest fires which destroyed many thousands of acres of some of the best forest land in the world. (3) Private individuals and organizations were allowed to take over valuable public timber lands and exploit them as they saw fit. 

Remedies. Several steps have been taken to remedy this situation. 

1. Reforestation.-Reforestation of public lands has been taking place extensively since the presidency of Theodore Roosevelt. This movement has received added impetus during the administration of Franklin D. Roosevelt. At present, the area of the National Forests is approximately 160 million acres. In addition, many states have undertaken public reforestation projects. 

2. Prevention of Forest Fires.-Various Federal and state agencies have been established to prevent and to extinguish

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

forest fires. In spite of this, there is still appalling waste from this source. In 1933, forest fires caused losses of over 60 million dollars. A good part of this was due simply to carelessness. The recently established CCC has constructed roads, lookouts, firebreaks, etc., which may decrease the number and severity of forest fires in the future. 

3. Efficient Lumbering Methods.-Certain simple precautions can be observed in lumbering (that is, in cutting timber) which will help to conserve our forests. Stumps should be cut close to the ground. Young trees should be protected. It is possible to correlate a program of cutting and of replanting so that trees which are cut down will be replaced by growing trees nearby. 

Much has been accomplished in all these fields in the past twenty-five or thirty years, but a great deal still remains to be done if our forests are to be saved from extinction. 

Conservation of Mineral Wealth.-The conservation problem in the field of mineral wealth centers about the mining and use of oil and coal. 

1. Oil. The demand for oil has increased tremendously in the last twenty-five years and there is every indication that it will grow still larger. Oil is used in automobiles, ships, locomotives, heating plants, etc., all of which are constantly increasing in number.

The principal source of waste in the production of oil is the method of drilling. Normally, whenever a hole is drilled into the "oil sands" (the natural underground reservoir which contains the oil) an intense gas pressure forces the oil to the surface. 

Thoughtless, competitive mining practices have resulted in the wasteful liberation of large quantities of natural gas. As a result, extensive deposits of oil have become commercially inaccessible. 

Another source of waste is the overflow from "gushers." In some cases, the oil shoots upward in a great spout and, if the producer is not prepared to catch it, much oil goes to waste. 

Fires at the oil wells also result in the destruction of considerable quantities of fuel. All of these sources of waste can be eliminated or reduced to a minimum by care and intelligence on the part of producers and refiners. 

2. Coal. As in the case of oil, mining practices in our coal mines have at times been wasteful: (a) Producers have been

CONSERVATION 

253 eager to work the nearest and richest veins and in many instances they have employed methods which make the somewhat less valuable deposits of coal commercially inaccessible. (b) 

Thousands of tons of slack (fine coal, dust, and dirt) are thrown away every year, although much of this could be utilized. (c) 

Coal is wasted in inefficient furnaces and engines which convert only a small part of the fuel they use into heat. 

The United States Government has set aside certain coal and oil reserves for future use. In addition, the Government leases some of its extensive holdings to private organizations. In these enterprises, the Government insists that the highest engineering standards be observed. However, there has been no extensive public supervision of private producers to insure careful and economical production, and the various wasteful practices mentioned above are still quite common. It is possible that the conservation of our mineral wealth will require more vigorous governmental action in the future.

Fisheries and Wild Life.-The Federal Government and the states have taken many important steps to conserve our fisheries and wild life. 

1. Fisheries.-Fisheries have been protected by laws which restrict fishing to certain periods of the year and which set aside various bodies of water in which fishing is forbidden. In addition, extensive programs of re-stocking have been undertaken. 

Large numbers of fish are transplanted to bodies of water which, for one reason or another, have been depleted of their own fish. 

2. Wild Life.-Many of the species of the rich and varied wild life which once inhabited America are now extinct or almost extinct. Federal and state governments are attempting to prevent further destruction by means of game laws which restrict hunting, by the establishment of game refuges, and by the direct protection and care of certain rare and interesting species, such as the buffalo.

Conservation of Water 

1. Drinking Water.-The drinking water of large cities is often carried considerable distances by an elaborate system of reservoirs, pipes, and aqueducts. It is absolutely necessary that there be an adequate supply of pure and palatable water. Many

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

cities, such as New York, have gone to great expense to protect the quality and quantity of their water supply. 

2. Waterfronts.—Many bodies of water near large cities become polluted through sewage and industrial waste materials. 

This is a possible source of disease and makes it unsafe to use the water for bathing and other recreational purposes. In recent years, much progress has been made toward a more efficient disposal of sewage and other waste materials. 

3. Waterways.-The United States is fortunate in having an extensive system of inland waterways (rivers, lakes, etc.) which facilitate transportation. In order to retain their maximum usefulness, these waterways must be kept free from accumulations of silt and other materials which obstruct the passage of boats. These obstructions are often particularly troublesome at the mouths of our great rivers, where many important harbor cities are located.

When the obstructing materials have accumulated, they must be cleared away by dredging. This is a difficult and expensive undertaking, but it is necessary if we are to conserve a natural resource which is of considerable value to transportation and commerce. In the last twenty-five years, many dredging projects, large and small, have been undertaken in the United States. 

In addition, we have supplemented our system of waterways by the construction of canals. 

4. Hydro-Electric Power.-One of the outstanding techno logical events of the twentieth century has been the development of hydro-electric power, that is, the use of the energy of running water to generate electricity. Hydro-electric power, which is cheap, clean and dependable, has made great strides in this country, but as yet we have developed only a small part of the resources which are suitable for this purpose. 

There is every reason to believe, therefore, that hydro-electric power will be far more extensively used in the future.

The principal problem which confronts the American people in connection with hydro-electric power is whether this future development shall be carried on by agencies of the government or by private enterprises under government control. The majority of the power plants in the country are privately owned, but there has been a fairly steady growth of government owner-

CONSERVATION 

255 ship and operation. Most of these public enterprises have been set up by local governments but recently the Federal Government has established a great project on the Tennessee River (the TVA). Through the Federal Power Commission, the Government has leased out water-power rights on many other navigable rivers to private concerns, but these leases are all for limited periods of time. (The arguments for and against government ownership are presented in Chapter XXI.) 

THE NEED FOR CONSERVATION 

NATURAL RESOURCES SCIENTIFIC TECHNIQUE CARE AND ECONOMY WASTE 

What effect would it have on all of us if the "thug" were to escape?

Conservation and National Planning.-In discussing the various aspects of the conservation problem, we have mentioned a number of specific remedies. It is obvious, however, that if these remedies are applied on a small scale, or in a piece-meal or disorganized fashion, they cannot be effective. We can check the forces of waste and destruction only by working together in accordance with a long-range, nation-wide plan. By a plan we mean that we must first decide, in detail, what must be done, and then determine the most effective way of doing it. 

Congress has the power to control navigable rivers.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Such a plan should be drawn up by the best authorities, and all the resources of Federal, state and local government must be marshalled to carry it out successfully. As we have seen, certain steps have already been taken toward such a program, but in view of the magnitude of the problem they cannot be regarded as more than a beginning. A comprehensive and thoroughly coördinated plan for conservation of our natural resources is a vital national necessity. 

POSITIVE ASPECTS OF CONSERVATION 

Introduction. Up to this point in our discussion, we have been concerned with how we can best conserve the various materials and resources which nature has given us. However, there is another phase to the wise and effective use of natural resources. Man learns not only to conserve but to create. Through the aid of science, we are able to utilize materials and forces which have always been present, but which were formerly of little or no value to us. As science and technology move on to new and greater achievements, this positive phase of conservation becomes more and more important. 

Let us see how the application of man's constructive intelligence enables him to make more effective use of what nature has given him.

Irrigation and Drainage. In our country, considerable tracts of desert land and swamp land which were formerly useless have been made productive. Desert lands are irrigated, that is the dry sands are provided with water, which is transported by aqueducts or canals from some other region. Swamp lands are drained, that is the surplus water is pumped out and, if necessary, sufficient quantities of dry earth are deposited. 

These activities have been carried on by the Federal and state governments, by private companies, and by coöperative associations. The lands which have been reclaimed so far in this manner are equal to the total area of the state of South Carolina. If necessary, much additional acreage can be reclaimed in the future.

By-Products. As we have previously explained, by-products are commodities whose production is incidental to the production of some other and more important commodity. In recent

CONSERVATION 

257 years, the application of scientific technique to industry has made possible a considerable extension of the number and value of by-products. Materials which were once thrown away as "waste" are now made into useful goods. For example, the seeds of the cotton plant were formerly considered worthless. Now they are made into an oil (cotton-seed oil) which is used in soaps, cookery and for other purposes. The cotton-seed oil business has a total annual value of many millions of dollars. In the meat-packing industry, the waste materials from cattle are made into several dozen by-products. We may expect further progress in this field in the future, for great business enterprises employ technicians who work constantly to develop new uses for waste materials.

Power. The principal sources of power in the modern world are coal and oil. The supplies of these materials are definitely exhaustible. It is obvious, therefore, that man will ultimately find it necessary to develop new sources of power. 

One such source, hydro-electric power, has already been discovered and extensively utilized. As we have stated, there is every indication that the use of hydro-electric power will be greatly increased in the future, particularly if certain technical obstacles are overcome. One of these obstacles is that, at present, we are not able to transmit electric current economically for more than 150 or 200 miles; in other words, the market for the current must be within several hundred miles of the generating plant. Because of this, vast water-power resources in sparsely populated regions in the Far West have remained undeveloped. 

However, the problem is receiving the attention of many scientists and engineers and we may confidently look forward to its eventual solution.

There are several other sources of power which, up to the present, have had little or no commercial application but are of great potential importance. (1) Some scientists believe that it may some day prove practicable to harness the great quantities of energy liberated by the winds. The winds have already been used to drive sailing boats, rotor ships and windmills, and it is quite possible that we will discover more important methods of utilizing this source of power. (2) The daily movements of the tides liberate enormous quantities of energy. Some progress has

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

already been made toward using this energy and more progress may be expected in the future. At present, the Federal Government is constructing a great tide-harnessing project at Passamaquoddy Bay in Maine. (3) There is good evidence that extremely high temperatures prevail some distance underneath the surface of the earth. Some day, we may find it possible to tap this source of heat (or energy) and put it to work at useful tasks. (4) The sun constantly liberates great quantities of radiant energy. Of course, we already utilize this energy in part, because no life could exist without the light and heat we receive from the sun. It is hoped, however, that we will develop techniques which will enable us to use solar energy directly as a source of industrial power. (5) One of the great dreams of modern science is the liberation of atomic energy. This means, in non-technical language, the complete utilization of the elemental forces which we know are involved in the basic structure of all matter. If we could get at these forces, the amount of energy they would place at the disposal of mankind is virtually unlimited. It has been estimated, for example, that a piece of coal the size of a pea contains enough energy to drive the Queen Mary across the Atlantic Ocean and back. The use of atomic energy, as yet, is only a dream, but distinguished physicists tell us that it is by no means a fantastic or impossible dream. Much research is now being carried on in this field. 

No one can say, with any certainty, which of these sources will be of practical use in the future. In the light of the history of the past three centuries, however, we can look forward with reasonable confidence to important new developments. These developments may well introduce some of the greatest social and economic changes in the history of the human race. 

New Products. Within comparatively recent years, man has learned to develop a large number of new products from the raw materials which the earth offers him. For example, aluminum was of no commercial importance until a cheap and efficient method of extracting the metal from the ore was discovered in the closing years of the nineteenth century. Today aluminum is used in almost every home in the country. Cellophane, which is used so widely as a protective wrapper, has been developed within the last few years. Other materials of commercial impor-

CONSERVATION 

259 tance which have been produced recently are bakelite, chromium, neon, tungsten, beryllium, and many others.

In addition to this, we have developed processes for producing already familiar materials from new sources. For example, it is now possible to manufacture nitrates (for use as fertilizers and in munitions) from the nitrogen in the atmosphere. Previously nitrates could be obtained only from the nitrate beds, almost all of which are located in Chile. When our natural petroleum deposits give out, it may be commercially practicable to manufacture oil from a type of rock known as oil shale. Natural silk has already been largely displaced by rayon, which is manufactured from wood pulp. There has been an extensive development of synthetic dyes.

In all of these cases, man uses only the materials which nature gives him, but he has learned to use those materials in such a way that a greater amount of wealth is made available for his consumption.

CHAPTER XX  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain the reasons for the conflicts between capital and labor. 

2. To discuss the handicaps of labor. 

3. To explain the meaning of "collective bargaining." 

4. To describe the various types of labor organizations in the United States. 

5. To discuss the methods used by employers and employees in industrial warfare. 

6. To discuss what has been done and what can be done to promote better relations between employer and employee. 

Conflict between Employer and Employee.-When a laborer takes a job, he wants to be paid as high a wage as possible. He prefers to work a comparatively small number of hours for this wage, and he wants pleasant working conditions. The employer, on the other hand, wants to keep his labor costs low. As a result of this, employer and employee frequently become involved in disputes over wages, working conditions, and hours of labor. 

Handicaps of Labor.-In the bargaining which takes place between employer and employee, labor suffers from several serious handicaps. 

1. It is generally true, especially at the present time, that there are more men looking for work than there are jobs available. Thus, the employee values his job highly, for he knows that if he loses it he will have great difficulty in finding another one. The employer, on the other hand, is not greatly concerned if he loses an employee because he knows that, save in a comparatively few exceptional cases, the employee can be easily replaced. 

2. As we explained in Chapter VI, Labor and Wages, labor has several peculiar characteristics. (a) Labor is perishable.

261

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

The laborer cannot store his efforts in a warehouse and wait for higher wages. This sometimes causes workers to accept a very low wage, for they know that if they miss a day's work, their labor will be wasted entirely. (b) Labor is immobile. Workingmen cannot move easily from one region to another and from one occupation to another. Because of this, workers often find it necessary to accept low wages even though higher wages are

Are these bonds ever broken? Explain. being offered in other industries and in other parts of the country. This is in contrast to the great mobility of commodities. 

A manufacturer can ship his goods wherever he believes it will be profitable to do so. (c) The laborer and his labor cannot be separated. This means that the worker, as a person, must be present wherever the work is taking place and may suffer from the danger or bad conditions involved. 

3. Another handicap to labor is the overbearing and selfish attitude which is characteristic of some employers. Employers of this type, who are the exception rather than the rule, try to take full advantage of their position and pay as low wages as

possible. They rebuff every effort which their men make to discuss their problems and threaten to discharge any "troublemakers." The low wages which employers of this type may pay give them an advantage over their competitors and sometimes force these competitors to cut wages in order to bring down their costs of production.

Collective Bargaining.—In an effort to overcome these serious handicaps, labor has developed a policy known as collective bargaining. This means simply that the employer does not bargain with the individual laborer, who as we have seen is comparatively powerless, but with the representatives of the organized body of his workers. If this organization is strong enough, it can bring great pressure to bear on the employer, for it can cut off his supply of labor and force him to suspend production. 

Types of Labor Organizations.-There is some disagreement in this country as to the most effective type of labor organization to carry on collective bargaining. Several different types of organizations have appeared. 

1. Trade Unions.-A trade union is an organization of workers who are employed at a single occupation, as, for example, a union of plumbers or of railroad engineers. (Trade unions are often known as horizontal or craft unions.) 

2. Industrial Unions.-An industrial, or vertical, union is an organization of workers who are employed in a single industry, regardless of the type of work each individual may be doing. 

The Amalgamated Clothing Workers of America, an organiza tion of workers employed in the manufacture of men's clothing, is one of the outstanding industrial unions in the country. This union includes all types of clothing workers, whether they be cutters, pressers, buttonhole makers, etc. 

3. Labor Unions.-A labor union is an organization of workers who may be engaged in any trade or industry. The only qualification for membership is that the applicant be a wage.. earner. The Knights of Labor (which is no longer in existence) and certain smaller unions of the A. F. of L. are examples of this type of union.

The American Federation of Labor.-The American Federation of Labor formerly included the great majority of the organ-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

ized workers in the United States. Only a few strong unions, such as the Amalgamated Clothing Workers and the five Railroad Brotherhoods, existed outside its ranks. In recent years, however, this dominance has been seriously challenged by the Committee for Industrial Organization or C. I. O. (see below). 

The A. F. of L. is a loose, decentralized confederation, consisting of about 100 national trade unions and a large number of local trade unions. The powers and activities of the central government of the Federation are comparatively limited. It works for the passage of favorable legislation, carries on educational work, seeks to organize new unions, and tries to keep member unions at peace with each other.* In addition, the central government of the Federation lays down general policies which, to an extent, guide the activities of the member unions. However, the vital power of calling a strike or of forbidding a strike rests with the member unions.

The Federation, as we have said, works for legislation favorable to labor, but it is essentially non-political in character. It has never attempted to elect any of its own candidates to public office, but supports that major party which appears more favorably inclined to the interests of labor. This avoidance of direct participation in politics reflects the fact that the American Federation of Labor does not wish to bring about any far-reaching changes in our social order, but is concerned with immediate practical gains, such as high wages, a short work-week, and good working conditions. 

The Rise of the C. I. O.—The outstanding event in the recent history of the American labor movement has been the rise of the C. I. O. This organization was formed in 1935 by the leaders of ten large A. F. of L. unions. The executive council of the A. F. of L. subsequently suspended the ten unions. This conflict may lead to a condition of dual unionism, which bodes ill for the future solidarity and strength of organized labor. 

The basic purpose of the C. I. O. is to organize the great numbers of workers in the mass production industries (steel, rubber, automobiles, etc.) on an industrial or vertical basis. 

The supporters of the movement maintain that the vertical form * Disputes among unions arise most commonly when two unions claim the right to control a particular group of workers. This is known as the jurisdictional dispute.

of organization is better suited to present-day conditions, because the routinized methods of modern large-scale production have created a large class of unskilled or semi-skilled workers, who cannot be considered members of any particular craft. The 

A. F. of L. horizontal unions, it is charged, are suited only to the needs of the highly skilled workers, the "aristocracy of labor." C. I. O. adherents concede that craft organization has proved its value in some fields, but maintain that it "abandons thirty million workers in the basic industries, ten times the strength of the A. F. of L.”

The C. I. O. has met with considerable success in its efforts to carry out its program. By July, 1937, it claimed a membership of 3,200,000 within its 28 affiliated union groups. (The 

A. F. of L., at the same date, claimed a membership of 3,600,- 

000.) It has carried out several successful and partially successful strikes; and has negotiated peacefully with the great steel-manufacturing companies, as a result of which union recognition and collective bargaining contracts were gained. 

The C. I. O. unions have made effective use of a new weapon in industrial warfare-the sit-down strike. When workers "sit

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

down," they not only cease work, but refuse to leave the factory or store. Thus, it is impossible for the employer to bring in strike-breakers and resume operations. Although such forcible occupation of private property is generally considered illegal, it is difficult to evict "sit-downers" without violence, and public officials often hesitate to take drastic steps. The strategic value of the "sit-down" was proved in the General 

Motors strike of 1937.
