UNION Do you think that the first unions established in this country were vertical or horizontal? Explain. 

Opposition of Employers to Collective Bargaining. Some employers prefer to deal with their employees as individuals, and they resent the attempts of "outsiders" to organize them.

Other employers, with the same attitude, have attempted to form company unions, that is, organizations composed only of workers in their own plant. The very nature of these company unions makes it possible for the employer to dominate them and labor leaders are bitterly opposed to them. Most disinterested people who have studied the question do not believe that company unions are an effective instrument for genuine collective bargaining.

It is a mistake to think that all opposition to collective bargaining is actuated by greed and a desire to exploit labor. Unquestionably, many business men are sincere in their belief that they can be trusted to protect the interests of their employees and that labor organizations are unnecessary and harmful. However, it would seem that the inequalities between the employer and the individual employee, as we explained them above, are so great that the workers must organize and bargain collectively if 

COLLECTIVE BARGAINING REMOVES THE BETTER WAGES AND WORKING CONDITIONS COLLECTIVE BARGAINING UNEQUAL BARGAINING POWER WORKER OBSTACLE WAGNER-CONNERY LABOR RELATIONS ACT 

In what way would the removal of this obstacle benefit the entire country? they are to maintain high wage-standards and good working conditions. This is the opinion of the Roosevelt Administration as was evidenced by the passage of the Wagner-Connery Act. 

Wagner-Connery Act.-This law, which was signed by President Roosevelt in July, 1935, gives the workers the right to form or join any labor organization they may desire. The employer is obliged to bargain collectively with any organization which is chosen by a majority of his men. The employer, moreover, is forbidden to discharge any worker for union activity, and to

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

attempt to control or interfere with any union. A National 

Labor Relations Board was created to administer the law. 

The Wagner-Connery Act, in effect, guarantees workers the right to collective bargaining and may eliminate the company union from the American industrial scene. 

Policies of the Unions.—What policies do the unions follow in their attempts to protect the interests of the workers? 

1. Apprenticeship.-Some unions, as, for example, the American Typographical Union, require that newcomers into the trade be trained thoroughly over a definite period of time before they are accepted as full-fledged members and are entitled to receive. full union wages. These apprenticeship requirements insure good workmanship and, by limiting the supply of labor, help to maintain high wages. 

2. The Closed Shop. This is a term applied to a factory or workshop in which only union labor is employed. Organized labor considers this situation highly desirable because it increases the strength and prestige of the union and because the workers can present a united front to the employer in case of any important disagreement. Some employers, on the other hand, wish to maintain their plants as open shops, that is, plants in which any worker may be employed, whether or not he is a union member. 

3. Restriction of Output.-Some unions attempt to restrict the output of their members, that is, to prescribe a certain maximum amount of work per day which the worker is not permitted to exceed. This is intended to create more jobs, for if the average output per worker is low, the employer will have to hire more workers. Another purpose of this policy is to limit the supply of labor (which depends on the amount of work which men do as well as on the number of men available to work) and thus raise wages.

In some instances this policy has been used to prevent employers from “speeding up" their workers to a point where their physical well-being is threatened. In other cases, however, restriction of output appears to be based on a misunderstanding of the nature of wages. The union leaders ignore the fact that wages are paid out of the wealth that labor creates and that a restriction of the creation of this wealth in the long run cannot

increase wages. * A limited number of men may benefit for a short time by restricting output, but the working class as a whole will suffer. 

4. Benefit Features.-Unions use part of the money which workers pay as dues to accumulate benefit funds. With these funds they aid members when they are sick and unemployed, pay old-age pensions, and support the widows and children of deceased members.

INDUSTRIAL WARFARE 

Causes. In some cases employers and employees cannot settle their disagreements by peaceful means. The disagreement may be over wages, working conditions, or over the employer's unwillingness to recognize the union, that is, to bargain collectively. In such cases, a condition known as industrial warfare ensues. In this conflict both sides employ various weapons. 

Weapons of the Employees 1. The Strike.-A strike is an attempt by a labor organization to cut off the labor supply of some employer or group of employers. All the union members quit work and they make attempts to induce non-union workers to quit with them. Strikes may be confined to a single plant or they may extend to all the plants engaged in the industry in the city, state, or even the nation. In some cases, workers in other industries will join in the strike, even though they have no immediate grievances themselves. This is known as a "sympathetic strike." 

An effective strike (that is, a strike which really succeeds in cutting off the labor supply) is a very powerful weapon, for it prevents the employer from producing and from earning profits. 

2. Picketing.-Workers who are on strike often march about the employer's place of business with signs announcing their grievances and asking the public not to patronize the establishment. In addition, pickets attempt to prevent strikebreakers from taking the places of the men who are out on strike. 

3. The Boycott.-A boycott is an attempt to induce as many people as possible not to buy the product of the employer against * The fallacy in restriction of output is similar to the fallacy in the wages fund theory (page 74).

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

whom the strike has been called. A successful boycott will greatly reduce the sales of the employer in question and may cause him to accede to the demands of the union. 

The use of the union label may also lead to discrimination against the products of employers whom the unions consider opposed to their interests. This label designates commodities produced under conditions approved by the union, and the friends of labor are urged to buy only products which bear this label. 

4. Sabotage.-Sabotage is willful destruction of the property of employers against whom the workers have grievances. It may take the form of burning buildings, smashing machines, wrecking trucks, etc. American labor organizations disapprove of the use of sabotage.

Weapons of the Employers. In the event of industrial warfare, the employers have a number of weapons with which they combat the employees. 

1. The Injunction.-An injunction is a court order which forbids a person or group of persons to do certain things which, in the opinion of the court, would cause irreparable damage to someone else. Injunctions have been used in many cases to forbid the calling of a strike. In other cases, strikers have been ordered to refrain from picketing, from boycotts, from collective demonstrations, and from other activities which are essential to the successful operation of a strike. Violation of an injunction is considered contempt of court and was formerly punishable without recourse to a trial by jury.

For these reasons, and because of its belief that the courts favored employers in the granting of injunctions, labor has always been opposed to this form of judicial action. In 1932, Congress passed a bill which forbade the issuance of injunctions in labor disputes unless evidence was presented that the strikers were endangering or injuring the employer's interests and that the employer had made "every reasonable effort" to settle the dispute. This bill also gave persons accused of violating an injunction the right to demand a jury trial with a judge other than the one who granted the injunction.

In this same bill, the so-called yellow-dog contract, which is

an agreement signed by an employee not to join a union during the term of his employment, was declared illegal. 

2. The Lockout.-Employers who are involved in disputes with their employees and who anticipate strikes and other disturbances sometimes close up their plants and announce that they will re-open only when the workers are willing to accept the terms offered. In this way the workers' incomes are cut off and in many cases they are ultimately forced to yield to the employer's demands. 

3. The Blacklist.-This is a list which circulates among employers and which contains the names of workers who are considered union organizers or "trouble-makers." 

The purpose behind the blacklist is to prevent these men from finding employment and to intimidate other men who might otherwise become active in the work of the union. 

4. Labor Spies.-Some employers who anticipate trouble with their workers have made use of labor spies or "stoolpigeons." These spies go about the factory or shop disguised as ordinary workers. Their function is to find out which employees are "trouble-makers" (that is active supporters of the union or of a strike) in order that they may be discharged or possibly blacklisted. There is some evidence that "stool-pigeons" have been used during the course of a strike or period of labor tension to provoke acts of violence or sabotage by workers. The object of this is to discredit the union in the eyes of the public and, possibly, to induce a judge to issue an injunction.

Legality of the Weapons of Industrial Warfare. In the course of the last fifty years, the courts have had many opportunities to pass on the legality of the various weapons used in industrial warfare. At first, some of the decisions of the courts contradicted each other and there was considerable confusion and uncertainty. Gradually, however, the courts have developed a fairly uniform and consistent attitude toward these important legal problems. This attitude can be summarized in the following manner: 

1. Strikes are considered legal. 

2. The boycott is considered legal if it involves merely a refusal on the part of the striking workers to buy from the boy-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

cotted firm. However, if the strikers urge other people not to patronize the firm, the boycott becomes illegal. 

3. Sabotage is illegal because it involves the destruction of property. 

4. Peaceful picketing is legal, but picketing which involves violence and endangers life and property is illegal. In practice it is often extremely difficult to say just when picketing becomes violent and which of the sides involved is responsible for the violence. As a result of this, picketing cases which are brought before the courts are a constant source of dispute and dissatisfaction. 

5. Injunctions are considered legal, with the modifications explained above. 

6. The lockout is legal. 

7. The blacklist is considered illegal. However, a blacklist is always held in strictest secrecy so that it is almost impossible to prove in the courts that it exists. 

Evils of Industrial Warfare.-Industrial warfare involves serious losses to all concerned. The workers suffer because their wages, usually their only source of income, are discontinued. 

Employers lose because the flow of profits is diminished or cut off entirely. The public is deprived of the use of commodities or services. In addition, industrial warfare is often accompanied by the loss of lives and the destruction of property. 

Therefore, it is highly desirable that industrial warfare be avoided.

Methods of Avoiding Industrial Warfare 

1. Conferences.-The employers and the representatives of the employees may meet, talk over their problems, and try to arrive at a peaceful and reasonable settlement. If both parties approach these conferences with the proper frame of mind, strikes can often be avoided. 

2. Conciliation.-A third person or group of persons may attempt to bring the employer and employees together and arrange a settlement of their dispute. The conciliator offers his services voluntarily and the disputants are not obliged to accept them. The conciliator does not attempt to decide the points at issue, but encourages employer and employee to discuss their

problems in a friendly spirit and, if necessary, to make mutual concessions. 

3. Investigation.-Some outside person or group of persons may attempt to investigate and ascertain the causes of the dispute. The investigators usually publish reports and make recommendations as to the best method of settling the dispute. It is hoped that, after the facts are known, public opinion will force a fair settlement. 

4. Arbitration.-Arbitration is the submission of the dispute to some impartial board or court. The disputants may submit their case voluntarily (voluntary arbitration) or the law may compel them to do so (compulsory arbitration). Compliance with the decision of the arbitrator (which is called the award) may also be compulsory or voluntary.

The Government and Industrial Warfare.-The Federal 

Government and many of the state governments have developed elaborate facilities for the settlement of labor disputes. A large number of special agencies have been created for this purpose and attempts are made to settle disputes by the various methods explained above. In spite of this, many strikes occur and there seems to be no immediate prospect of their reduction or elimination.

There is another type of governmental legislation which may be more fruitful in the elimination of industrial warfare. This is legislation which will remove the causes of the dissatisfaction which really creates industrial warfare. For example, most states have laws which require that the employer furnish safe and sanitary working conditions. Many other states regulate the labor of women and children. Workmen's compensation (payment to workers who have been injured at their work, and the provision of free medical attention) has been established in forty-two states.

In the last few years the Federal Government has passed a great deal of legislation which is intended to improve the conditions of the workingman. Minimum wages and maximum hours were prescribed by the NRA codes, and later by the Fair Labor 

Standards Act of 1938 (page 322). The Wagner-Connery Act, as described above, guarantees workers the right to collective bargaining. The Social Security Act establishes unemployment insurance and old-age insurance.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

It is hoped that legislation of this sort will increase the well. being and the security of the great masses of wage-earners and will leave them no reason for resorting to the wasteful methods of industrial warfare.

Summary.-Workers, in an attempt to overcome the individual worker's lack of bargaining power, have formed unions. 

These organizations carry on collective bargaining with the employers. If the points at issue cannot be settled by collective bargaining, industrial warfare may follow. This results in losses to employers, employees and the public. The government attempts to avoid or to end industrial warfare by investigation, conciliation, or arbitration.

CHAPTER XXI PUBLIC UTILITIES PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain what is meant by a “public utility.” 

2. To explain why certain industries are considered public utilities. 

3. To summarize the history of the regulation of public utilities. 

Definition. A public utility is any industry which is so affected with the public interest as to require a greater degree of governmental regulation than is commonly applied to other industries. Leading public utilities in the United States are railroads, street-car lines and subways, telephone and telegraph companies, and gas, electric and water supply companies. It is possible that in the future other industries will be regarded and treated as public utilities. 

Importance of Public Utilities. It is estimated that the value of the property owned by industries which are considered public utilities (exclusive of the railroads) is between five and six per cent of the total national wealth. These same industries contribute between four and five per cent of the total income of the country. It is further estimated that four per cent of all the expenditures made in the country are for communication, light, water, and local transportation, all of which are public utilities. There is practically no one in the country who is not served by public utilities. 

Reasons for Considering Certain Industries Public Utilities. 

-Why does the government consider certain industries public utilities and regulate them accordingly, while other industries are not subject to such regulation? 

1. Natural Monopolies.-As we explained in our discussion of monopolies (page 121), certain types of businesses are nat- 277

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

ural monopolies. Gas and electric companies, and firms which supply water and telephone service fall in this class. Once businesses of this type are established, it is extremely unlikely that any competition will enter the field. The great cost of establishing a competing enterprise and the prospect of a long and bitter fight with the original company make it an extremely unattractive investment.

If the owners of these businesses were not regulated, they would have all the advantages of a monopoly position. They would be able to exploit consumers by charging high monopoly prices and by providing inferior service. Because of this possibility, the government considers the industries mentioned public utilities and regulates them closely. 

2. Public Interest.-Another reason why the government may consider certain industries public utilities is that they are clothed with a special public interest. For example, water companies are considered public utilities not only because they are natural monopolies but also because the public's need for a plentiful supply of cheap pure water is so great that the government feels it must regulate the various enterprises that supply the water. 

3. Special Privileges.-The government feels that it is justified in considering certain industries public utilities and in regulating them closely because it has granted these industries special privileges. (a) The government exercises its power of eminent domain in behalf of certain businesses. This refers to the government's power to take property from its owner if it is considered necessary for the public interest and if the owner is adequately compensated. The government exercises this power, for example, when it enables street railway companies to obtain land for trackage, barns, power plants and transmission plants. (b) The government has made extremely valuable gifts and subsidies to some industries. For example, the builders of the transcontinental railroads were aided with great grants of public lands. (c) The government grants franchises to certain businesses. A franchise is a special permit or privilege to engage in certain business activities which would otherwise not be permitted. For example, a street car company must receive a franchise before it is allowed to run its cars on a public street.

PUBLIC UTILITIES 

279 

REGULATION OF PUBLIC UTILITIES 

Periods of Regulation.-Professor C. W. Fackler has divided the history of public utility regulation into the following four well-defined periods. 

1. In the period between 1800 and 1865, the industries which are now considered public utilities were, generally speaking, not regulated at all. 

2. The period between 1865 and 1895 was marked by ineffective steps toward regulation. In 1876, the United States 

Supreme Court (Munn vs. Illinois) recognized the principle of governmental regulation. This encouraged the creation of public service commissions in several states, but their powers were almost entirely of an advisory nature. 

3. In the period from 1895 to 1917, the powers of the public service commissions were expanded. Their attention was focussed, for the most part, on the rates which the public utilities were charging. 

4. In the period since 1917, we have seen (a) a further extension of the activities and powers of the state public service commissions, and (b) the growth of public ownership. Most of the roads and bridges, and the postal system have been publicly owned and managed since the early days of our country. In recent years, a number of cities have acquired gas and electric plants, street-car lines and subways, and sell these services to their citizens. In addition, a majority of the water-supply systems in the country are now publicly owned. 

In the Tennessee River Valley, the Federal Government has set up an extensive project (the TVA) which is supplying cheap electric power to the surrounding region. 

In some cases, the competition or threat of competition from government-owned enterprises has forced the private utilities to reduce their rates. For example, in 1935, Mayor La Guardia of New York City obtained a considerable reduction in electric rates in his city by threatening to erect a $15,000,000 power plant to be operated by the city in competition with the private power companies. * Walter E. Spahr, ed., Economic Principles and Problems, Vol. II, pp. 

35-37. New York: Ray Long and Richard R. Smith, Inc., 1932.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Extension of Regulation.—It is apparent from this outline that regulation of public utilities in our country has grown steadily more thorough and more stringent. In addition to development along this line, there has been a tendency to extend the status of public utility to industries which previously were not considered in that category.

An example of this can be seen in the case of grain elevators, which originally functioned as ordinary business enterprises and now are regarded and treated as public utilities in almost all the grain-producing states. Another example is the bituminous coal industry, which was practically made a public utility by the Guffey-Snyder Coal Act of 1935. This law declared that the bituminous coal industry is "affected with a national public interest," and the Federal Government undertook to regulate it accordingly. The regulation covered such matters as wages, hours of labor, trade practices, minimum prices charged for the product, quotas of production, and labor disputes. In May, 1936, the Act was declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. However, the tendency which the law represented remains highly significant. 

These changes are a response to changes in social and economic conditions. Methods and degrees of regulation which are suited to the needs of one generation are found inadequate by the next. This implies, of course, that we may look forward to additional changes in the future. Professor Rexford G. Tugwell has suggested, for example, that the production and distribution of milk be made a public utility. Whether or not this and similar innovations are desirable can be decided only by careful study and consideration.

Regulation of Rates. Although there has been a limited amount of governmental competition, the great bulk of public utilities in the United States are privately owned and managed. 

We have centered our efforts on regulating the rates which these private companies charge, rather than on supplanting them with government ownership.

If the government is to regulate the rates of public utilities, it must have some basis for determining whether or not a rate is reasonable. What bases do we have for making such judgments?

PUBLIC UTILITIES 

281 The first thing that can be said is that rates should be high enough to allow companies to pay adequate returns to bondholders and stockholders, to meet operating expenses, and to amass reserves to cover depreciation costs. These charges represent the actual cost of producing the service, and if a community wants any type of good, it must be prepared to pay enough to warrant its production.

However, this gives only the lower limit below which rates, in the long run, must not fall if the company is to continue in operation. The problem in utility rate-making has been, generally, to set the upper limit of the rates charged, or to prevent exploitation of the consumers.

The United States Supreme Court decided in the case of Smyth v. Ames that the reasonableness of a rate is to be determined by the value of the property that the business uses in producing the service. For example, a company whose property has a value of $1,000,000 is entitled to a greater net profit (not rate of profit) than a company whose property is valued. at only $500,000. In either case, the rates should be adjusted so that the company can earn a "fair" return on the capital invested.

Bases of Evaluation. This decision introduced the problem of evaluating the property on which the rates were to be based. 

Several different methods of doing this have been proposed. 

1. Original Cost.-This method evaluates the property at the same figure that it cost to build or to acquire it. The weakness of this method is that costs of production and property values change considerably from time to time. 

2. Evaluation as a Going Concern.-It has been argued that the value of a business cannot be determined merely from the value of its physical assets. Such matters as the good will of the community, privileges obtained from the state (as franchises), and possibilities for future expansion must be considered. In other words the business should be evaluated as a going concern. In practice, however, it is often extremely difficult to determine the dollar-and-cents value of these intangible factors. 

3. Market Value of Securities.-It has been proposed that the value of a business be determined from the market value of

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

its securities. It is argued that the prices at which these securities are actually bought and sold reflect the value which society places on the business. The weakness in this theory is the fact that the market prices of securities depend largely upon profits, which arise, in turn, from the rates which the company charges. Therefore, the determination of values for the purpose of setting equitable rates in the future would depend on rates charged in the past. This is an instance of "circular" reasoning. 

EVALUATION|| A DIFFICULT PROBLEM GOING CONCERN ORIGINAL COST BUSINESS ENTERPRISE COSTS OF REPRODUCTION MARKET VALUE OF SECURITIES 

Why doesn't the government find it necessary to evaluate department stores? 

4. Reproduction Costs.-Another proposal is that a business be evaluated by the money it would cost to reproduce it. The great merit of this method is that it establishes the value of the business on present-day conditions, on what it would cost to replace it today. Its weakness is that prices at any given time may be abnormally high or subnormally low as compared to the long-term trend of prices. Thus, too high or too low a value might be set upon the business.

The courts have been favorably disposed toward this method of evaluation, particularly if proper adjustments have been

PUBLIC UTILITIES 

283 made to cover the depreciation which the property has suffered since it was built. This attitude was expressed by the United 

States Supreme Court in the O'Fallon Case (1929). However, no single method of evaluation has been found thoroughly satisfactory.

The TVA-a "Yardstick."-One of the purposes of the TVA is to serve as a "yardstick" by which the reasonableness of the rates charged by private concerns can be measured. The gov- 

ELECTRIC RATES CONSUMER UTILITY OWNER TVA-A YARDSTICK FOR ELECTRIC RATES 

What other purposes may TVA serve besides acting as a yardstick? ernment believes that after it has determined at what price the TVA can profitably produce and sell electric power, it will have some indication of the rates which will yield a fair profit to private producers in that part of the country. In this way, it is hoped that the tangle of evaluation theories which we have described may be avoided. 

There are some indications that "yardsticks" for measuring the reasonableness of utility rates may be established in other parts of the country.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

Holding Companies.—In the last twenty-five years in the

United States, there has been a marked tendency toward the consolidation of utility companies, especially companies producing electric power, into a comparatively small number of large organizations. In 1917, approximately 6500 generating and distributing systems served the United States. Today, the number has fallen off to 3500, although the amount of business transacted has increased greatly. In 1934, 215 of these 3500 networks supplied 93% of all the electricity manufactured for public use in this country.

Most of these great enterprises have been built up by acquiring a controlling share of the stocks of companies which were formerly independent. Such combinations are known as holding companies (page 125).

Holding companies have become an important public problem for the following reasons: (1) The subsidiaries are required to pay "service fees" to the controlling holding company. 

Critics of present conditions maintain that these fees are not paid because the holding companies render any useful services, but merely because they possess financial control. Ultimately the burden falls on the consuming public, for the fees are considered one of the costs of production which must be covered by the rates. (2) Holding companies are often interstate in character. For this reason it is difficult for the states to exercise any effective control over them. In many cases, states and holding companies have become involved in long and costly litigation. 

The legal expenses which the utilities incurred in this way were also considered in the costs of operation which the rates are supposed to cover. Therefore, the public paid for both sides of these legal controversies. (It paid the government's legal expenses through taxes.) (3) Holding companies make possible a concentration of financial and industrial power. Some people believe that this condition is opposed to the interests of the public and to traditional American ideals. 

Public Utility Holding Company Act.-The Roosevelt Ad ministration advanced the above arguments and proposed legislation which would eliminate all "unnecessary" holding companies, that is combinations which did not promote cheap and efficient service. (It is admitted that in some cases holding

PUBLIC UTILITIES 

285 companies may make possible coördination among units which, from an engineering point of view, should form a single integrated system.)

The great utility companies fought this legislation bitterly, pointing out that the average rates charged for electricity have dropped steadily since the World War. Therefore, they claimed, it is obvious that on the whole the growth of holding companies has not injured the public interest.

In 1935, Congress passed a law which is generally considered a compromise measure. It requires the eventual dissolution of only the more objectionable types of holding companies (those more than twice removed from the operating companies). 

In addition, the law provided for more stringent Federal regulation of those utility companies which are engaged in interstate commerce. Previously, as we indicated above, almost all such regulation of public utilities had been carried on by the public service commissions of the various states. The enforcement of this law is lodged in the Securities and Exchange Commission, which we have previously mentioned in connection with the stock market.

Communications Act (1934).—Another field in which changing conditions have caused an extension of Federal authority is that of communications. The Communications Act, passed in 

1934, empowered Congress to regulate interstate and foreign communication services by telephone, telegraph, cable and radio. 

The Federal Government previously had possessed some power in this field but the Communications Act expanded its authority. 

Congress considered such authority necessary to protect the public welfare, just as it is necessary in the field of transportation and electric power. For example, the Federal Communications 

Commission (created by the law) is empowered to allocate broadcasting frequencies. If the Commission did not have this power, several stations in the same locality might broadcast on the same frequency. This would result in the greatest confusion and would harm the public and the radio industry alike. 

Public Ownership. The extension of public ownership in the public utility field is one of the big issues before the country today. The advocates of public ownership maintain that the services concerned are so essential to the public welfare that

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

they ought not to be left in the hands of private concerns which are interested primarily in profits. They point out the various ways in which investors and consumers have been exploited and claim that these abuses can be eliminated by government-owned enterprises which will be interested only in serving the public, not in making fortunes for the big stockholders. 

The opponents of government ownership advance the following arguments: (1) Experience has shown that government ownership leads to graft, political favoritism, and general inefficiency and corruption. (2) Advocates of government ownership tend to emphasize the occasional abuses of our privately owned utilities and to overlook its great merits. On the whole, the people have been served at reasonable rates with a high degree of efficiency. For example, our telephone system is generally admitted to be the most efficient in the world. (3) An extension of public ownership is opposed to our traditional American individualism and might lead to Socialism. 

There are no defects in the present utility set-up which cannot be eliminated by intelligent and moderate regulation. 1 (4) Summary.-Public utilities, since they are natural monopolies and provide services essential to the public welfare, are regulated by the government. The purpose of this regulation is to insure efficient service and a fair rate. The determination of a fair rate has created the problem of evaluation. Because of the difficulties involved in this, the government intends to use TVA and other similar projects as a "yardstick" to measure the reasonableness of rates. Some people believe that government ownership should supplant the various private public utility companies.

CHAPTER XXII TRANSPORTATION PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To discuss the need for transportation. 

2. To describe the various stages of transportation in the United States. 

3. To discuss the characteristics of railroads which have made them a special problem. 

4. To trace the course of railroad legislation in the United States. 

Transportation, a Public Utility. The various facilities which are used for transportation are among the most important public utilities in our country. These facilities are either publicly owned, as roads, bridges, and many local street-car lines, or they are extensively regulated by the government. In this chapter we wish to trace some of the history of the development of transportation in the United States and to discuss the various economic factors and problems involved in this development. 

The Function of Transportation. The function of trans portation is to move people and goods to places where they will be better able to satisfy human needs and desires. In other words, transportation creates place utility (page 23). For example, woolen cloth is worth more and has a greater power to satisfy human wants after it has been moved from the woolen mills of New England to the clothing factories of New York. 

Similarly, the finished suits of clothing have greater utility when they have been distributed to the ultimate consumers.

The Growing Need for Transportation.In the days of 

Mark Overton, when a family produced almost all the things that it consumed, there was comparatively little need for transportation. Self-sufficiency of this sort, however, is a thing of the past. Our modern economic life, as we have explained, is char- 289

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

acterized by large-scale production and territorial division of labor. This means, in most cases, that the producer must obtain his raw materials from distant sources and, after his goods have been produced, he must sell them to consumers who are scattered all over the country.

Stages of Transportation. This growing need for transportation is reflected in the history of the transportation facilities of our country. 

1. Trails and Waterways.-Transportation was first accomplished in this country by the use of trails and waterways. Men paddled their canoes across lakes and rivers. Indian trails and portages provided a means of overland transportation even before the white race set foot on this continent. This was a slow and laborious method of transportation and only a very limited amount of freight could be moved from place to place. 

2. Roads and Turnpikes.-The second stage of transporta tion, which began late in the eighteenth century, was the development of roads and turnpikes. Some of these roads were constructed by widening and improving old Indian trails. The road which parallels the Wabash River from Vincennes to Lafayette, Indiana, was originally a trail. Other roads did not have this origin but were built by the white man to meet a definite need of the white man. Horses, wagons and stagecoaches were used on these early roads. 

3. Canals. Early in the nineteenth century, a large number of canals were built in this country. The canals connected important waterways (lakes and rivers) and greatly facilitated the transportation of goods. For example, the Erie Canal, built in 1825, connected Lake Erie with the Hudson River and made possible cheap transportation from the Great Lakes region to the Atlantic coast. The great disadvantage of this type of transportation was that the waterways were locked by ice in the wintertime. 

4. Railroads.-The next great step forward in transportation came with the development of the railroad, which first appeared about 1830. Railroad transportation was far more speedy than transportation by water and was not subject to so many interruptions because of weather conditions.

TRANSPORTATION 

291 

CANALS AND RIVERS ROADS & TURNPIKES TRAILS 

AND

WATERWAYS MAN CONQUERS SPACE STAGES OF TRANSPORTATION RAILROADS INTERNAL COMBUSTION MOTOR 

Will the same persistence and intelligence which conquered space enable us to conquer unemployment?

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

5. The Internal Combustion Engine.-This engine, which is used to power automobiles, airplanes and the new "zephyr" trains, became of great importance in the twentieth century. 

It provides economical, speedy and convenient transportation. 

RAILROADS Characteristics of Railroads.-The transportation problem in this country has centered about the railroads. There are several features of the railroads which have made them a special social problem and have brought about governmental regulation. 

1. A railroad is, by its very nature, a monopoly. Once a road has been built, in most cases (although by no means in all cases) competition will not appear. The great expense of building a competing line and the certainty of a long and bitter fight with the original road discourage the entry of such competition. 

2. There is a great social need for transportation. Under modern conditions, practically no one is self-sufficient and our individual and national well-being requires efficient transportation facilities. 

3. The fixed charges of railroads are very high. Fixed charges, as we have seen, refer to costs which do not fall even though the volume of business transacted decreases greatly. The principal item in the high fixed charges of the railroads is the interest payments on the bonds which have been issued to obtain the large amounts of capital funds necessary to establish a railroad.

To make profits in the face of high fixed charges, the railroads must do a great volume of business (pages 49-50). In their efforts to increase their volume of business (in proportion to the burden of fixed charges), many railroads resorted to consolidations and combinations of competing lines, and also to certain practices which were designed to favor the more important shippers at the expense of the smaller shippers.

For all these reasons, the government has subjected the railroads to a great deal of regulation in order to root out abuses and to safeguard the interests of the great masses of the people whom the railroads serve. For the most part, this regulation has centered about the rates which the railroads charge.

TRANSPORTATION 

293 

How Railroad Rates Are Determined. There are very defi nite limits within which railroad rates must fall. They cannot rise so high that the total cost to the shipper exceeds the additional value which the transportation gives to his commodity. 

A Florida orange-grower will gladly pay some freight charges to have his crop transported to New York where it has a greater value than in Florida, but he cannot pay more than he gains from the added value of the fruit. On the other hand, railroads must charge rates which will at least cover the cost of maintenance and operation. The diagram below will make this clear. 

VALUE ADDED TO THE PRODUCT THE CHARGES WILL FALL SOMEWHERE ALONG THE DIAGONAL LINE UPPER 

LIMIT 

MAINTENANCE AND OPERATION COSTS LOWER 

LIMIT The charges cannot be greater than the value added to the product. The attempt of the railroads to set the charges as near as possible to this upper limit is called charging what the traffic will bear.

On the other hand, the charges for transporting the oranges cannot be so low that the railroad would be losing money, because continued losses will cause the road to suspend operations.

In practice, it is extremely difficult to determine how much it costs a railroad to transport one bale of goods or one passenger because the train is carrying other freight and passengers at the same time. The expense of equipping and running the train must be met, whether or not another piece of freight is added. 

This is known as the principle of joint costs. However, the total income from charges must at least cover the total expense. 

The point on the diagonal where the charges will fall is determined by such factors as competition, the demand for transportation, and governmental regulation. Governmental regu-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

lation came about largely because roads charged exorbitant rates in their efforts to pile up profits. 

Factors Determining Costs.-Although it is difficult to say how much a particular shipment of freight costs a railroad, we can distinguish several variable factors which influence the cost of shipment under a given set of conditions. By a "given set of conditions," we mean the general financial and industrial position of the railroad. This includes the fixed costs, the operating costs (labor, fuel, etc.), the volume of traffic on the road, etc.

The first consideration is the nature of what is being shipped. 

Other things being equal, greater bulk and weight mean greater costs. In addition, articles which require special care, such as fruits which must travel in refrigerated cars, cost more to ship. 

Another factor is that of distance. Under a given set of conditions, it is only natural that it costs more to ship something 1000 miles than 500 miles. In this connection, however, we must consider the influence of terminal charges. This term refers to the cost of loading and unloading the freight. Now, it is clear that it will cost the same to load and unload the freight if it travels 500 miles or 1000 miles. Therefore, the total cost of shipping something 500 miles may be almost as great as the cost of shipping it 1000 miles.

It may be said, then, that on the whole transportation charges for different shipments on a given railroad should vary consistently with the nature of the articles, the distance, and the terminal charges. However, railroads in the past have taken advantage of their monopoly position and various abuses and discriminatory practices have appeared. Abuses of this sort, together with a generally exorbitant level of rates, caused a clamor of popular protest which brought about governmental regulation.

GOVERNMENTAL REGULATION 

The Granger Laws.-Prior to 1887, many of the states of the Middle-West had passed laws regulating the rates and practices of the railroads. These were known as Granger Laws and were administered by state boards or commissions. However, the Supreme Court decided, in the case of the Wabash Railroad

TRANSPORTATION 

295 v. Illinois, that the states had no authority to regulate railroads engaged in interstate commerce. The need for Federal regulation of the railroads was clearly indicated. 

The Interstate Commerce Commission.-The Interstate Com merce Act was passed by the Federal Government in 1887. This law created an Interstate Commerce Commission, which was authorized originally to check certain abuses in the administration of the railroads. One of these abuses was discrimination among various shippers. This meant that the railroads favored one shipper with a low rate, while another shipper (usually a smaller shipper) had to pay a high rate for an equivalent service. A common method of practicing discrimination was to make refunds or rebates to the favored shipper. This meant, of course, that such shippers paid less for their transportation and had a great advantage over their competitors.

Another type of unfair practice which had become quite common was the "long- and short-haul" abuse. This meant that a railroad charged more for short hauls in regions where there was no competition than they did for long hauls under competitive conditions. In other words, they did not base their charges uniformly on distance, terminal costs, and the nature of the article shipped, but reduced their rates where competition forced them to do so and charged exorbitant rates where they enjoyed a monopoly position. As a result of this discrimination, a farmer who lived in a competitive district might be able to undersell a farmer who lived in a non-competitive district. 

The Commission was authorized to check this practice except in particular instances in which circumstances justified it. For example, competition in a certain region may have forced a railroad to reduce rates below a level which would yield a fair profit. This does not mean that a railroad can or should reduce rates proportionately along its entire line. Under these circumstances, a road might justifiably charge less for a long haul in the competitive district than for a short haul in the noncompetitive district.

This law also forbade pooling. Pooling is a type of agreement among competing business enterprises in which they set uniform prices which they will all charge and divide the market for their product. Pooling was common in the railroad industry

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

prior to 1887 and it strengthened the trend to monopoly which already existed in the industry. 

The Interstate Commerce Act was a great step forward but it gradually became evident that the Interstate Commerce Commission did not possess sufficient power to regulate the railroads effectively in the public interest. In subsequent years a considerable number of laws were passed which expanded the powers of the Commission.

Present Powers of the Commission.-As a result of this steady expansion of its authority, the Interstate Commerce Commission has become one of the most important and powerful of the many extra-legislative bodies created by the Federal Government. The Commission controls not only railroads, but many other agencies of interstate commerce, such as express companies, sleeping car companies, pipe lines and, with certain limitations, trucks and busses, and steamship lines. However, the regulation of railroads remains the most important of the Commission's functions and it is in this field that its authority is most comprehensive. The Commission has the following powers: 

1. Rates.-The Commission has the power to fix the rates which the railroads charge. It attempts to avoid unreasonably high rates and rates which are so low that the roads will not be able to earn a fair profit. 

2. Service. The Commission enforces certain standards of service. This involves such matters as schedules, heating trains, facilities for passenger comfort, etc. 

3. Coöperation.-The Commission requires that the railroads work together for the best interests of the public. For example, cars may be transferred from one company which has no immediate use for them to another company which does have such use. Railroads may be forced to accommodate passengers by providing convenient connections at junction points. 

4. Discrimination.-The Commission enforces a ban on free passes, rebates, and unjustifiably discriminatory rates to shippers and places. 

5. Expansion.-The permission of the Commission must be obtained before a railroad can undertake the construction of a new line. Such permission is also necessary if a railroad wishes

TRANSPORTATION 

297 to abandon an unprofitable line. In all such cases, the railroad must prove that its action is justified by the public interest. 

6. Mergers. All railroad mergers or pooling agreements must be approved by the Commission. 

7. Intrastate Rates.-The jurisdiction of the Commission, properly, is over interstate commerce. However, the Commission may set rates for intrastate railroad transportation (transportation within a single state) when these rates affect interstate transportation. For example, if an intrastate railroad were to charge excessively high rates, this would discourage industrial activity within the state and might have a disastrous effect on the business of interstate railroads.

The Railroads during the War. The policy of the government and of the Interstate Commerce Commission prior to the World War was designed to preserve and to sharpen competition. Therefore, the railroads did not form a coördinated and integrated system when the United States entered the War. It was necessary for the Federal Government to step in and operate all the railroads as a unit in order to speed movements of men and materials. The railroads continued under government operation until March, 1920.

The Transportation Act of 1920.--This Act returned the railroads to private ownership and operation. However, the law provided for coöperative action among the roads and it forecast the consolidation of all our roads into a few large systems. 

Under this law the Interstate Commerce Commission was given the authority to establish rates which would enable the roads to earn a fair profit. In this connection, the problem of evaluation, which we considered in Chapter XXI, became of great importance.

The Act also provided that if a road earned over six per cent in profits (which was considered a "fair" return) half of such excess profits were to be taken over or "recaptured" by the Interstate Commerce Commission. The money was to be used to establish a fund from which the weaker roads could borrow. 

The "recapture" clause was repealed in 1933. 

Labor Relations Legislation.—In recent years, several laws have been passed concerning the relations between railroad owners and their employees. The Watson-Parker Act of 1926

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

provided for the settlement of labor disputes in the railroad industry by means of conferences, mediation, arbitration or investigation, which was to be carried on by a board appointed by the President of the United States. The Crosser-Dill Act of 

1934 expanded the facilities for peaceful settlement of disputes in the railroad industry.

Financial Difficulties of the Railroads. For a number of years, the railroads of this country have been in a deplorable financial condition. There were several causes of these difficulties. (1) The roads suffered from the heavy fixed charges mentioned above. (2) The competition from busses, airplanes and trucks was reflected in a decreased volume of business. (3) Competition among various roads resulted in wasteful duplication of facilities and in ruinous rate reductions. 

The general business depression, which came in 1929, intensified the difficulties of the railroad. 

The Railroad Reorganization Act. The Federal Govern ment passed this law in 1933 in an effort to solve some of the problems of the railroads. The act created the office of Federal 

Coördinator of Transportation. This officer was authorized to take measures to avoid duplication of services, promote financial reorganization, improve credit facilities, and reduce fixed charges if the public interest justified it. The anti-trust laws were to be set aside whenever the proper application of the 

Act required it.

The office of Coördinator of Transportation was terminated in June, 1936. However, the Railroad Reorganization Act, as well as the Transportation Act of 1920, indicates clearly that today the government is concerned with efficient and profitable operation of railroads, rather than with the preservation of competition. It seems probable that the day will come when all the important railroads of the country will be in the hands of a few large systems under the direct supervision of the authorities of the Federal Government. Indeed, a number of authorities, among them Joseph B. Eastman, former Federal 

Coördinator of Transportation, believe that the day will ultimately come when the Federal Government will be obliged to take over the railroads and operate them as a government monopoly.

TRANSPORTATION 

299 

Attempts to Combat Competition. As we indicated above, there has been a tremendous increase in the number of busses, trucks, and airplanes, and this has resulted in a decrease in the business of the railroads. The railroads have attempted to meet this competition by improving the service which they offer the public. Many railroads have installed air-conditioned cars, which greatly increase the comfort of travelling in warm weather. FIXED 

CHARGES FEDERAL AID IMPROVED RAILROAD SERVICE COMPETITION OF AUTOMOBILES TRUCKS BUSSES AIRPLANES AMERICAN RAILROAD SYSTEM HELP NEEDED RAILROAD MANAGEMENT 

Do you think that government ownership would solve the railroad problem?

Streamlined trains, powered by Diesel engines, have been widely adopted and have increased the speed of railroad service. 

In addition, many railroad lines have acquired or established airplane and bus systems, and fleets of trucks. In 1935, Congress adopted legislation which applied governmental regulation to busses and trucks engaged in interstate transportation. The legislation provides for maximum and minimum rates, regulation of wages, etc.

CHAPTER XXIII TARIFFS PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain what is meant by a tariff. 

2. To describe the different types of tariffs. 

3. To discuss the arguments for and against a protective tariff. 

4. To examine the tariff policy of the United States. 

Definition. A tariff is a tax levied upon goods which are imported into or exported out of a country. In the United States we levy duties only upon imports, since export tariffs are forbidden by our Constitution.

Kinds of Tariffs.-Tariffs are classified according to the manner in which the goods are taxed. If the tax is imposed according to the number of tons or bales, the tariff is said to be specific. If the tax is based on the money value of the goods, the tariff is said to be ad valorem.

Tariffs are also classified according to the purpose for which they are levied. A revenue tariff is levied in order to raise funds to meet the expenses of government. A protective tariff is intended to protect the industries of a country from the damaging effects of foreign competition. Suppose, for example, that a foreign shoe manufacturer has such low production costs that he can undersell an American manufacturer, even with the added expense of transportation. In such a case, a tariff levied on shoes would protect the American manufacturer, because it would add to the expenses of the foreign competitor, and force him to charge a higher price for shoes sold in this country. 

Arguments for a Protective Tariff.-One of the most debated questions in the field of economics is whether protection or free trade (the elimination of protective tariffs) is a more de- 302

TARIFFS 

303 sirable policy. The following arguments have been advanced for protection: 

1. "Infant Industries" Argument.-Infant industries are those which have been recently set up, and are still in an undeveloped condition. Such industries will find it difficult to compete with well-established and efficient producers in other countries. Protective tariffs will enable them to grow and to establish themselves without interference by stronger foreign firms. Many industries in the United States and in Germany have grown up behind the shelter of tariff walls. 

2. "Diversification of Industries" Argument. Under a pol icy of protection, many industries will exist which could not survive if the tariffs were eliminated. In this way, a protective tariff makes possible a diversified industrial development. 

The advantage of this is that a nation does not depend upon a single industry for its prosperity, and will not face disaster if any single industry declines in importance. Another advantage is that a nation which can produce all the things, or almost all the things, which it needs, is better equipped in case of war. 

Such a nation does not face the danger of being cut off from sources of vitally important supplies. 

3. "Protection of Labor" Argument.-This argument ap plies particularly to such countries as the United States, where wages are relatively high. It is argued that wages in some foreign countries are so much lower than they are here that, if protective tariffs did not stand in the way, foreign manufacturers would be able to flood our market with low-priced goods. In order to meet this competition, American producers would be forced to cut wages, and this would destroy the American standard of living.

It is also argued that the many industries which are supported by tariffs provide a great deal of employment for labor. 

If such industries were exposed to the destructive forces of foreign competition, these men might be thrown out of work. 

4. "Home Market" Argument.-Under a protective tariff, the home market is reserved for domestic manufacturers and for domestic labor. It is argued that this is a good thing, because the home market is to be preferred to foreign markets where transportation costs and risks, the threats of war, and other dis-

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

turbances, make business less stable and profitable. This argument implies that foreign trade should be reduced to a minimum. 

Arguments against a Protective Tariff.-In spite of the above arguments, there has been, and still is, a considerable group of economists and business men, who favor free trade. 

They advance the following arguments: 

1. "Natural" Distribution of Production.-All nations have advantages which make it easier for them to produce certain commodities, rather than others. These advantages may be in the form of raw materials, a skilled labor force, or an accumulation of efficient capital goods. It is argued that each nation ought to concentrate its productive efforts on what it is best fitted to produce, and obtain the other commodities it needs by foreign trade. In this way the maximum amount of wealth will be produced and in the long run every nation will gain. A protective tariff works against such a "natural" distribution of production. It provides artificial encouragement for the use of capital and labor in fields for which the nation is not so well fitted as it is for certain others. Thus the total productivity of the nation is decreased and, ultimately, the living standards of all classes must suffer.

The free traders illustrate this argument with the example of our great automobile industry. In spite of the fact that 

American automobile manufacturers pay much higher wages than foreign producers, this industry has never asked for tariff protection. The reason is that American manufacturers enjoy certain advantages (an adequate supply of skilled and unskilled labor, extensive supplies of raw materials, highly efficient machinery and methods, etc.) which make it impossible for foreigners to compete with them. The comparatively high wages paid American workers are more than offset by the greater productivity of the individual worker brought about by these advantages.

Free traders claim, in other words, that the development of an unprotected automobile industry in this country is an example of "natural" distribution of production. It is only when we are attempting to produce goods for which other nations are better fitted that manufacturers will need protection to compete with foreign merchandise.

TARIFFS 

305 2. Reluctance to Give up Protection.-Even though it may be advisable to protect infant industries, experience has shown that once protection is granted to an industry, it becomes extremely reluctant ever to give it up. We have in this country, today, a number of industries which were granted protection in their "infancy" and now, in an advanced stage of development, refuse to stand on their own feet but use all possible influence to have the protection continued. 

EFFECTS OF THE TARIFF|| TWO POINTS OF VIEW 

HIGH PRICES FOREIGN COMPETITION HOME INDUSTRY LABOR PUBLIC HIGH STANDARD HIGH WAGES VIEW OF THE PROTECTIONIST FOREIGN COMPETITION HOME INDUSTRY LABOR PUBLIC VIEW OF FREE TRADER 

Is it possible that the tariff may have both effects? Explain. 

3. Increased Prices to Consumer.-Free traders point out that protective tariffs raise the cost of living for the consumer. 

As a general thing, this is obviously true. Suppose, for example, that American producers can manufacture an article to sell for $1.50, while foreign producers can afford to sell it for $1.00.

A tariff which succeeded in protecting American producers would necessarily force consumers to buy the American article, that is, pay $1.50.

The situation becomes more serious if the tariff is a high one and the American producer enjoys a monopoly or semi-monopoly

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

position in the home market. Suppose that the tariff in the preceding example was $1.00 per unit. If the American producer had no domestic competition, he could raise the price of his article not merely to $1.50 (which we have assumed would yield him a fair profit) but to $2.00. It is the contention of many free traders (and of some students of the question who are not entirely committed to a free trade policy) that our tariff schedules, at times, have made possible this sort of exploitation of the consuming public.

On the other hand, there are certain circumstances under which a protective tariff will not add greatly to the price which the public must pay. Suppose, for example, that the unit production costs of American manufacturers are only slightly higher than the costs of European manufacturers of the same commodity. If the tariff is set so that it just equalizes this advantage, American producers will not be able to charge exorbitant prices. If they attempt to do so, consumers will be able to purchase European goods. Under these conditions, the price of the commodity in question will be little, if at all, higher than if free trade existed. (This will be particularly true if lively competition prevails among the American producers.) 

On the whole, however, there can be no doubt that the effect of a protective tariff, such as we have in this country, is to raise the cost of living substantially. Protectionists admit this, but they maintain that the advantages which result from the tariff more than offset the added strain on the consumer's pocketbook. 

The Tariff Policy of the United States.-Arguments for, and arguments against-which is one to believe? This is another question which cannot be answered with finality. The wisdom of a protective tariff has been debated for generations and it is a lively issue, even today.

The history of the United States tariff policy, with its many changes, reflects the disagreement which exists on this important question. The chart on the following page summarizes the more important information:

We can see from this table that, in spite of constant revision, protective tariffs have been the rule in our country. Republican administrations have raised the rates to high levels. Democratic administrations have generally attempted to lower the rates,

TARIFFS 

307 but they have always been advanced again when the Republicans returned to power. Our great industrial development has been made behind the shelter of tariff walls. This in itself, of course, does not mean that our best interests will be served by maintain-

It does mean, however, that rapid elimination or drastic reduction of our tariffs would be a very disturbing force on our economic structure because this structure has come to depend on the protection afforded by tariffs.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS Principle of the United States Tariff Policy.-The United 

States tariff policy, today, is based on the principle that duties should be so adjusted that they make up for the differences between costs of production in the United States and costs of production in competing foreign countries. To keep this adjustment as accurate as possible the United States Tariff Commission was established in 1916. This body of experts studies production costs in foreign countries, and other matters relating to the tariff, and submits its findings to Congress and to the President of the United States. Whenever the data gathered by the Tariff Commission indicate a change in production costs in foreign countries, the President is authorized to raise or lower any tariff rate by as much as 50%.

The Tariff and Our Economic Life.-What effect has this predominantly protectionist tariff policy had on our national economic life? Does the record of history support the contentions of the protectionists? Does it support those of the free traders? 

1. Protectionist Arguments.-The argument that tariffs serve to protect "infant industries" has been borne out in many instances. In recent years, for example, tariff protection has made possible the production of magnesium (a metal) in this country. European producers have been established in this field for many years and the new American industry would not have been able to meet their competition without the assistance of a tariff.

The tariff has made possible a more diversified industrial development. We have seen for example, that the magnesium industry would not exist without the tariff. In the same way, tariffs have made us more nearly self-subsistent. 

Since high protective tariffs have succeeded in keeping out foreign products, they have helped to reserve the home market for American goods. In this way foreign trade has been greatly hampered.

Under our protective policy, American workers have been paid comparatively high wages. As we have seen, however, high wages have been paid in unprotected industries, such as the automobile industry. This supports the argument of the free traders that an industry in which we have natural advantages does not need a tariff wall in order to pay good wages and com-

TARIFFS 

309 pete with foreign producers. Free traders point out that the basic reasons for the high American standard of living are: (a) our rich natural resources, (b) our great supply of capital goods, (c) our efficient labor. 

2. Free Trade Arguments. The contention of the free traders that the tariff works against a "natural" distribution of production has been borne out. For example, various European countries seem to have many natural advantages (labor, raw materials, etc.) in the production of magnesium. Some authorities doubt that the American magnesium industry will ever be efficient enough to compete with foreign producers.

Nevertheless, the tariff encourages us to use land, labor and capital in this field rather than in other fields for which we are better fitted.

There can be no doubt that many industries have sought to have tariff protection continued after they emerged from their "infancy."

Finally, the tariff has certainly raised the consumer's cost of living.

Thus we see that the tariff has actually had most of the effects claimed by both its advocates and its opponents. Whether we favor free trade or protection depends on which set of objectives we consider more essential to the national welfare. 

The Present Situation.-The trend throughout the world, today, seems to be very definitely toward protection. High tariff barriers appear on all sides; even Great Britain has abandoned her traditional free trade policy. Our own Democratic Party is not so firmly committed as formerly to sweeping tariff reduction. The "Solid South" (Democratic stronghold) is becoming industrialized, and Southern manufacturers want protection. 

By its reciprocal trade agreements program, however, the Roosevelt Administration has taken an important step toward breaking the "log jam" of world trade. (See Reciprocal Tariff

Act in table on page 307.) Among the nations with which we have concluded reciprocity treaties are France, Brazil, Cuba, and Canada. In the Canadian agreement, which was fairly typical, we lowered our duties on Canadian lumber, dairy products, and live-stock; while Canada allowed more favorable rates on American fruits and vegetables, automobiles, tractors, electrical equipment, and other iron and steel products.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS The Second World War and Foreign Trade. The success of the totalitarian dictatorships in the war which began in 1939 was a severe blow to our foreign trade. Trade with nations under German domination (France, Poland, Denmark, Norway, Holland, and Czechoslovakia) ceased almost entirely. Moreover, it seemed only too probable that Germany and Italy would attempt to invade our Latin American markets. In Germany and 

Italy, foreign trade is carried on by the government, rather than by individual business men, and various large-scale barter methods have been developed. Naturally, it is very difficult for private American exporters to compete with such methods. To meet this situation, some authorities have suggested the formation of an Inter-American Export Cartel. This would be a governmental corporation, empowered to handle all Latin American import and export trade. Latin American exporters, for example, would sell to the Cartel, rather than to private American customers. The Cartel would then resell the products, both in the United States and abroad. If necessary, the Cartel would accumulate considerable surpluses, and wait for suitable opportunities to dispose of them. This scheme, it is maintained, would enable the United States to compete successfully with totalitarian methods, and would prevent the growth of Fascist economic and political power in Latin America, our own "back yard."

CHAPTER XXIV  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To explain what is meant by the "business cycle.” 

2. To show how and why the various stages of the business cycle succeed each other. 

3. To examine the various plans proposed for the elimination of the business cycle.

Changes in Business Conditions. We all know that business conditions are not always the same. At times sales of merchandise are brisk; bankers, manufacturers, and storekeepers are making money, labor is employed, and we say that, on the whole, the nation is prosperous. At other times sales are low, profits are small or non-existent, there is widespread unemployment, and we say that we are in a period of depression. 

Now, experience has taught us that neither periods of depression nor periods of prosperity are permanent. There seems to be a transition from one to the other in a more or less regular manner. The study of the whys and wherefores of these transitions is the study of the business cycle. 

Definition. The business cycle is the process by which a nation passes from a period of general economic prosperity to a period of general economic depression, and from a period of depression to a period of prosperity. 

Length of the Business Cycle.-The United States has experienced severe depressions in 1837, 1857, 1873, 1893, 1920, and 1930. In this country the cycle, if measured from depression to depression, has averaged about 18½ years in length. 

Causes of the Business Cycle.-What really causes this rhythmical upward and downward swing in business? There are two factors which are of basic importance. One of these is the change in the general outlook, or prospects for profits. The 312

second factor, which is an outgrowth of the first, is overexpansion and liquidation. 

1. Outlook for Profits.-Business enterprises wish to make profits and to avoid losses. Because of this, they are very sensitive to the general economic outlook. If the outlook is encouraging, that is, if the chances to earn profits seem good, business will probably try to increase its production. This expansion will result in the employment of more workers and in the purchase of more producers' good (machines and raw materials) for use in the enterprises. The result is prosperity. 

If the general outlook is not encouraging, business enterprises will have no desire to expand their scale of operations, and may even think it wise to contract their production to avoid possible losses. If the outlook is definitely unfavorable, such a general contraction of business activity will take place. This reduces employment and decreases the demand for producers' goods. 

The result is depression. 

2. Overexpansion and Liquidation.-Closely allied to these changes in the outlook for profits, is the matter of everexpansion and liquidation. In homely language, overexpansion is a matter of "living too high." Liquidation is a return, often violent and painful, to a more modest way of living. 

How does a business enterprise behave during a period of overexpansion? How does it live too high? A manufacturer who sees opportunities for high profits will seek to expand his scale of operations. He will buy raw materials and put men to work on them. He will purchase new and improved machinery, and may even decide to build an addition to his factory. 

Now, in all probability, the manufacturer will find that his own capital funds are not sufficient to finance all this expansion. 

He will get these funds from outside sources, either by bank loans or by the sale of stocks and bonds to the investing public. 

The manufacturer is willing to assume the added expense involved in these loans because he is convinced that the opportunities for profit justify it. The credit he obtains in this manner expands his purchasing power exactly as though he had come into possession of additional money.

Suppose, however, that the manufacturer has miscalculated the chances for making profits. In such a case, he will find that

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

he has overexpanded. His new equipment enables him to produce more goods but he cannot sell them at a profitable price. 

At the same time, interest charges on his loans and other fixed costs (pages 49-50) must be met.

What is to be done? The answer is to liquidate something, that is, to turn something into cash by selling it. Throw something on the market, the machinery, the raw materials, the finished product, anything that will sell. Sell it at a loss, if neces-

If thousands of business enterprises have made the same miscalculations and indulged in overexpansion, the rush to liquidate will become general. Prices fall, and industrial activity declines sharply. In other words, our economic structure is undergoing a return to a more modest way of living. 

Overproduction.-The discussion above indicates that depression comes when large numbers of business men discover that *New England textile manufacturers have been known to sell the machinery out of their mills to pay only the interest on their loans.

they can no longer sell goods at a profit. In other words, the nation's ability to purchase (purchasing power) is not sufficient to absorb the goods which can be produced, nor to support the financial commitments involved in the establishment of the means of production (machines, factories, raw materials, etc.). We have overproduced, in the sense that there is no profitable market for the goods which we are equipped to turn out. However, this does not mean that we have turned out more goods than the

Are there any psychological factors involved in the process of decline? people of the country can use beneficially. There has never been such a situation in the history of our country or of any other country. When economists speak of overproduction, they mean that the goods cannot be sold at a profit, not that people do not want and need the goods. 

Stages of the Business Cycle.-Now that we have considered the two factors which may be considered the basic causes of the business cycle, let us follow such a cycle through and see how the factors operate as the nation passes from a period of depression through the succeeding stages of recovery, prosperity, and decline.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

1. Depression.-In this period, profits, as a whole, are low and some business enterprises are running at a loss. As the opportunities for profits diminish, many firms go out of business, while others cut their production. As a result of this, unemployment is widespread. Wages and interest rates are low.

Prices are low also, but the low prices do not attract many buyers because most people do not have much money to spend or do not care to spend what they have.

PRICES 

Is it possible for particular industries to be prosperous during a period of general depression?

In this period of depression, bad as it seems, certain forces are operating which will produce better conditions. People must buy some commodities, and their purchases are exhausting the stocks of the storekeepers. The low prices of materials and labor encourage some men to build houses and factories which they want for immediate use or which they hope to use profitably when conditions improve.

The low prices, in addition, encourage some men to go into business. It is true that there is no great demand for goods, but production costs, such as wages, rentals, and raw materials, are

very low. Moreover, the Federal Reserve Banks have probably decreased their rediscount rates, so that business men can obtain credit at lower rates of interest. In addition, the equipment of many bankrupt firms is thrown on the market. This can be purchased at extremely low prices, often far below the cost of manufacture. Because of the low fixed and operating costs which result from all this, if a well-managed business can sell its products at all, it will probably earn some profits. RECOVERY
