PUBLIC CONFIDENCE 

BANKS FEDERAL RESERVE SYSTEM 

Are we well advanced on the road to recovery at present? Which of the forces indicated have helped pull us out of the depression? 

2. Recovery. People continue to buy the low-priced merchandise which is being turned out by various business firms. 

Retailers order more of these articles in order to replenish their stocks. To fill these orders, manufacturers buy more raw materials and hire additional workers. This, in turn, stimulates the recovery which is getting under way, for these newly employed workers spend their wages and further increase the demand for goods. This increased demand encourages new firms to go into business and old firms to expand, and more machines and buildings will be used. Business men will borrow to buy these capital

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

goods, and the banks will be able to loan out their funds and collect interest charges. 

Conditions are improving rapidly for all classes of the population, manufacturers, bankers, storekeepers, and laborers. The groundwork is being laid for a period of prosperity. 

3. Prosperity.-In this period the forces which appeared during the period of recovery continue and grow stronger. 

Prices continue to go up and business becomes more and more profitable. Labor earns higher wages, and the prices which are paid for land and capital also increase. In short, all the factors of production are in demand and, to a greater or lesser extent, all classes of the population share in the good times. 

It seems that this pleasant state of affairs will go on indefinitely, but certain disturbing forces begin to assert themselves in the economic structure. Costs of production continue to rise and to cut into the entrepreneur's margin of profit. The high prices and the general optimism bring new business houses into existence, and the resulting competition creates new costs in the form of intensive advertising and "high-pressure salesmanship.”

Finally, the expansion of business has enormously increased the production of goods. Toward the end of the period of prosperity it becomes evident that more is being produced than the buying public can consume. Warehouses begin to fill with unsold goods, profits tend to drop, and business men, on the whole, feel less confident. 

4. Decline. As the masses of unsold goods on the market accumulate, and as the rate of profit declines, business men cut prices and curtail production. This causes unemployment and a further drop in the demand for goods. The price level continues to move downward, and some firms are forced out of business. 

This, in turn, may cause banks to fail, because bankrupt firms cannot repay the money which they have borrowed. When banks fail many people lose their money and a further shock is given to the confidence of the nation.

All these forces, unsold goods, a falling rate of profit, unemployment, bank failures, etc., react upon and reinforce each other. The process of decline gathers momentum. More banks and businesses fail. Unemployment increases still further. The production and distribution of goods and services become lower

AMERICAN BUSINESS ACTIVITY SINCE 1790 

How Can Depression Be Avoided?-This is the most important question which faces the American people today. We all know that this last and greatest of depressions has been a terrible

and lower and the nation sinks into the rut of depression. Ultimately, as we have indicated, the nation will emerge from the depression, but this is a long, hard road attended with an overwhelming amount of waste and suffering. ៖ ៖ ៖ Co 

MAPKET 

100

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

experience, and we are anxious to attain complete recovery and to avoid future depressions. What can be done about it? Many suggestions have been made. 

1. Better Planning by Business Men.-Some students of eco nomics have advanced the idea that if business men knew more about general business conditions they might make wiser decisions and avoid the overexpansion we have described. It does not seem that there is much practical value in this suggestion. 

As we have said, business men are interested in immediate profits and, as individuals, they are not in a position to take a disinterested, long-range view of the general economic situation. 

2. Control of the Interest Rate.-Some economists believe that the control of credit might prevent the business cycle and depressions. The idea is to raise the interest rate when business expands too rapidly. This will raise production costs and will discourage further expansion. When business is slow, on the other hand, the interest rate will be lowered, and this will decrease production costs and stimulate business activity. 

We have seen, in a previous chapter, that the Federal Reserve System can exercise a certain measure of control of this type over the business world by means of its rediscount policy and its open-market operations. The fact that the application of this control has not succeeded in avoiding depressions does not mean that it is without value. It has been of help to us in the past, and can be of help to us in the future, especially if we apply the lessons that we have learned the last ten years. But control of credit, in itself, is evidently not enough to enable us to avoid depressions. 

3. Public Works.-Another plan to avoid depression calls for the construction of public works when private business slows up and unemployment begins to spread. It is claimed that this will curb the business decline, because the people engaged on the government projects will continue to draw wages and to spend them for food, clothing, rent, etc. Likewise, the government expenditures for producers' goods (to be used on the public works) will make up for the drop in the demand of private industry for those products. As conditions improve and more private employment appears, the government is supposed gradually to bring its construction work to an end.

Opponents of this scheme point out that public works are very expensive and that they necessitate increased taxation. 

This, they claim, adds to the expenses of business, further decreases profits, and discourages production and expansion. If the public works are financed by extensive governmental borrowing, they will absorb capital funds which might otherwise be invested in private business. Moreover, borrowing in itself causes increased taxation, because the government must pay interest charges.

SAM 

BUSINESS CAN GOVERNMENT SPEND- ING AVOID DEPRESSION? 

PUBLIC WORKS OVERPRODUCTION LOW PURCHASING POWER 

Why is it desirable that the funds for government emergency spending be accumulated in periods of prosperity?

This indicates that an effective public works program ought to be planned and financed in the good times which precede a depression. During the period of prosperity when incomes are high, the government can use its taxing power to set up a surplus or reserve fund, without working any undue hardship on the people. At the same time, plans can be drawn up to spend the money in the most useful and productive way. Then, when the signs of depression appear, a public-works program can be qut into effect quickly and without financial strain.

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

4. Mass Purchasing Power.-Many people believe that a lasting prosperity can be achieved in this country only by the creation and maintenance of mass purchasing power. This means that the great masses of our people, the workers, the farmers, the small business men, must have incomes large enough to enable them to buy the goods that our highly efficient productive system is turning out. If they do not have this purchasing power, unsold goods accumulate on the shelves of retailers, factories are idle, farmers and miners can find no market for their products, and the nation sinks into the rut of depression. 

There are many conflicting views as to how mass purchasing power is to be attained. The NRA, with its minimum wage scales, was one attempt to solve the problem. For various reasons, it was not a very successful attempt. Some of the reasons for this were: (a) The minimum wage scales were set at a low level. (b) The minimum wage tended to become the maximum. (c) In some fields there was violation of the minimum wage requirements of the codes. (d) The price of goods rose so that real wages (page 73) were not increased so much as were money wages.

In 1938, Congress passed the Fair Labor Standards Act, which is designed to place a "floor" under wages, and a "ceiling' over hours. The following provisions were made applicable to all industrial enterprises engaged in interstate commerce: Minimum wages during the first year of the act, 25 cents per hour; during the next six years, 30 cents per hour; thereafter, 40 cents per hour. Maximum working hours-during the first year of the act, 44 hours per week; during the next year, 42 hours; thereafter, 40 hours.

One of the primary purposes of the Wagner-Connery Act (page 267), which insures employees the right to collective bargaining, was to increase mass purchasing power. As we have seen, organized workers generally have greater bargaining power than unorganized workers and can obtain a higher scale of wages. 

Union leaders maintain that a strong labor movement is one of the best guarantees of sustained mass purchasing power and of a stable prosperity.

Unemployment insurance and old age pensions are other devices which tend to prevent sudden, ruinous falls in mass pur-

chasing power. On the other hand, when these insurance plans are financed by taxes on the wages of workers and by payroll taxes which can be shifted-the method of financing provided in the present Social Security Bill (page 72)—they will tend to decrease immediate mass purchasing power. 

Conclusion. We have not given you a conclusive answer to the problem of economic depression because the whole answer has probably not yet been found. The object of this chapter, rather, has been to present certain facts and ideas for your consideration and to stimulate your interest. This is important because, as future voters, you are going to help determine the policies which this nation adopts in its fight against depression. When the great masses of our citizens develop an intelligent interest in the economic problems that beset us, we shall be well on the road toward their permanent solution.

CHAPTER XXV  PURPOSES OF THIS CHAPTER 

1. To discuss the weaknesses of our economic system. 

2. To examine the various types of radical doctrines. 

3. To point out the weaknesses of the radical criticism of our society. 

4. To discuss the services which the radicals have performed for our society. 

5. To examine other suggestions for economic reorganization. 

CAPITALISM 

Capitalism. The economic system under which we live is known as capitalism. We have seen that the basic institutions of capitalism are private property, private enterprise, freedom, contracts, and competition. This is the economic system of the greater part of the civilized world today. 

Weaknesses of Capitalism.-In the course of this survey of our economic life we have seen that the system under which we live is not perfect. There is always some poverty and unemployment and, during the periods of depression which seem to recur quite regularly, suffering among the poorer classes is widespread and intense. On the other hand, there is a comparatively small group of people who possess great fortunes. In some cases the services which these rich men perform for the community do not justify the great wealth which they possess. We have seen, in addition, that there is sometimes bitter conflict, industrial warfare, between employer and employee, and that this is a source of human suffering and of waste of our productive forces. 

Basic Reforms within Capitalism.-There are many people who consider capitalism fundamentally sound but believe that certain far-reaching changes must be made in the system as we know it today. 

1. A "Planned Economy."-Among these advocates of reform, are a considerable group who think that the planlessness 326

of present conditions is the source of our troubles. They point out that each business enterprise is an independent, profit-seeking organization which goes its way without concern for the welfare of society as a whole. The result of this, they maintain, is that there is a lack of balance and coördination among the various factors in our economic life. For example, there may be overexpansion in some fields of industry, and under-expansion in others. However, no individual business man is in a position to do a great deal about this, and it may be a long time before the proper adjustment is made in the two industries. Disorganization similar to this appears from time to time in every branch of our economic life. The general effects of these conditions are to throw our economic system out of gear, and to bring on depressions.

Those who favor a planned economy advocate that the government become a sort of directing and coördinating agency. 

Private enterprise, the basic institution of capitalism, will be retained, but the government will restrict and guide the activities of these enterprises so that they work together for the common good.

To a considerable extent, this is the philosophy which underlies the New Deal. By means of such agencies as the NRA, the 

AAA, and the SEC, the Roosevelt Administration has attempted to introduce a certain amount of planned coördination into our economic system. 

2. Fascism. In common with the doctrine of a planned economy, Fascism holds that coördination and control must be introduced into the framework of the capitalist system. The Fascists find support for this idea in the great importance which they attribute to the State, as opposed to the individual. All classes and all business enterprises must forget their immediate private interests and work together for the interests of the Corporative State. The Fascists do not believe, however, that such harmony and coöperation can be achieved under a democratic form of government. Instead, there must be a concentration of power in the hands of one man, a dictator. 

The Fascist governments which have established themselves in Germany and Italy have attempted to regulate production, prices, wages, profits, etc. A typical example of the manner in which these governments function is to be seen in the field of

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

labor relations. In both the Fascist countries, labor unions, as we know them, have been abolished. Instead, a government department handles and regulates all relations between employers and employees. Fascists claim that, in this way, strikes and other forms of industrial warfare have been eliminated. On the other hand, the opponents of Fascism point out that this has been accomplished by a sacrifice of freedom, since working people have been deprived of the right to form organizations for the promotion of their own interests.

OF 

WAGES 

AND 

PRICES THE STATE NO STRIKES NO LOCKOUTS 

Do you think it likely that the United States will adopt Fascism? 

3. Laissez Faire.-Laissez faire is a doctrine which holds that the influence and the control which the government exercises over our economic life should be reduced to the barest minimum. The supporters of laissez faire maintain that the proper function of government is to perform a few essential services, such as police protection, and that business is a self-regulating mechanism which works best when it is left alone. If the forces of the market, that is, supply and demand, are permitted to function without interference, they will adjust the prices of all commodities and services (including the price of labor) at the

proper or "natural" level. However, when the government does interfere, the operation of these forces is impeded and the balance which otherwise would prevail among all the elements of our economic system is upset.

For example, when the depression period set in, wages fell sharply. The Federal Government attempted to resist this fall by inserting minimum-wage clauses in the various NRA codes. 

The advocates of laissez faire maintain that this attempt was futile and harmful. The fall in wages was a natural price movement resulting from general economic conditions. Business men could not afford to increase wages and, when the government tried to make them do so, they were forced to curtail production or to go out of business altogether. Thus, the laissez faire advocates said, NRA had the effect of intensifying the bad conditions it was intended to correct.

It is claimed that similar interference with the workings of natural economic forces played a great part in precipitating our nation into the depression and is now hampering our efforts to get out of it.

At present, attempts to introduce fundamental reforms into the framework of capitalism narrow themselves to a choice between a planned economy and a laissez faire economy. A considerable number of people favor laissez faire, but the trend of our historical development has been in the direction of a planned economy. Fascism is regarded with disfavor in democratic nations such as the United States. 

RADICALISM 

Meaning of Radicalism. Some people have advanced the idea that the evils which we have mentioned are so great and so incurable that they justify the destruction of our present economic system and the creation of a new set of institutions to take its place. This doctrine is known as radicalism, and the men who support it are called radicals. 

The term "radicalism" has been used and is still used to indicate many different varieties of political and economic opinion. Under present-day conditions, however, the term is best confined to those who wish to destroy the "roots" (which is the literal meaning of radical") of our economic system. As we

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

have seen, those "roots" are private property and private enterprise. People who wish to introduce reforms within these basic institutions should not be called radicals. 

The basic idea which all present radicals have in common is that the means of production should be owned not by individuals, but by the community as a whole, and that they should be operated for public use rather than for private profit. It is clear that such a method of social organization, which is sometimes called collectivism, would destroy private enterprise and private property, which are the cornerstones of our capitalist world. 

Radicals are united in their opposition to capitalism and private property, but they differ widely among themselves and many conflicting types of radicalism have arisen. 

Types of Radicalism.—The four principal types of radical ism which have appeared in the modern world are Socialism, Communism, Syndicalism, and Anarchism. 

1. Socialism. This doctrine, which is represented in the 

United States by the Socialist Party, and in England by the Labor Party, advocates that the collectivized society be achieved by evolutionary methods. This means that there is not to be a sudden, violent transition from capitalism to Socialism, but rather a peaceful, long-term process. During this period, the 

Socialists will advance their cause by educational propaganda and by working to strengthen all labor organizations. In addition, they will try to elect their candidates to various legislative bodies, where they will work for the extension of public ownership, particularly in public utilities, and for the passage of favorable legislation, such as minimum wage laws and child labor laws. 

2. Communism.-This doctrine holds that the collectivized state can be achieved only by revolution. Communists do engage in political and labor union activities, but these are regarded only as preparation and training for the great violent class struggle which, it is maintained, is necessary for the overthrow of capitalism. Communists, the world over, derive leadership and inspiration from Soviet Russia which, since 1917, has officially accepted the Communist philosophy and claims to be working toward the creation of a real Communist society. 

The philosophy and program of Communism are derived from

the writings of a nineteenth-century German economist named Karl Marx. Socialists also have great respect for the writings of 

Marx, but they do not profess to accept his doctrines as completely and as literally as the Communists do. 

3. Syndicalism.-This doctrine advocates that all the workers of each industry be organized in an industrial or vertical union, and that each union own and manage its industry. Thus the economic life of a nation would be dominated by organized groups of all its productive workers. To gain power, the syndicalists advocate the use of militant labor union policies, such as sabotage and the general strike. 

Syndicalism, in its origins, is a French movement, and is based on the writings of a Frenchman, George Sorel. In the 

United States, the philosophy of syndicalism is represented by the Social Labor Party, and by an organization known as the 

Industrial Workers of the World (the I.W.W.). The movement is not important in the United States but it is extremely powerful in several European countries, one of which is Spain. 

4. Anarchism. This doctrine holds that any restriction or compulsion in the social and economic relationships of men is bad. Therefore the set of institutions which we call the "state" (the army, the police, the law courts, the various law-making bodies, etc.) must be abolished, and complete individual freedom established.

The anarchist believes that when this is done our capitalist society will break down, and men will voluntarily work together in a peaceful collectivist society. Anarchism, as we can see from the above, is based on a profound faith in the goodness of human nature.

Weakness of Radicalism.-Radicals, whatever their party, are convinced that the economic reorganization which they advocate would result in greater benefits for the masses of the population than capitalism has been able to give them. In their arguments radicals stress the evils of capitalism, which we have mentioned above.

This tendency to stress evils, which is commendable in itself, is the source of the characteristic weakness of radicalism. As a whole, the radical sees only the bad side of our economic system. 

He calls attention to the tremendous wealth and extreme poverty

VISUALIZED ECONOMICS 

which exist at the same time, but ignores the great numbers of middle-class people who live comfortably on moderate incomes. 

He points out the waste of productive forces, which often occurs under our system, but fails to realize that it is under that system that we have developed our efficient methods of mechanized mass production. He maintains that the so-called individualism of our society leads to exploitation of one man by another and to social injustice, but he is blind to the fact that, under individualism, GREAT INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT 

AAA 

HIGHER STANDARD OF LIVING 

​RADICAL 

​الضبون الحوري 

MULTI- 

MILLIONAIRE BREAD LINES EVILS OF FACTORY SYSTEM THE RADICAL SEES ONLY THE BAD SIDE OF OUR ECONOMIC SYSTEMI 

Has this emphasis on the evils of our system served any useful social purpose? Has it had any bad effects? men have an incentive to work and to create which might be lacking in a collectivist society. Finally, the radical ignores or belittles the value of the freedom which men enjoy in such capitalist countries as the United States and Great Britain, and which they could not enjoy under the discipline and regimentation of Communism or Socialism. 

The proof of the merits of capitalism can be seen in the relatively high standard of living which the common people enjoy in the leading capitalist countries today. This standard has risen

steadily throughout the last 150 years. In other words, capitalism has worked. The radical persistently ignores these facts and demands that we abandon capitalism in favor of another system whose merits have never been proven in actual practice.* 

Services of Radicalism.-Radicals have served our society by the vigorous manner in which they have called attention to the evils which do exist and, in some cases, by the constructive suggestions which they have advanced for the elimination of these evils. For example, many years ago, radicals favored the imposition of a graduated income tax, both as a good method of raising money, and as a means of counteracting the tendency for wealth to concentrate in the hands of a comparatively few people. 

Today this type of tax is established in almost all civilized countries. Radicals have played a part in the adoption of social security legislation (unemployment insurance, old-age insurance, etc.) in many countries.

Services of this sort are the leading influence which radicalism has had or seems likely to have on American economic life. 

The great majority of the American people firmly believe that we should not abandon our traditional individualism for collectivism.

Column B 

Overvalued money tends to drive undervalued money out of circulation. 

Population tends to increase faster than the food supply. 

Proportional expenditures for necessities decrease as the family income increases. 

Price is fixed at the point of the largest net profit.

In consumption the satisfaction derived from each successive unit gradually decreases to zero. 

There is a point in production where further applications of labor and capital, while they still increase the product, do not increase it proportionately. 

Throughout a market the price of a commodity tends to be the same. 

If one member of a group purchases a luxury, every other member is tempted to do likewise.

Sales of any article will vary inversely with its price and directly with its utility and with the purchasing power of the public. 

Countries specialize in the production of those goods in which they have the greatest relative advantage or the least relative disadvantage and acquire other goods by foreign trade.
